Exponentiable Virtual Double Categories and Representability of Exponentials
Ea E. Thompson, Kevin D. Carlson

Abstract
Virtual double categories provide an effective framework for formal category theory. Recent work has investigated the question of higher morphisms between virtual double categories, following on from work on higher morphisms between double categories, and building up to Arkor’s recent conjecture on exponentiable virtual double categories–those virtual double categories, morphisms out of which can themselves be enriched to a whole virtual double category. In this paper we resolve Arkor’s conjecture by providing a number of equivalent characterizations of the exponentiable virtual double categories in terms of existence of decompositions of cells. We also show that virtual double categories of cospans are always exponentiable, as are the virtual double categories arising from pseudo double categories or from exponentiable multicategories, as studied by Pisani. We give conditions under which the virtual double category of virtual double functors admits composites, following work of Paré for the non-virtual case. We base our work on a general approach to exponentiability for categories of models of limit sketches, which we apply to give new treatments of exponentiability for semicategories, categories, multicategories, and their functors.



1
Introduction

Double categories, originally defined by Ehresmann in
[
Ehr64
]
, and later developed by Paré and collaborators in
[
DP93
,
GP99
,
GP04
,
GP07
,
GP08
,
Par11
,
Par13
]
, have proved effective in a range of areas in mathematics for modelling structures with two interacting classes of maps. Applications of double categories can be found in homotopy theory, such as in algebraic
K
-theory (c.f.
[
Zak11
,
Cam+23
,
Mer+25
]
) and enriched
(\infty,n)
-category theory (c.f.
[
MRR23
,
Abe23
,
GH15
,
Hau16
]
), as well as use cases in modelling physical systems such as in biochemistry
[
Adu+23
]
, epidemiology
[
Bae+23
]
, and dynamical systems theory more broadly
[
LM25
]
.

One application of particular interest to the authors comes from Lambert and Patterson’s work
on cartesian double theories in
[
LP24
]
, where double categories
are used as a foundation for categorical logic.
Lambert and Patterson are particularly interested in morphisms and higher morphisms among
“models” of such a theory, which are given by lax double functors and lax transformations between them.
But as is shown in Theorem 10.3 of
[
LP24
]
, the collection of models of
a cartesian double theory need not form a genuine double category,
but instead only a virtual double category in the sense of
[
CS10
]
–roughly,
a double category in which one direction of the arrows may not admit compositions.
This work extends a growing understanding, originating from the study of the virtual double
category of lax double functors by Paré in
[
Par13
]
, that virtual double categories
arise unavoidably in the study of double category theory. In this paper, we take the virtual
double categories as our subject of interest in their own right, and investigate
in particular the question of internal homs for virtual double categories.

Internal homs for category-like structures

The fact that Paré’s virtual double category of lax double functors is not a pseudo-double category
in general is a glimpse into a subtle story about internal homs in categories with category-like objects.
That the category
\mathsf{Cat}
of small categories is cartesian closed is quite remarkable,
insofar as such of its near neighbors as the categories of monoids
and of multicategories (
[
Pis14
]
) fall well short of this condition.
An even closer, but still non-cartesian closed, analogue is the category
\mathsf{Semicat}
of
small semicategories (a semicategory has an associative composition operation, but may lack identity arrows.)
It is also famously the case (
[
Con72
]
) that most slices of
\mathsf{Cat}
are not cartesian closed.

Indeed, the only reasonably spacious general class of cartesian closed categories into which
\mathsf{Cat}

seems to fall is the class of
“categories
\mathcal{V}-\mathsf{Cat}
of categories enriched in a cartesian closed category
\mathcal{V}
,”
in this case for
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{Set}.
covers in particular the cases of strict
n
-categories for larger
n.

But for most other kinds of categorical structures, such as the multicategories already
mentioned, but also for pseudo-double categories and virtual double categories, cartesian closure fails.
These are cases in which, unlike the symmetric multicategories
with their Boardman-Vogt tensor product or
\mathcal{V}
-categories for a non-cartesian monoidal

\mathcal{V}
, the cartesian product really is the natural monoidal product, so that we are at an
impasse if we wish to develop the appropriate generalization of functor categories.

We are thus led to look for a partial solution: do certain exponentials
A^{B}
exist, in categories
like
\mathsf{Semicat}
,
\mathsf{Cat}/B
,
\mathsf{Multicat}
, or the category
\mathsf{Vdc}

of virtual double categories? This is the problem
of
exponentiability
, most famous from the case of topological spaces (
[
DK70
]
). An object

B
of a category
\mathsf{C}
with finite products is
exponentiable
if the functor
(-)\times B
admits
a right adjoint, so that every exponential
A^{B}
exists. We will restrict our interest here to
locally presentable categories, whose adjoint functor theorems allow for a much simpler theory
of exponentiability than the reader may recall from the study of
\mathsf{Top}.

Contributions

In this paper we will provide a complete classification of the exponentiable virtual double categories,
in particular, resolving all but one branch of Conjecture 3.15 of
[
Ark25
]

positively. The last branch, regarding the pro-double categories, cannot be quite made sense of as written, and we provide an
alternative formulation in terms of pro-double categories with unbiased associators in Appendix
B
.

The approach is one which
should find usability quite generally in questions for exponentiability in
locally presentable categories: we give a sketch for virtual
double categories and show that exponentiability of an object
A
may be characterized via preservation,
by
A\times(-),
of the colimits arising from the distinguished cones of a sketch.
We illustrate how straightforward it is to apply this approach to get complete proofs of the
characterization of exponentiable objects–not, for instance, leaving the verification of the universal
property of a putative exponential to the reader–in the preliminary
cases of semicategories, categories, and multicategories in Section
3
.

The technical heart of the paper runs from Subsection
4.2
through
Subsection
5.2
. Here we show that the functor
(-)\times\mathbb{D},
for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
,
preserves the colimits of VDCs arising from the sketched cones in the sketch for VDCs (Definition
4.8
)
if and only if every multicell of
\mathbb{D}
admits essentially unique decompositions “of every possible shape.”
These possible shapes are parameterized by rooted finite uniform planar trees (Definition
4.5
),
and the reduction of the conditions for exponentiability from Theorem
4.14
to
Corollary
5.6
depends on some study of the combinatorics of such trees.
The idea of this parameterization of decompositions by trees may be indicated by the diagram below;
the essential uniqueness is up to unary cells acting on the intermediate layers.

We will also extend Paré’s factorization property and representability results
in
[
Par13
]
to the setting of exponentiable VDCs,
thus characterizing when an existing exponential of VDC’s
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{dc}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})

admits composites of loose arrows. Roughly speaking, we show that
\mathbb{A}
’s cells must all
factor through globular cells, and that
\mathbb{B}
must admit
local colimits, which will sound much the same as Paré’s characterization in the narrower non-virtual case.
In the process of characterizing exponentiable VDCs and their representability we will show that the
theory extends the characterization of exponentiable multicategories proved by Pisani in
[
Pis14
]
,
while also providing a generalization of Day’s classification of pro-monoidal multicategories in terms of
bi-cocontinuous monoidal structures on copresheaf categories in
[
Day70
]
. (Example
7.11
and the paragraph following.)

Related and future work

When
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{Vdc}
is the category of VDCs,
exponentiable morphisms were partially investigated in
[
FL25
]
where it was
shown that discrete opfibrations between VDCs, which generalize discrete opfibrations between
categories, are all exponentiable. Exponentiable VDCs were also partially investigated in recent
work of Arkor
[
Ark25
]
, where it is shown
that representable VDCs (i.e. pseudo-double categories) are exponentiable, while conditions for arbitrary exponentiable VDCs are
conjectured in
[
Ark25
, Conjecture 3.15]
.

Additionally, in recent work of Blom, Loubaton, and Ruit
[
BLR26
]
,
a characterization of exponentiable morphisms between algebrads for
\infty
-categorical algebraic patterns
is provided. These algebrads correspond to
T
-multicategories for certain parametric right adjoint
monads on pre-sheaf
\infty
-categories. Taking
T=\mathsf{fc}
to be the free category monad,
the classification in
[
BLR26
]
can be modified slightly
to obtain a classification of exponentiable VDCs, essentially
reproducing our first characterization in Theorem
4.14
. Our tighter characterizations
do not fall out of Blom, Loubaton, and Ruit’s work, however.

In future work the first author hopes to extend the results of this paper to classify exponential normal
VDCs and to understand exponentiability in the context of
virtual double categories of lax functors and lax transformations, as appearing in
[
LP24
]
.

Size Considerations.
We fix two Grothendieck universes
\mathcal{U}\in\mathcal{V}
.
Elements of
\mathcal{U}
are referred to as small, while elements of
\mathcal{V}
are referred to as large.
We will write
\mathsf{Set}
and
\mathsf{Cat}
for the large categories of small sets and small categories,
respectively, and we will write
\mathsf{SET}
and
\mathsf{CAT}
for the very large categories of large sets and
large categories, respectively. All sets, categories, and virtual double categories will be assumed small,
unless stated otherwise.

Notation.
Categories and multicategories will be denoted with sans serif font
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C},\ldots
,
while (virtual) double categories will be denoted with blackboard bold font
\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B},\mathbb{C},\ldots
.
We will also denote bicategories using bold font
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{C},\ldots.

Paper Outline.

•

In Section
2
we review the necessary preliminaries on virtual
double categories (VDCs) and their relation to pseudo-double categories. The reader
with a background in virtual double category theory should be able to simply scan this section for
notational conventions.

•

In Section
3
we outline our general approach to studying exponentiability using
limit sketches. We apply Pultr’s theorem on the universal property of the category of models of a sketch
to characterize exponentiable models as those whose product preserves just those colimits of models
arising from the sketched cones in Corollary
3.3
. We illustrate
this technology with the motivating examples of semicategories, categories, and multicategories.

•

In Section
4
we obtain a first
characterization of exponentiable VDCs as those for which every multicell admits essentially
unique decompositions according to every tree shape, in Theorem
4.14
.

•

This is then extended to our main result, Theorem
5.11
, in Section
5
. Here
we first show that unique decompositions reduce to those shaped by trees that branch only one cell per layer,
and only using binary or nullary branches, which we call pro-representable virtual double categories. We
also show that pro-representable VDCs are equivalent to malleable VDCs, those whose composition cell is cartesian
when viewed in a certain manner as generalized multicategories. Finally,
we show that the VDC of spans of sets detects exponentiability by giving a
Yoneda lemma (Lemma
5.17
) for VDCs.

•

In Section
7
we provide sufficient conditions for the exponential of two
VDCs to have (weak) composites, as well as conditions for Paré’s VDC
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{ax}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{B})

to be representable when
\mathbb{A}
is an exponentiable VDC, and not necessarily representable.

•

In Section
6
we give examples of exponentiable VDCs. Perhaps most interesting is that the
VDC of cospans in any category
\mathsf{E}
, which is not a pseudo double category unless
\mathsf{E}
should admit pushouts,
is nevertheless always exponentiable. We also give a tool for checking exponentiability of certain tiny VDCs in
Lemma
6.1
and show that exponentiable multicategories also provide examples of exponentiable VDCs.
Finally we show that the definition of pro-double category proferred in
[
Ark25
]

is not workable in Proposition
6.7
.

•

The proof of the characterization of exponentiable VDCs is extended in Appendix
A

to provide a characterization of exponentiable morphisms of VDCs. In Appendix
B
we give a presentation of
virtual double categories in terms of operator categories, which may be familiar from the study of algebraic
patterns, interpret our exponentiability conditions in this context. In Appendix
C
we rephrase
the Yoneda lemma for VDCs in terms of a Yoneda lemma for actions of a monoid in a VDC.

23-1-0133.

2
Background on Virtual Double Categories

In this section we recall some basic definitions appearing in the theory of virtual double categories (VDCs), along with certain profunctors associated to virtual double categories which will be important for the main proofs in the paper. The primary references used for the background on VDCs comes from
[
Lei02
,
Lei04
,
CS10
]
. Note that in
[
Lei02
,
Lei04
]
VDCs are referred to as
\mathsf{fc}
-multicategories.

First, we recall that for a category
\mathsf{C}
with finite pullbacks and countable coproducts preserved by the finite pullbacks,
we have a natural free category monad
\mathsf{fc}
on the category
\mathsf{Grph}(\mathsf{C})
of graphs in
\mathsf{C}
.
Explicitly,
\mathsf{fc}
sends a graph
y\rightrightarrows x
in
\mathsf{C}
to the graph with the same object of
vertices
x
, and with object of edges
\coprod_{n\geq 0}(\underbrace{y\times_{x}\cdots\times_{x}y}_{n})
,
where for
n=0
the nullary pullback yields
x
. We will often abuse notation and apply
\mathsf{fc}
directly to the
object of edges, leaving the object of vertices implicit if it is clear from the context.
For
n\geq 2
we will write
\mu_{\mathsf{fc}}^{n-1}:\mathsf{fc}^{n}\Rightarrow\mathsf{fc}
for the composite of multiplication
transformations for the monad. For each integer
k\geq 0
, will also write

\iota_{k}:\mathsf{fc}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{fc}
for the sub-endofunctor given by the
k
-fold pullback on the edge
object, so that on edges
\mathsf{fc}=\coprod_{n\geq 0}\mathsf{fc}_{n}
is the sum
of paths of all finite lengths in the graph.
We will most frequently use this monad when
\mathsf{C}
is either
\mathsf{Set}
, the category of (small) sets, or

\mathsf{Cat}
, the category of (small) categories.

The definition of virtual double category

Definition 2.1

(Virtual Double Categories)
.

A virtual double category (VDC)
\mathbb{D}
consists of the following data:

1.

An underlying category
\mathbb{D}_{0}
of objects
x,y,z,w,\ldots\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{D})
and tight arrows
a,b,c,d,\ldots
.
We write
\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})
for the set of all tight arrows, and
\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})_{x,y}
for the set of tight arrows from
x
to
y
in
\mathbb{D}
.

2.

For each pair of objects
x,y\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{D})
, a set
\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{D})_{x,y}
of loose arrows
\varphi,\chi,\psi,\ldots:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
from
x
to
y
.
The set of all loose arrows in
\mathbb{D}
is denoted by
\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{D})
, and is part of a graph
\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{D})\rightrightarrows\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{D})
.

3.

For each integer
n\geq 0
and each frame of objects, tight arrows, and loose arrows as below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

a set of
n
-ary multicells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

with the specified boundary, denoted by
\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. If
a
and
b
are identity arrows, we also write the set of multicells as
\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\mathbb{D}){\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}\\
\psi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
, and we refer to such multicells with identity tight source and target as
special
or
globular
.
We also refer to
0
-ary multicells as nullary, and
1
-ary multicells as unary.
For an integer
n\geq 0
, we write
\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
for the set of all
n
-ary multicells, and more generally if
k_{1},...,k_{n}
is a sequence of non-negative integers, we will write

\mathsf{Sq}_{k_{1},...,k_{n}}(\mathbb{D}):=\mathsf{Sq}_{k_{1}}(\mathbb{D})\times_{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})}\ldots\times_{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})}\mathsf{Sq}_{k_{n}}(\mathbb{D})

for the set of sequences of compatible multicells, with the
i
th multicell in the sequence having arity
k_{i}
. The special case of the empty sequence is defined by
\mathsf{Sq}_{()}(\mathbb{D}):=\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})
.

4.

For each loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
a loose identity multicell
\text{id}_{\varphi}:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
.

5.

For every
n\geq 0
and
k_{1},...,k_{n}\geq 0
, a composition function

\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\times_{\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{D}))}\mathsf{Sq}_{k_{1},...,k_{n}}(\mathbb{D})\xrightarrow{}\mathsf{Sq}_{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}}(\mathbb{D})

which we denote using fractions, so that the composite of a diagram of multicells as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\varphi_{1,1}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{a_{0}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\varphi_{1,k_{1}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\varphi_{2,1}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{a_{1}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\varphi_{n-1,k_{n-1}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\varphi_{n,1}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{n-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\varphi_{n,k_{n}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{b_{0}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{b_{1}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

is denoted by
\frac{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}}{\beta}
.
As a warning, when
n=0
this reduces to a composition function

\mathsf{Sq}_{0}(\mathbb{D})\times_{\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{D})}\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})\xrightarrow{}\mathsf{Sq}_{0}(\mathbb{D})

which says we can whisker nullary multicells by tight arrows. In this case we can think of the tight arrows as
“nullary multicells with nullary loose target,” an intuition which is reified in the theory of augmented virtual double categories
[
Kou20
]
.

This data is subject to the condition that composition of multicells is compatible with composition of tight sources and targets, is unital with respect to the identity multicells, and is associative with respect to the order of vertical pastings. ∎

In order to simplify notation,
we will often write
\boldsymbol{\varphi}:=(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})

to denote the sequence of loose arrows in the source,
and we write
|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|:=n
for the length of this sequence.
A length zero sequence of loose arrows corresponds to an object
a
in
\mathbb{D}
, denoted
(a).

Similarly, we will denote a pasting of multicells, as below right, using the notation below left:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\varphi_{1,1}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces:=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{b_{0}}}}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{b_{1}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

As above, a length zero sequence of multicells corresponds to a tight arrow in
\mathbb{D}
.
It may be enlightening to note that there is a strict double category (the free one on
\mathbb{D}
)
with the same tight category as

\mathbb{D}
, loose arrows paths of loose arrows in
\mathbb{D},
and multicells arising from paths of
cells in
\mathbb{D},
such that the compact notation above corresponds to working in this
double category.

Note that for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
, we have a category
\mathbb{D}_{1}
whose objects are
loose arrows and whose morphisms are unary multicells in
\mathbb{D}
. For a VDC
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{D}_{1}
fits into a natural graph of categories

s,t:\mathbb{D}_{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{D}_{0}
which we can use to obtain the category

\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
whose objects are sequences of loose arrows and whose morphisms are sequences of unary multicells.

In fact, this assignment extends to a
2
-functor fitting into a
2
-adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Cat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

for the morphisms and 2-cells of VDCs described below in Definition
2.5
and Definition
2.6
, respectively,
where we define the “suspension” by
\Sigma(\mathsf{C})_{0}=\mathsf{C}+\mathsf{C}

and
\Sigma(\mathsf{C})_{1}=\mathsf{C},
with source and
target functors
\Sigma(\mathsf{C}_{1})\rightrightarrows\Sigma(\mathsf{C}_{0})
given by the two coproduct inclusions.
This is a complete specification insofar as

\Sigma(\mathsf{C})
has no non-unary multicells.

With these categories defined, we have a natural profunctor

\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}):\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})^{op}\times\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}
which at a pair

(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\psi)
gives the set of multicells in
\mathbb{D}
with loose source
\boldsymbol{\varphi}

and loose target
\psi
. We will write
\mathsf{MCell}_{n}(\mathbb{D}):=\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\iota_{n}(-),-)
for the
profunctor of
n
-ary multicells, the restriction of
\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})

along the inclusion
\iota_{n}:\mathsf{fc}_{n}{\mathsf{Sq}}(\mathbb{D})\hookrightarrow\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
.
It will also be beneficial to work with the associated categories of these profunctors under the
Grothendieck construction, which by abuse of notation we will denote with the same names.
For instance,
\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})
is the category whose objects are multicells and whose morphisms

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\varphi}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)\to\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}&\\
a^{\prime}&&b^{\prime}\\
&\psi^{\prime}&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
are a pair of a unary multicell
\sigma=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\psi&\\
c&&d\\
&\psi^{\prime}&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and a sequence of unary multicells
\boldsymbol{\tau}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\varphi}&\\
e_{0}&&e_{n}\\
&\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
such that we have the pasting equality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi^{\prime}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

Basic examples of VDCs

Before continuing with definitions, let’s recall some important examples of VDCs which will be relevant for our current work.

Example 2.2

(Quintets)
.

For any 2-category
\mathsf{E}
, we have a VDC
\mathbb{Q}(\mathsf{E})
whose tight category is the underlying
category of
\mathsf{E}
,
whose loose arrows
x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
are just ordinary arrows
x\to y
in
\mathsf{E}
, and the

n
-ary multicells filling a frame

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\varphi}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
correspond to the 2-morphisms

\psi\circ a\to b\circ\varphi_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{1}
in
\mathsf{E}
.
Below, we will use the quintet construction only in the case that
\mathsf{E}
is a mere category,
so the existence of a multicell comes down to a commutativity condition.

Example 2.3

(Span and Cospan Constructions)
.

For any category
\mathsf{E}
we have a VDC
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{E})
whose underlying tight category is
\mathsf{E}
, whose loose arrows
x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
for
x,y\in\mathsf{E}
are cospans
x\xrightarrow{a}z\xleftarrow{b}y
, and whose
n
-ary multicells are commuting diagrams of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{n-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

Composition of multicells is by pasting diagrams.

If the category
\mathsf{E}
has finite pullbacks, then we can similarly define the VDC
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{E})
whose underlying tight category is once again
\mathsf{E}
, but whose loose arrows
x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
are now spans
x\xleftarrow{a}z\xrightarrow{b}y
in
\mathsf{E}
, and whose
n
-ary multicells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{c_{0}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{a_{1}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{b_{1}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{a_{2}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{b_{n-1}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{a_{n}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{b_{n}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{c_{1}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{d_{0}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{d_{1}}}}}

are maps of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}\times_{x_{1}}\ldots\times_{x_{n-1}}y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{c_{0}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{c_{1}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{d_{0}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{d_{1}}}}}

When
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Set}
we write
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}:=\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{Set})
for the large VDC of spans of small sets.

Example 2.4

(Profunctors)
.

There exists a (large) VDC
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
whose underlying category is
\mathsf{Cat}
, the category of small categories and functors,
and whose loose arrows
\mathsf{A}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\mathsf{B}
are profunctors, i.e. functors

\mathsf{A}^{op}\times\mathsf{B}\to\mathsf{Set}
.
A multicell
\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&P_{1}\cdots P_{n}&\\
F_{0}&&F_{1}\\
&Q&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

consists of a family of maps

P_{1}(a_{0},a_{1})\times P_{2}(a_{1},a_{2})\times\cdots\times P_{n}(a_{n-1},a_{n})\to Q(F_{0}(a_{0}),F_{1}(a_{n}))

which are natural in
a_{0}
and
a_{n}
, and extra-natural in
a_{1},...,a_{n-1}
.
Equivalently, such a multicell is given by a natural transformation

\alpha:P_{1}\odot\cdots\odot P_{n}\Rightarrow Q(F_{0},F_{1})
where
\odot
denotes the composition of profunctors
and
Q(F_{0},F_{1}):\mathsf{A}_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\mathsf{A}_{n}
is the pro-functor obtained by restricting
Q:\mathsf{B}_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\mathsf{B}_{1}

along the functors
F_{0}
and
F_{1}
; the more elementary definition is, nevertheless, of interest insofar as
it can be given in more general settings lacking the colimits on which the existence of
\odot
depends.

Morphisms of VDCs

We now define the morphisms and higher morphisms of virtual double categories.

Definition 2.5

(Virtual Double Functors)
.

A virtual double functor (VDF)
F:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
between VDCs
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{B}
consists of the
following data:

1.

A functor on underlying tight categories
F_{0}:\mathbb{A}_{0}\to\mathbb{B}_{0}
.

2.

For every pair of objects
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{A})
an assignment on loose arrows

\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{A})_{x,x^{\prime}}\to\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{B})_{Fx,Fx^{\prime}}
.

3.

For every
n\geq 0
, an assignment on
n
-ary multicells
F:\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\mathbb{A})\to\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\mathbb{B})
respecting tight and loose sources and targets.

This data is required to strictly preserve identity multicells and composition of multicells.
∎

The resulting
1
-category
\mathsf{Vdc}
of (small) VDCs is locally finitely presentable, and hence complete and
cocomplete.

Definition 2.6

(Tight Transformations)
.

A
transformation
between VDFs
F,G:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{B}
, denoted
f:F\Rightarrow G
,
consists of the following data:

1.

A natural transformation of underlying tight functors
f_{0}:F_{0}\Rightarrow G_{0}
.

2.

For each loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{A}
, a multicell

f_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{Sq}_{1}(\mathbb{B})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&F\varphi&\\
f_{x}&&f_{x^{\prime}}\\
&G\varphi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

This data must be natural with respect to pasting multicells in the sense that for an
n
-ary multicell
\alpha
in

\mathbb{A}
with loose source
(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})
and loose target
\psi
, we have the pasting equality

\frac{F(\alpha)}{f_{\psi}}=\frac{f_{\varphi_{1}}\cdots f_{\varphi_{n}}}{G(\alpha)}

∎

To understand how virtual double categories generalize the classical notion of (pseudo) double categories, we can use the notion of opcartesian multicells, which act as witnesses to loose composites.

Definition 2.7

(Opcartesian multicells)
.

A globular
n
-ary multicell

\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}&\\
\text{id}_{x}&&\text{id}_{y}\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in a VDC
\mathbb{D}

is said to be
opcartesian
if any multicell of the form

\beta:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0},\boldsymbol{\varphi},\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}&\\
a&&b\\
&\omega&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

factors uniquely through
\alpha
as a composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{id}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{id}_{\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exists!\beta^{\flat}}}

In this case we say
\alpha
witnesses
\psi
as a loose composite of
(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})
.
If we only have such unique factorizations when
|\boldsymbol{\chi}_{0}|=|\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}|=0
, we say
\alpha

is
weakly opcartesian
, and that
\alpha
witnesses
\psi
as a weak loose composite of

(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})
. We will write a general opcartesian multicell as
\mathsf{opcart}
.
∎

If a sequence of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})
in a VDC
\mathbb{D}
admits a weak
loose composite, we denote the weak composite by
\varphi_{1}\odot\ldots\odot\varphi_{n}
or

\odot(\boldsymbol{\varphi})
. In the case of an empty sequence of loose arrows
(a)
, with

a
an object of
\mathbb{D},
we will denote its (weak) loose composite by
I_{a}:a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}a
, and refer to it as the (weak)
loose unit
at
a
.

Remark 2.8

(The significance of strong composites)
.

When we only have weak composites, these composites need not be associative. Indeed, for any triple of loose arrows
(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3})
we have globular multicells as below comparing associates for the composites, but these need not be isomorphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{1}\odot\varphi_{2}\odot\varphi_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{1}\odot\varphi_{2}\odot\varphi_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\varphi_{1}\odot\varphi_{2})\odot\varphi_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{1}\odot(\varphi_{2}\odot\varphi_{3})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!}

Properties and examples of these VDCs with units and weak composites appear in
[
Gra25
]
under the name of colax double categories. The primary examples of such VDCs in
[
Gra25
]
come from VDCs of topological algebraic objects, where the failure of associativity comes from the non-commutativity of the monoidal structure with topological quotients.

On the other hand, from
[
CS10
, Theorem 5.2]
pseudo-double categories correspond precisely to those
VDCs, called
representable
, with all loose composites.
In particular, loose composites satisfy associativity and unitality axioms via the universal property of the opcartesian multicells witnessing them.
Additionally, under the identification of pseudo-double categories with VDCs, the co-dual of
[
DPP06
, Proposition 2.11]
states that the VDFs are precisely the lax double functors, while the tight transformations of both structures correspond.
We will write
\iota_{\mathbf{Dbl}}:\mathbf{Dbl}_{\ell}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Vdc}
for the fully-faithful 2-embedding of the
2
-category of pseudo-double categories, lax double functors, and tight transformations into the 2-category of VDCs.
We will write
\mathbb{H}:\mathbf{BiCat}_{\ell}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Dbl}_{\ell}
for the horizontal embedding of bicategories into pseudo-double categories.

Definition 2.9
.

We define the loose, tight, and chaotic embeddings into
\mathbf{Vdc}
as follows:

1.

We define the loose embedding
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}:\mathbf{Bicat}_{\ell}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Vdc}

as the composite of the horizontal embedding
\mathbb{H}
with
\iota_{\mathbf{Dbl}}
;

2.

We define the tight embedding
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}:\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Vdc}

by sending a category
\mathsf{C}
to the VDC
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{C})
with tight category
\mathsf{C}
and no loose arrows;

3.

We define the chaotic embedding
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{haotic}:\mathbf{Cat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Vdc}

by sending a category
\mathsf{C}
to the VDC
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{haotic}(\mathsf{C})
with tight category
\mathsf{C}
,
a single loose between every pair of objects in
\mathsf{C}
,
and a unique multicell filling every possible boundary of loose and tight arrows.

∎

The embeddings
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}
and
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{haotic}
fit into a 2-adjoint triple
with the underlying category functor
(-)_{0}
in the center:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Cat}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\mathsf{haotic}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

In particular, limits and colimits in
\mathbf{Vdc}

are computed by limits and colimits in
\mathbf{Cat}
on the level of underlying tight categories.

We will also be interested in the 2-category
\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}
whose objects are “normal VDCs”, that is, VDCs equipped with a choice of loose unit and witnessing opcartesian nullary multicell for each object. The morphisms in
\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}
are VDFs which strictly preserve choices of loose units and opcartesian multicells, while the
2
-cells in
\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}
are arbitrary tight transformations between the VDFs. Then we have a natural forgetful
2
-functor
U:\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}\to\mathbf{Vdc}
, which from
[
FL25
]
fits into an important 2-adjoint triple

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

We will now explicate the 2-functors
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}
and
\mathbb{F}_{u}
appearing in the adjoint triple. First, the 2-functor
\mathbb{F}_{u}
universally adds loose units to a VDC. Explicitly, for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{D})
has the same underlying tight category and has as loose arrows those in
\mathbb{D}
along with a new loose arrow
I_{x}:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x
for every object
x\in\mathbb{D}
. Multicells in
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{D})
with target other than
I_{x}
are given by multicells (possibly with empty loose target) in
\mathbb{D}
after removing all the new loose arrows from the source. There
is a nullary multicell into
I_{x}
for each
x,
and the only other multicells with target
I_{x}
are given by whiskering this nullary multicell with a tight arrow on the domain.

Next, the 2-functor
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}
produces VDCs of modules as defined in
[
Lei04
, Definition 5.3.1]
:

Definition 2.10

(Modules in VDCs)
.

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a VDC. The VDC
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{X})
of
monoids and modules
consists of the following data:

1.

The objects,

, consist of an object

in
\mathbb{X}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

satisfying associativity
\tfrac{(\mu,\text{id}_{\varphi})}{\mu}=\tfrac{(\text{id}_{\varphi},\mu)}{\mu}
and unitality
\tfrac{(\eta,\text{id}_{\varphi})}{\mu}=\text{id}_{\varphi}=\tfrac{(\text{id}_{\varphi},\eta)}{\mu}
.

2.

The tight arrows
(x,\varphi,\mu,\eta)\to(y,\psi,\nu,\theta)
, called
monoid homomorphisms
, consist of a tight arrow
a:x\to y
in
\mathbb{X}
and a unary multicell
\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\varphi&\\
a&&a\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

which is compatible with the multiplications and units in the sense that
\tfrac{(\alpha,\alpha)}{\nu}=\tfrac{\mu}{\alpha}
and
\tfrac{a}{\theta}=\tfrac{\eta}{\alpha}
.

3.

The loose arrows
(x,\varphi,\mu,\eta)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}(y,\psi,\nu,\theta)
, called
modules
, consist of a loose arrow
\chi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{X}
, and left and right action multicells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{R}}

satisfying the bimodule axioms:

\displaystyle\frac{(\mu,\text{id}_{\chi})}{\chi_{L}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\text{id}_{\varphi},\chi_{L})}{\chi_{L}};

\displaystyle\frac{(\text{id}_{\chi},\nu)}{\chi_{R}}=\frac{(\chi_{R},\text{id}_{\psi})}{\chi_{R}}

\displaystyle\frac{(\eta,\text{id}_{\chi})}{\chi_{L}}=\text{id}_{\chi}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\text{id}_{\phi},\theta)}{\chi_{R}};

\displaystyle\frac{(\chi_{L},\text{id}_{\psi})}{\chi_{R}}=\frac{(\text{id}_{\varphi},\chi_{R})}{\chi_{L}}

4.

The
n
-ary multicells
\Theta:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{n}&\\
(a,\alpha)&&(b,\beta)\\

called

, are multicells
\Theta:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{n}&\\

\mathbb{X}

which are compatible with left and right actions of the horizontal multicells:

Example 2.11

(Profunctors via Modules)
.

For any category
\mathsf{E}
with pullbacks, we have the VDC
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathsf{E}):=\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{E}))
whose underlying tight category is the category
\mathsf{Cat}(\mathsf{E})
of categories and functors internal to
\mathsf{E}
, whose loose arrows are internal profunctors, and whose multicells are internal natural transformations. Explicitly, an internal profunctor between internal categories
(\mathsf{A}_{1},\mathsf{A}_{0})
and
(\mathsf{B}_{1},\mathsf{B}_{0})
is a span in
\mathsf{E}
,
\mathsf{A}_{0}\xleftarrow{s}P\xrightarrow{t}\mathsf{B}_{0}
, together with action maps
\mathsf{A}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{A}_{0}}P\to P
and
P\times_{\mathsf{B}_{0}}\mathsf{B}_{1}\to P
, satisfying associativity and unitality axioms. An internal natural transformation
\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&(P_{1}\cdots P_{n})&\\
(F_{1,0},F_{0,0})&&(F_{1,1},F_{0,1})\\
&Q&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

consists of a map of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{A}_{0,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}\times_{\mathsf{A}_{0,1}}\cdots\times_{\mathsf{A}_{0,n-1}}P_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{A}_{0,n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{B}_{0,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{B}_{0,1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0,0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0,1}}}

compatible with the internal and external actions.

As an application of the adjunction
U\dashv\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}
, if
\mathbb{D}\in\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
is any unital VDC with a choice of loose units, we have a natural bijection

\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathsf{E}))\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{E}))

(1)

between normal VDFs into
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathsf{E})
and arbitrary VDFs into
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{E})
.

The same kind of construction produces the VDC of profunctors
between categories enriched over an arbitrary monoidal category
\mathsf{V}

(with no cocompleteness assumptions)
as the modules in the VDC of
matrices
over
\mathsf{V},

and provides an analogous universal property.

For a category
\mathsf{C}
we will write

\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}_{u}(\mathsf{C})=\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{C}))
for the unital tight embedding,
which adds loose units and unique multicells for the loose units to
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{C})
.
These constructions preserve non-empty products, though
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(-)
does not preserve the terminal object.
This leads to the construction of a number of important little VDCs.

Definition 2.12

(Walking multicell VDCs)
.

We now give definitions of the VDCs freely representing
the various shapes of multicells.

•

\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}:=\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}([0])
is the walking object VDC, while
\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}:=\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}_{u}([0])=\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}([0])
is the terminal VDC.
1
1

1

Note that
\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}
has no loose arrows, so no VDC with loose arrows can map into it.

•

\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}:=\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}([1])
is the walking tight arrow VDC which has two objects,
0
and
1
, and a single tight arrow
t:0\to 1
between them, along with identity tight arrows.

•

The walking loose arrow, which we denote by
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
, is the VDC with two objects
0
and
1
, and a single loose arrow
\ell:0\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}1
. Note that
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}=\Sigma([0])
is
the suspension of the terminal category.

•

The walking
n
-ary multicell for an integer
n\geq 0
, denoted
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}
, is freely generated by the multicell

\square_{n}
as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(0,n)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(1,1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square_{n}}

Explicitly, the walking
n
-ary multicell
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}
has, other that the multicells represented above,
nothing but its
n+3
identity tight arrows and its
n+1
identity unary multicells.

∎

It follows that the functors

\mathsf{Ob},\mathsf{Tight},\mathsf{Loose},\mathsf{Sq}_{n}:\mathsf{Vdc}\to\mathsf{Set},\;\;n\geq 0

sending a VDC to its set of objects, its set of tight arrows, its set of loose arrows, and its set of
n
-ary multicells, respectively, are all corepresented, by the corresponding double categories defined above.

The final notion we will use from basic VDC theory is the dual to opcartesian multicells, known as cartesian multicells, which allow for the sliding of tight morphisms up to loose morphisms.

Definition 2.13

(Cartesian Multicells)
.

For a VDC
\mathbb{D}
, a multicell
\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\varphi&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is said to be
cartesian
if any multicell in
\mathbb{D}
of the form below left, factors uniquely through
\alpha
as a composite below right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\chi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\chi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\beta^{\#}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

In this case, we say that
\alpha
witnesses
\varphi
as the
restriction
of
\psi
along
a
and
b
. When such a restriction exists we denote it by
\psi(a,b)
. We will write a general cartesian multicell as
\mathsf{cart}
. If
\mathbb{D}
has loose units, for tight morphisms
a:x\to y\leftarrow z:b
we write
y(a,b)
for the restriction of the loose unit
I_{y}:y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
along
a
and
b.
In particular,
y(a,\operatorname{id}_{y})
is called the
companion
of
a
and
y(\operatorname{id}_{y},b)
is referred to as the
conjoint
of
b.

∎

3
A Sketch Approach to Exponentiability Theorems

In this section, we introduce our general approach to establishing exponentiability in locally
presentable categories, using sketches. The key result we use, which is novel
to us at most in the details of its formulation, is Corollary
3.3
,
which shows that exponentiable objects can
be detected in such categories by preservation of certain canonical colimits
determined by the given cones of the sketch. We then move on to apply this result to several examples building towards
our main case, starting with semicategories, and then revising known results on categories
and multicategories. We find that in all cases, the exponentiable objects are those admitting
“essentially unique decompositions” in an appropriate sense.

3.1
Exponentiability via sketched colimits

Definition 3.1

(Limit Sketches and Models)
.

A
limit sketch

(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{S})
consists of a category
\mathsf{C}
together with a class

\mathsf{S}
of cones in
\mathsf{C}.
We denote
the sketch simply by
\mathsf{C}
by abuse of notation.

A
model
is a functor out of
\mathsf{C}
sending each cone in
\mathsf{S}
to a genuine limit cone. We
denote by
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})
the category of
\mathsf{Set}
-valued models of
\mathsf{C}.

∎

If
\mathsf{C}
is small, then it is a fundamental fact (
[
AR94
, Corollary 1.52]
) that

\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})
is locally presentable and accessible reflective in
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}},
say via the
reflector
L.
We can construct a canonical co-model of
\mathsf{C},
that is, a functor
on
\mathsf{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
sending the cocones in
\mathsf{S}^{\operatorname{op}}
to colimit cocones,
by composing the Yoneda embedding with the reflection:

\mathsf{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}}\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}\xrightarrow{L}\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C}).
In fact we
shall see this is the generic co-model of
\mathsf{C}
in a cocomplete category. If the sketched cones are actual
limit cones in
\mathsf{C},
i.e. the sketch is
realized
, then the co-model just mentioned
is simply the corestriction of the Yoneda embedding to
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C}).

The following theorem is essentially due to
[
Pul70
]
,
but for a more accessible source with discussion of the non-realized case,
one can see
[
Bra21
]
, Theorem 5.6 together with Remark 5.12.

Theorem 3.2

(Pultr)
.

(Universal property of models of a limit sketch)
Let
\mathsf{C}:=(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{S})
be a small limit sketch.
Then the composite
\mathsf{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\xrightarrow{\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}}\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}\xrightarrow{L}\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})

exhibits
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})
as the initial cocomplete category equipped with a
co-model of
\mathsf{C}.

This result characterizes the category of cocontinuous functors
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{D}
as
equivalent to the category of co-models of
\mathsf{C}
in
\mathsf{D}.
For us, this enables the
most important branch of the following corollary. Condition (4) is a convenient “if it quacks like
an exponential, it’s an exponential” criterion.

Corollary 3.3

(Conditions for exponentiability)
.

Consider a small limit sketch
\mathsf{C}=(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{S})
and a model
A
of
\mathsf{C}.

Then the following are equivalent:

1.

A
is exponentiable in
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C}).

2.

For each cone
\gamma:\Delta c\Rightarrow D:\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{C}
in
\mathsf{S},

the functor
(-)\times A:\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})
preserves the colimit

\gamma^{\operatorname{op}}\cdot\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}\cdot L,
where
\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}
is the Yoneda embedding of

\mathsf{C}^{\operatorname{op}}
and
L
is the reflection of
C
-sets into models;
that is, the restriction
L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(-))\times A:\mathsf{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})
is a co-model of
\mathsf{C}

in
\mathsf{C}
-models.

3.

For every
\mathsf{C}
-model
B,
there exists a model
E_{B,A}
together with isomorphisms

\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})(L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C))\times A,B)\cong\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})(L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C)),E_{B,A})
natural in

C\in\mathsf{C}.

4.

Assuming
\mathsf{C}
is a realized sketch: the exponential
B^{A}
as calculated in
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}

is a model of
\mathsf{C}
for every
\mathsf{C}
-model
B.

Proof.

(1)\implies(2)
is a special case of
(-)\times A
preserving all colimits for
A
exponentiable.

(1)\implies(3)
is a special case of the definition of exponentiability itself.

For
(2)\implies(1),

suppose
(-)\times A
preserves the sketched cocones.
Then by the theorem of Pultr, the functor
(-)\times A:\mathsf{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})

extends essentially uniquely to a cocontinuous functor
\bar{A}:\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C}).

Let us check that this extension is indeed
(-)\times A.
One checks using the universal property that
the composite
L\cdot\bar{A}:\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})
must coincide with

\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}\xrightarrow{(-)\times A}\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}\xrightarrow{L}\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C}),

and thus the result follows from the fact that the inclusion
i:\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}

preserves products.

For
(3)\implies(2),
consider a cone
\gamma:\Delta c\Rightarrow D:\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{C}
in

\mathsf{S}.
Then we have the following isomorphisms, natural in
B
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})(L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(x))\times A,B)

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})(L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(x)),E_{B,A})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{j\in\mathsf{J}}{\lim}\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})(L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(Dj)),E_{B,A})

\displaystyle\cong\underset{j\in\mathsf{J}}{\lim}\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})(L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(Dj))\times A,B)

and thus by the Yoneda lemma, the canonical map
L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(x)\times A)\to\lim_{j\in\mathsf{J}}L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(Dj))\times A
is an isomorphism.
(Here we used (3) in the first and last steps, and the fact that
c^{\operatorname{op}}\cdot\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}\cdot L
is a colimit cone in the second step.)

For
(3)\iff(4)
, in the realized sketch case, since
\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C)
is a model, the unit map
\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C)\to iL(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C))
is an isomorphism,
which implies
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})(L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C))\times A,B)\cong\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C)\times A,B)
and

\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})(L(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C)),B^{A})\cong\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}(\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}(C),B^{A}),
which shows that
E_{B,A}
, if it exists,
and
B^{A}
are the same
\mathsf{C}
-set.
∎

Slicing sketches

In exponentiability questions, it is often of interest to find the exponentiable

morphisms
in a finitely complete category
\mathsf{E},
i.e. the exponentiable objects in a slice
category
\mathsf{E}/X,
or equivalently those morphisms, pullback along which admits a right adjoint.
We thus extend the results above to see how to handle exponentiability in slices of categories of models of sketches.

Proposition 3.4
.

Given a limit sketch
\mathsf{C}=(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{S})
and a model
X:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
of
\mathsf{C},

the slice category
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})/X
is equivalent to the category of models of the limit sketch

(\int X,\bar{S}),
where
\int X
is the category of elements of
X
and
\bar{S}
consists of those
cones in
\int X
whose composite with the projection
\pi:\int X\to\mathsf{C}
lies in
\mathsf{S}.

Proof.

It is well-known that the category of
\int X
-sets is equivalent to the slice
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}/X,

with one direction of the equivalence given by the action of the Grothendieck construction on morphisms
in
\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}},
and the other by the left Kan extension
\pi_{!}:\mathsf{Set}^{\int X}\to\mathsf{Set}^{\mathsf{C}}.
Thus it suffices to show that some
\int X
-set
Y:\int X\to\mathsf{Set}
is a model of
(\int X,\bar{S})
if and only if its pushforward
\pi_{!}Y:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
is a model of
\mathsf{C}.

Now, by definition
\pi_{!}Y(c)=\sum_{x:X(c)}Y(x).
If
\gamma:\Delta c\Rightarrow D:\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{C}
is
a cone in
\mathsf{S},
then
\pi_{!}Y
is a model at
c
if and only if the canonical map is
an isomorphism
\pi_{!}Y(c)\to\lim_{j\in\mathsf{J}}\pi_{!}Y(Dj),
i.e.
\sum_{x:X(c)}Y(x)\to\lim_{j\in\mathsf{J}}\sum_{x_{j}:X(Dj)}Y(x_{j}).
Meanwhile
Y
is a model of
(\int X,\bar{S})
at all the lifts
of
\gamma
along
\pi
if and only if, for each
x:X(c),
the comparison map

Y(x)\to\lim_{j\in\mathsf{J}}Y(X(\gamma_{j})(x))
is an isomorphism.

Setting

Q=\lim_{j\in\mathsf{J}}\sum_{x_{j}:X(Dj)}Y(x_{j})
and
Q^{\prime}=\sum_{x:X(c)}\lim_{j\in\mathsf{J}}Y(X(\gamma_{j})(x))
,
it is thus enough to show that
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are isomorphic via an isomorphism commuting
with the canonical maps. Indeed,
Q
has elements given by, for each
j,
choosing
an
x_{j}
and a
y_{j}\in Y(x_{j}),
such that if
f:j\to j^{\prime}
then
Y(f)(y_{j})=y_{j^{\prime}}.
In particular
this implies that
X(f)(x_{j})=x_{j^{\prime}},
so
(x_{j})
is in
\lim_{j}X(Dj),
which gives the desired
isomorphism
Q\to Q^{\prime},
using the model condition
X(c)\cong\lim_{j\in\mathsf{J}}X(Dj)
for
X.

∎

This allows for proofs of exponentiability of morphisms in slices of categories of models of sketches
not much harder than in the absolute case.

Corollary 3.5
.

Let
\mathsf{C}=(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{S})
be a limit sketch and
X:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
a model of
\mathsf{C}.
Then a morphism
f:A\to X
is exponentiable in
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})/X
if and only if, for every
cone
\lambda:\Delta c\to(D:\mathsf{J}\to\mathsf{C})
in
\mathsf{S}
and every morphism
\bar{c}:L\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}c\to X,
the functor
(-)\times_{X}A:\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})/X\to\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})/X
preserves the colimiting
cocone
\bar{\lambda}:L\circ\mathord{\text{\begin{CJK}{min}よ\end{CJK}}}\circ D\Rightarrow\Delta\bar{c}.

Since colimits in slices are computed as in the base category, this condition is merely a fibered
version of exponentiability in
\mathsf{Mod}(\mathsf{C})
itself, and thus frequently no harder to check.

3.2
A case study: exponentiable semicategories

We warm up by investigating the case of exponentiable
semicategories, which is a bit too trivial to allow for many
exponentiable objects in the end, but simple enough to clearly exhibit the key ideas.

Definition 3.6

(Category of semicategories)
.

A
semicategory

A
consists of a graph
A_{1}\rightrightarrows A_{0}
equipped
with an associative composition operation
A_{1}\times_{A_{0}}A_{1}\to A_{1}.
A
semifunctor

A\to B
is a graph morphism respecting composition. We write
\mathsf{Semicat}
for the
category of small semicategories and semifunctors.

Alternatively,
\mathsf{Semicat}
is the category of models of a limit sketch
whose underlying category
\mathsf{C}
is the opposite of the category of the

{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.05 0.91 L 14.38 5.71 L 5.93 16.66 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

∎

A semicategory is thus like a category possibly lacking identity morphisms, though these are unique
when they exist, by the usual argument.
As defined,
\mathsf{C}
is a full subcategory of
\mathsf{Semicat},
which implies this
sketch is realized. We may write

also for the semicategories
represented by the objects of the sketch above.
Note that, given the absence of identities, the free semicategory
\bullet
on one object actually contains
no
morphisms at all–in particular,
\bullet
is not the terminal semicategory.

Thus we have the colimit decompositions
\triangle\cong{\downarrow}+_{\bullet}{\downarrow}
and
