and by
Corollary
3.3
, a semicategory

A
is exponentiable if and only if the product functor with
A
preserves those two colimits.
Let us now flesh out what this preservation means concretely.

Proposition 3.7

(Explicit conditions for exponentiability)
.

A semicategory
A
is exponentiable if and only if every morphism
f:x\to y
in
A
admits a unique
binary factorization
f=g\cdot h.

Proof.

We can write the objects of
A\times\triangle
as
(a,i)
for
a\in A
and
i=0,1,2,
and similarly
the morphisms as
(f,i,i^{\prime}):(a,i)\to(a^{\prime},i^{\prime})
for
f:a\to a^{\prime}
in
A
and
i<i^{\prime}.

The objects of
A\times{\downarrow}\underset{A\times\bullet}{+}A\times{\downarrow}
are the same,
as are morphisms of the form
(f,0,1)
and
(f,1,2),
but the morphisms
(a_{0},0)\to(a_{2},2)
are
now given precisely by composable pairs
a_{0}\to a_{1}\to a_{2}
in
A.

Therefore, the canonical semifunctor

c:A\times{\downarrow}\underset{A\times\bullet}{+}A\times{\downarrow}\to A\times\triangle

is always bijective on objects and on the two “short” classes of morphisms, while
for the “long” morphisms,
c
is full at
(a_{0},0),(a_{2},2)
if and only if every morphism

a_{0}\to a_{2}
has some factorization
a_{0}\to a_{1}\to a_{2}
in
A,
and faithful if and only if
there is at most one such factorization. Thus,
c
is an isomorphism if and only if
every morphism in
A
admits a unique binary factorization. Analogously,
the functor
A\times(-)
preserves the distinguished colimit for

if and only if every morphism admits a unique ternary factorization.

To reduce this pair of conditions to the single condition given in the proposition
statement, consider that uniqueness of ternary factorizations reduces to uniqueness of
binary factorizations. Indeed, given binary decompositions,
we can give a ternary decomposition of any morphism by repeated binary decompositions,
while for uniqueness, if we have
f_{1}f_{2}f_{3}=f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}f_{3}^{\prime}
in
A,
then by uniqueness of binary
factorizations we have
f_{1}f_{2}=f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}
and
f_{3}=f_{3}^{\prime}
, and then another application
of uniqueness of binary factorizations gives
f_{1}=f_{1}^{\prime}
and
f_{2}=f_{2}^{\prime}.

∎

Thus we have a single, simple necessary and sufficient condition for exponentiability
of a semicategory: the existence of unique binary factorizations.
Note that this condition is enormously strong. For instance, it
never
holds for a
non-discrete semicategory which happens to admit identity morphisms, since if
f:x\to y
then we
have
f=\operatorname{id}_{x}\cdot f=f\cdot\operatorname{id}_{y}
as two distinct factorizations of
f.
Thus no non-discrete
category is exponentiable when viewed as a semicategory.
The most complex connected exponentiable semicategory, which isn’t saying much,
is given by augmenting
\triangle
with an identity morphism at the middle
object
1.

Having rather thoroughly understood the exponentiable objects of
\mathsf{Semicat},
we now
consider the exponentials themselves, via techniques that will serve us well in the more
complicated cases below.

Proposition 3.8

(Structure of exponential semicategories)
.

Suppose
A
and
B
are semicategories such that the exponential
B^{A}
exists. Then:

•

The objects of
B^{A}
are the functions
\operatorname{Ob}(A)\to\operatorname{Ob}(B).

•

The morphisms
\alpha:F_{1}\to F_{2}
in
B^{A}
are the dependent functions
2
2

2

This is a choice of morphism in
B
for each morphism in
A,
over the
F_{i},
satisfying no naturality conditions.

\alpha~:~\underset{f:a_{1}\to a_{2}\in A}{\prod}B(F_{1}(a_{1}),F_{2}(a_{2}))

•

Given
\alpha:F_{1}\to F_{2},

\beta:F_{2}\to F_{3},
and
\gamma:F_{1}\to F_{3}
in
B^{A}
,
we have the equation
\gamma=\alpha\cdot\beta
just if for every composable pair
f,g
in
A,

we have
\gamma_{f\cdot g}=\alpha_{f}\cdot\beta_{g}
in
B.

Proof.

The three claims follow easily from the representability of objects and arrows and the universal
property of exponentials. Indeed, the objects of any semicategory
C
are the semifunctors

\bullet\to C,
and so the objects of
B^{A}
are the semifunctors
\bullet\times A\to B.
Since

\bullet
contains no arrows, the domain
\bullet\times A
is the discrete semicategory on the
objects of
A,
which computes the objects as desired.

Similarly, a morphism in
B^{A}
is given by a semifunctor
{\downarrow}\times A\to B,

where the domain consists of two copies of
\bullet\times A,
with the only arrows
of the form
f:(a,0)\to(a^{\prime},1)
for
f:a\to a^{\prime}
in
A.
This gives the desired characterization.

Commutative triangles in
B^{A}
correspond to semifunctors
T:\triangle\times A\to B.
As we have
seen in deriving the unique binary decomposition condition above, the commutative triangles
in
\triangle\times A
have as their edges maps
(f,0,1),(g,1,2),(fg,0,2)
for composable
f,g
in

A.
Thus such a
T
is given by a triple
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
of dependent functions as above such
that
\gamma_{f\cdot g}=\alpha_{f}\cdot\beta_{g}
for composable
f,g
in
A,
as was to be shown.
∎

Another angle on exponentiability which inspired Arkor’s conjecture in
[
Ark25
]
is via Grothendieck constructions.
This angle is made clearest when we consider exponentiability of a semifunctor
\pi:E\to B
in
\mathsf{Semicat}/B,
generalizing from the case
\mathsf{Semicat}=\mathsf{Semicat}/1.
In the nicest case,
such a
\pi
can be disintegrated into a semifunctor
\nabla\pi:B\to\mathsf{Set},
sending
b\mapsto E_{b}.
But semifunctoriality of this operation requires
\pi
to be a
discrete opfibration
3
3

3

The only natural notion of opfibration for semicategories is the discrete, since there is no way to arrange a whole semicategory in a “fiber” over an object of a semicategory, thus no Grothendieck construction for (pseudo)semifunctors into
\mathsf{Semicat}.
, a strong condition.

When
\pi
is not necessarily a discrete opfibration, we may instead disintegrate
\pi
into a
virtual double functor
\nabla\pi:\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(B)\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan},
where
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}
extends the embedding
of bicategories to VDCs discussed in the previous section to semi(bi)categories; thus
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(B)
has
discrete tight category on the objects of
B,
loose arrows given by arrows of
B,
and
cells given by composites in
B.

Then we define
\nabla\pi
on objects by
b\mapsto\pi^{-1}(b)
and on a morphism

f:b_{1}\to b_{2}
by the span
\pi^{-1}(b_{1})\xleftarrow{\operatorname{dom}}\pi^{-1}(f)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{cod}}\pi^{-1}(b_{2}),

with action on multicells given by composition in
E.

It is then natural to consider the possibility that
\nabla\pi
preserve opcartesian multicells, which
is the virtual analogue of the condition that a lax double functor be pseudo. This condition means
precisely that every factorization of a morphism
\pi(f)
in
B
lifts uniquely to a factorization
of
f
in
E.
Since semicategories over
B
can be sketched by coloring the sketch for plain
semicategories by objects and arrows of
B,
(cf. Proposition
3.4
) we can repeat
the argument for the absolute case above
to see that this condition is equivalent to exponentiability of
\pi
in
\mathsf{Semicat}/B,

and furthermore that it suffices to consider binary factorizations alone. We have finished
the proof of our general result for semicategories:

Proposition 3.9

(Exponentiable semifunctors)
.

For a semifunctor
\pi:E\to B,
the following are equivalent:

1.

\pi
is exponentiable in
\mathsf{Semicat}/B.

2.

The virtual double functor
\mathbb{L}B\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
corresponding to
\pi
preserves opcartesian multicells.

3.

Given a morphism
f
in
E,
every factorization of
\pi(f)
in
B
lifts uniquely to a factorization in
E.

4.

As in (3), but for binary factorizations alone.

3.3
Exponentiable categories and essentially unique factorizations

We now move to more general category structures where, once some identity maps come into play,
exponentiability becomes more complicated, while still following the general shape of the story outlined
for mere semicategories above.
The exponentiable objects in (slices of)
\mathsf{Cat}
and of
\mathsf{Multicat}
are known. In the first
case, it is an old result that the exponentiable functors
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{B}
are precisely the Conduché fibrations (
[
Con72
,
Gir64
]
)

Definition 3.10

(Conduché fibration)
.

A functor
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{B}
is a
Conduché fibration
if for every morphism
f:e_{1}\to e_{3}
in
\mathsf{E}

and for every factorization
\pi(f)=g_{1}\cdot g_{2}
in
B,
the category
\mathsf{Fact}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
of factorizations of
f
lifting

(g_{1},g_{2})
is connected.
∎

To be sure, we spell out that:

•

A connected category has one connected component; in particular, it is nonempty.

•

For the category
\mathsf{Fact}_{g_{1},g_{2}}:
If
e_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}e_{2}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}e_{3}
and

e_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}^{\prime}}e_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f_{2}^{\prime}}e_{3}

are two factorizations lifting
(g_{1},g_{2}),
then a morphism
(f_{1},f_{2})\to(f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime})
in
\mathsf{Fact}_{g_{1},g_{2}}
is given by a morphism
h:e_{2}\to e_{2}^{\prime}
such that

\pi(h)=\operatorname{id}_{b_{2}}
and
f_{1}^{\prime}=f_{1}\cdot h,

f_{2}=h\cdot f_{2}^{\prime},
as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{2}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}^{\prime}}

Mnemonically, we can say that exponentiable functors admit
essentially
unique liftings of
factorizations, whereas exponentiable semifunctors had to be provide the much stronger

strictly
unique
liftings. As we alluded to above, essential uniqueness does not even make sense in the world of semicategories, because in the absence of identity morphisms one has no notion of fiber.
On the other hand, we
can
give the definition with unique liftings in the case of categories,
and then we will have recovered the notion of
discrete
Conduché fibration.
4
4

4

Conduché
fibrations jointly generalize Grothendieck fibrations and opfibrations while discrete Conduché
fibrations jointly generalize discrete fibrations and discrete opfibrations.

Let us put our sketch machinery to work to re-derive this characterization of exponentiable functors.
Though the theorem is old, we suggest that our approach allows for a more complete proof than
most authors have had the patience to give without the sketch machinery.

For a functor
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{B},
we can treat it as a semifunctor and construct the corresponding virtual double functor
\nabla\pi:\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{B})\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
,
but this
\nabla\pi
will preserve opcartesian multicells only when
\pi
is a
discrete

Conduché fibration.
To capture the general case, we recall (Eq.
1
) that virtual double functors
into
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
from unital VDCs correspond to normal
virtual double functors into
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
.
What is crucial is that this correspondence does
not
reflect which virtual double
functors preserve opcartesian multicells, since the composition in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
quotients
that in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
by the inner actions of the profunctors being composed. Thus it is a weaker
condition that
\nabla\pi
preserves opcartesian multicells when viewed as a virtual double functor into

\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof},
and that weaker condition is the crucial one:

Theorem 3.11

(Conduché, Giraud)
.

(Exponentiable functors) For a functor
\pi:\mathsf{E}\to\mathsf{B},
the following are equivalent:

1.

\pi
is exponentiable in
\mathsf{Cat}/\mathsf{B}.

2.

\pi
admits essentially unique liftings of factorizations, i.e. is a Conduché fibration.

3.

The double functor
\nabla\pi:\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{B})\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
preserves opcartesian multicells.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{Cat}
is locally finite presentable, (1) is equivalent to the preservation of
sketched colimits in a sketch for categories over
\mathsf{B}
by the product functor with
\pi

(Corollary
3.3
).
We can give such a sketch in terms of object, arrow, triangle, and tetrahedron types colored by
the objects and arrows of
\mathsf{B},
by extending the semicategory sketch above
with structure for identity morphisms and then applying Proposition
3.4
.
Then there is one sketched colimit for each commutative triangle and for each commutative
tetrahedron in
\mathsf{B}.

Given a commutative triangle
g=(b_{0}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}b_{1}\xrightarrow{g_{2}}b_{2})
in
\mathsf{B},
the corresponding colimit
is

g:\triangle\to\mathsf{B}=(g_{1}:{\downarrow}\to\mathsf{B})\underset{b_{1}:\bullet\to\mathsf{B}}{+}(g_{2}:{\downarrow}\to\mathsf{B}).

Now, the product
\mathsf{E}\times_{\mathsf{B}}g
has as arrows over
g
precisely the arrows of

\mathsf{E}
over
g,

while the pushout
(\mathsf{E}\times_{\mathsf{B}}g_{1})\underset{\mathsf{E}\times_{\mathsf{B}}\{b_{1}\}}{+}(\mathsf{E}\times_{\mathsf{B}}g_{2})
has as arrows over
g
the composable pairs of lifts of
g_{1}
and
g_{2},

respectively, up to arrows over
\operatorname{id}_{b_{1}},
that is,
the set of connected components
\pi_{0}\mathsf{Fact}_{g_{1},g_{2}}.
This shows that

(1)\implies(2).

Now,
(2)
is equivalent to the binary case of
(3)
by unfolding the definitions: if

g=(b_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{1}}}{{\to}}b_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{2}}}{{\to}}b_{2})
in
B
and we have the corresponding
opcartesian cell
\alpha:g_{1},g_{2}\Rightarrow g
in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{B}),
then by definition of
the multicells in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof},
the image
\nabla\pi(\alpha)
is the map
\int^{\pi_{b_{1}}}\pi_{g_{1}}(e_{0},e_{1})\pi_{g_{2}}(e_{1},e_{2})\to\pi_{g}(e_{0},e_{2}),

natural in
e_{0}\in\pi_{b_{0}}
and
e_{2}\in\pi_{b_{2}},
and
induced by the composition in
E.
The equivalence relation induced on paths from
e_{0}
to
e_{2}

over
(g_{1},g_{2})
in the coend is precisely that of lying in the same connected component in the
category of factorizations lifting
(g_{1},g_{2})
defined above. Thus
\nabla\pi(\alpha)
is an isomorphism if and only if
\pi
admits essentially unique liftings of the factorization

(g_{1},g_{2}).

Next, the binary case of
(3)
implies the general case because every opcartesian multicell
in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{B})
may be decomposed in terms of binary and unary ones, where a unary opcartesian
cell is just an identity multicell, thus certainly preserved. In
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof},
this becomes the observation
that, for instance, we have a natural isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int^{\pi_{b_{1}},\pi_{b_{2}}}\pi_{g_{1}}(e_{0},e_{1})\pi_{g_{2}}(e_{1},e_{2})\pi_{g_{3}}(e_{2},e_{3})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int^{\pi_{b_{2}}}\left(\int^{\pi_{b_{1}}}\pi_{g_{1}}(e_{0},e_{1})\pi_{g_{2}}(e_{1},e_{2})\right)\pi_{g_{3}}(e_{2},e_{3})},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

which is a case of the Fubini theorem for coends.
[
Kel82
, 2.8]
Thus we have
(2)\iff(3).

Finally, for
(3)
implies
(1),
it remains only to show that if
\pi
is a Conduché fibration,
then product with
\pi
preserves the sketched colimits for tetrahedra as well. Preserving the
colimit in
\mathsf{Cat}/\mathsf{B}
for a commutative tetrahedron
h=(b_{0}\xrightarrow{h_{1}}b_{1}\xrightarrow{h_{2}}b_{2}\xrightarrow{h_{3}}b_{3})
means that if
\pi(f)=h,
then there are essentially
unique factorizations of
f
lifting
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}),
where the category of such factorizations has
morphisms of this form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{2}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{3}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{3}}

But the set of such factorizations is then precisely the coend

\int^{\pi_{b_{1}},\pi_{b_{2}}}\pi_{g_{1}}(e_{0},e_{1})\pi_{g_{2}}(e_{1},e_{2})\pi_{g_{3}}(e_{2},e_{3}),

which gives the result.
∎

We thus recover the very well-known fact that all small categories are exponentiable. In terms
of the explicit structure of the exponential
\mathsf{A}^{\mathsf{B}},
the key point is that, rather than
the components
\alpha_{f}
for morphisms
f:x\to y
in
\mathsf{B}
from the semicategory case, which
are generally not composable, we can use the naturality condition

\alpha_{f}=\alpha_{x}\cdot G(f)=F(f)\cdot\alpha_{y}
to reduce to the components at identities, which
one knows how to compose. In
\mathsf{Cat}/\mathsf{B},
one must use the decomposition condition
more explicitly to compose fibered natural transformations.

3.4
Exponentiable multicategories

Pisani
[
Pis14
]
characterized the exponentiable multicategories as the promonoidal categories.
This result is correct, but he relied on an inaccurate description of promonoidal categories in his
reference
[
DPS05
]
, so we take this opportunity to clarify the situation.

A multicategory
\mathsf{M}
is a model of a limit sketch including a type
O
for objects and types
A_{n}
for

n
-ary arrows for all
n\geq 0,
as well as for the gluings
A_{n}\star_{i}A_{m}
of an

n
-ary morphism to an
m
-ary morphism at position
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\},
and finally for the
threefold gluings
A_{n}\star_{i}A_{m}\star_{j}A_{\ell}.
There are sketched cones for the twofold gluings
for every
n,i,m
and the threefold gluings for every
n,i,m,j,\ell.

By Corollary
3.3
, a multicategory
\mathsf{M}
is exponentiable if and only if the functor

(-)\times\mathsf{M}:\mathsf{Multicat}\to\mathsf{Multicat}
preserves the colimits gluing two, respectively
three, morphisms at all arities at positions. Let us write
O,A_{n},
and so on also for the
multicategories freely generated by an element of the corresponding type in the sketch.
Then for exponentiability
we must have, first of all, isomorphisms

\mathsf{M}\times A_{n}+_{(\mathsf{M}\times O)_{i}}\mathsf{M}\times A_{m}\cong\mathsf{M}\times(A_{n}\star_{i}A_{m}).

Here on the left, we have
n+m-1
-ary morphisms given by formal composites
of every composable pair of the form below in
\mathsf{M},
taken up to unary morphisms on either
side at the
y_{i}
position:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}

Such formal composites on the left map to their genuine composite on the right. We thus have as a
necessary condition for exponentiability that each map from a coend, taken over unary morphisms:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\int^{y_{i}}\mathsf{M}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m};y_{i})\times\mathsf{M}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{i},\ldots,y_{n};z)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{M}(y_{1},\ldots,y_{i-1},x_{1},\ldots,x_{m},y_{i+1},\ldots,y_{n};z)}}

is bijective. This is precisely what it means for
\mathsf{M}
to be a promonoidal category.

5
5

5

The
issue in
[
DPS05
]
is that the authors restrict to the cases

m=0,n=2
and
m=n=2,
which is insufficient. This corrected definition is the same as the notion
recently given by Román
[
Rom24
]
and called there
malleable
multicategories, but
Román does not recall the connection to exponentiability.

To finish applying our corollary, we have only to get past the sketched cones for gluings
of threefold composites. This can be reduced to the binary case much as in the situation of semicategories
treated above, though one has to track the coend equivalence relation rather than working
with equalities directly. Thus it’s more expedient to use the Fubini theorem on iterated coends,
which again gets the desired result:

Proposition 3.12

(Pisani)
.

(Exponentiable multicategories)
A multicategory
\mathsf{M}
is exponentiable if and only if it is the multicategory associated to a
promonoidal category.

With these warmups complete, we are ready to turn to our subject of primary interest.

4
Exponentiability via Decompositions

We will characterize the exponentiable VDCs through a collection of equivalent formulations in terms of decomposition properties for the multicells in the VDC
\mathbb{D}
.
However, before characterizing the exponentiable VDCs, we can use their universal property to describe the objects, tight arrows, loose arrows, and multicells
in the exponential when it exists.
Here we will use the observation from Section
2
that the functors
\mathsf{Ob},\mathsf{Tight},\mathsf{Loose},\mathsf{Sq}_{n}:\mathsf{Vdc}\to\mathsf{Set}
,
n\geq 0
, are all corepresented.
Additionally, we will need to understand products in
\mathsf{Vdc}
with the corepresenting VDCs, along with functors out of such products.

4.1
The structure of the exponential, if it exists

Proposition 4.1

(Elements of the exponential VDC)
.

If
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{E}
are VDCs such that the exponential
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}}
exists, then the VDC
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}}
consists of the following data:

1.

Objects
F\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b},\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}\times\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
correspond to functors
F:\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{0}
on underlying tight categories.
6
6

6

Note that, since
\mathsf{Ob}
, lacking loose arrows and multicells, is not terminal, the objects of an exponential are not simply the virtual double functors. Exponentiability will nonetheless be seen below to imply the existence of the virtual double category of virtual double functors.

2.

Tight arrows
f\in\mathsf{Arr_{T}}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight},\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}\times\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
correspond to natural transformations
f:F\Rightarrow G
between functors
F,G:\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{0}
.

3.

Loose arrows
\Phi\in\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose},\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\times\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
correspond to maps of spans of categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{E}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{0}}}

4.

Unary multicells in
\mathsf{Sq}_{1}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{1},\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{1}\times\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
correspond to 2-cells in the 2-category of spans of categories between maps of spans as above.

5.

Multicells
\Gamma\in\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n},\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}\times\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E}),
for
n\neq 1
are given by a map of profunctors
\mathsf{MCell}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\to\partial^{*}\mathsf{MCell}_{n}(\mathbb{E})
over
\mathbb{E}_{0}^{2},
where

\partial:\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathbb{D}_{1})^{op}\times\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathbb{E}_{1})^{op}\times\mathbb{E}_{1}
is the functor which together with the natural transformations putting
\mathsf{MCell}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
over
\mathbb{E}_{0}^{2}
determines the boundary data of
\Gamma.

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

Since
\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}
contains nothing but a single object with its tight identity arrow,
we see crossing with
\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}
eliminates all loose arrows and multicells, that is,

\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}\times\mathbb{D}
is the virtual double category with tight category
\mathbb{D}_{0}

and no loose arrows or multicells. This and the analogous computation for

\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}\times\mathbb{D}
provide the proofs of claims 1. and 2.

Next, observe that
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\times\mathbb{D}
consists of two copies of the underlying tight category of

\mathbb{D}
, corresponding to the two objects
0
and
1
in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
, along with a loose arrow

(\ell,\varphi):(0,x)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}(1,y)
between these two copies for each loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{D}
.
Since
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
contains one unary multicell, namely
\text{id}_{\ell}
, the product
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\times\mathbb{D}
contains,
for each unary multicell
\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\varphi&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\mathbb{D}
,
a unary multicell
(\text{id}_{\ell},\alpha):\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&(\ell,\varphi)&\\
(\text{id}_{0},a)&&(\text{id}_{1},b)\\
&(\ell,\psi)&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in

\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\times\mathbb{D}
.

Thus, a VDF
\Phi:\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
consists of the data of two functors
on underlying tight categories,
F_{0},F_{1}:\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{0}
, along with for each loose arrow

\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{D}
, a loose arrow
\Phi(\ell,\varphi):F_{0}(x)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}F_{1}(y)
in
\mathbb{E}
, and for each
unary multicell
\alpha
in
\mathbb{D}
, a unary multicell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}(y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}(z)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}(w)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\ell,\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\ell,\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\text{id}_{\ell},\alpha)}

in
\mathbb{E}
. Further, this assignment is functorial under vertical composition of unary multicells, and hence amounts to a map
of spans of categories as in claim 3.

From our description of
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}
in Section
2
,
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}\times\mathbb{D}
has
n+3
copies of
\mathbb{D}_{0}
,
loose arrows and unary multicells as in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\times\mathbb{D}
between these copies for each loose arrow in
\mathbb{D}
and
loose arrow in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}
, tight arrows between the copies on the edges of
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}
, and for each
n
-ary multicell

\alpha
in
\mathbb{D}
, an
n
-ary multicell
(\square_{n},\alpha)
.
A VDF
\Gamma:\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
thus consists of the data of
n+3
functors on underlying tight categories,

F_{0},...,F_{n},G_{0},G_{1}:\mathbb{D}_{0}\to\mathbb{E}_{0}
, together with two natural transformations

f_{0}:F_{0}\Rightarrow G_{0}
and
f_{1}:F_{n}\Rightarrow G_{1}
,
n+1
functors

\Phi_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{n},\Psi:\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathbb{E}_{1}
, as above, and for each

n
-ary multicell
\beta:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\phi}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\mathbb{D}
,
an
n
-ary multicell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi_{1}(\varphi_{1})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{0,a}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi_{n}(\varphi_{n})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{1,b}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Psi(\psi)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma(\beta)}}

where for instance
f_{0,a}=f_{0,x_{0}}\cdot G_{0}(a)=F_{0}(a)\cdot f_{0,y_{0}}.

This data is functorial in the sense that the composite of the pasting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}w_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}w_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi_{1}(\varphi_{1}^{\prime})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{F_{0}c_{0}}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi_{2}(\varphi_{2}^{\prime})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{F_{1}c_{1}}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi_{n}(\varphi_{n}^{\prime})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{F_{n}c_{n}}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{\Phi_{1}(\varphi_{1})}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{0,a}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi_{2}(\varphi_{2})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi_{n}(\varphi_{n})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{1,b}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Psi(\psi)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{G_{0}d_{0}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{G_{1}d_{1}}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\Psi(\psi^{\prime})}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{1}(\delta_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{2}(\delta_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{n}(\delta_{n})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma(\beta)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi(\gamma)}}

is equal to
\Gamma\left(\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{n}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{19.09854pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\beta\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{19.09854pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\gamma\end{array}\right)
.

To establish the lighter axiomatization for the unary case as in 4,
consider that in the product
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{1}\times\mathbb{D},

for any
\beta:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\chi&\\
a&&b\\
&\omega&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\mathbb{D},
we have

(\square_{1},\beta)=\frac{(\square_{1},\text{id}_{\chi})}{(\text{id}_{\ell},\beta)}=\frac{(\text{id}_{\ell_{1}},\beta)}{(\square_{1},\text{id}_{\omega})}.

This shows that the unary multicells in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{1}\times\mathbb{D}
are generated by those coming from
the boundary together with those of the form
(\square_{1},\text{id}_{\chi})
, which is the fundamental
reason why
a modulation may be defined with components indexed by proarrows of
\mathbb{D}
, rather than unary multicells.

Under this presentation, the only relations in the category of loose arrows of
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{1}\times\mathbb{D}
are of the form

\frac{(\square_{1},\text{id}_{\chi})}{(\text{id}_{\ell},\beta)}=\frac{(\text{id}_{\ell_{1}},\beta)}{(\square_{1},\text{id}_{\omega})}.

Thus we can give a unary cell in
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}}
by giving

\Gamma_{\varphi}:=\Gamma(\square_{1},\text{id}_{\varphi}):\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\Phi_{\varphi}&\\
f_{0,x}&&f_{1,y}\\
&\Psi_{\varphi}&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

for every
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{D},
subject to the naturality condition, for every

\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\varphi&\\
&&\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\mathbb{D}.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{1}y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\varphi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{0,x_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1,x_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\varphi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi_{\varphi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{0,y_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1,y_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi_{\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi_{\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma_{\varphi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi_{\alpha}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma_{\psi}}}

Indeed, such a
\Gamma
amounts to a 2-cell in the 2-category of spans of categories.
∎

The exponential versus the VDC of virtual double functors

The exponential
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}}
is often not the VDC we are interested in, since its objects
are functors between underlying tight categories rather than VDFs between the VDCs.
However, once we apply the
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(-)
construction (Definition
2.10
), the resulting
VDC
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E}):=\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})
does have VDFs as objects.
Note that if
\mathbb{D}={\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}}
is the terminal VDC, which is representable, as is the
terminal object in any cartesian category.
That is,
\mathbb{E}^{{\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}}}\cong\mathbb{E}
for any VDC
\mathbb{E}
, so that

\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}({\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}},\mathbb{E})\cong\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E}),
which essentially reproduces
Benabou’s old observation that lax functors from the point are monads (in a bicategory,
for Benabou.
[
Benabou1967
]
)

Lemma 4.2
.

If
\mathbb{D}
is an exponentiable VDC, then the VDC
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E}):=\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})
exists for any VDC
\mathbb{E}
, and it has VDFs as objects with tight transformations as tight arrows.

Proof.

The existence of the VDC
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E}):=\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})
follows immediately from the existence of
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}}
since
\mathbb{D}
is assumed to be exponentiable. We can give an elegant description of the data in
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
using the 2-adjoint triple

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

described in Section
2
. Explicitly, this adjoint triple induces a pair of 2-adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U\circ\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U\circ\mathbb{F}_{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U\circ\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{U\circ\mathbb{F}_{u}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

From these adjunctions we obtain the natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathbf{Vdc}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Vdc}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}}))\cong\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{A}),\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathbf{Vdc}(\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{A}),\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}})\cong\mathbf{Vdc}(\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{A})\times\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})

Thus for
\mathbb{D}
exponentiable, we have the adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{u}(-)\times\mathbb{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(which also appears as Theorem 6.10 in
[
Ark25
]
)

Using this adjunction we can describe the data for the VDC
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
as follows:

1.

Objects are VDFs
\mathbb{D}\cong{\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
.

2.

Tight arrows are VDFs
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
.
Such VDFs
F:\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
correspond to tight transformations
between the VDFs
F_{0},F_{1}:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
obtained by restricting
F

along
s\times\text{id},t\times\text{id}:\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}\times\mathbb{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight})\times\mathbb{D}
.
The component of the tight transformation
f:F_{0}\to F_{1}
associated to
F

at an object
x\in\mathbb{D}
is given by
f_{x}:=F(t,\text{id}_{x})
,
where
t
is the unique non-identity tight arrow in
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight})
,
while the component at a loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{D}

is given by
f_{\varphi}:=F(I_{t},\text{id}_{\varphi})
.
Naturality of
f
follows from functoriality of
F
as follows
for
a:x\to y
and
\alpha:\boldsymbol{\varphi}\to\psi
:

\frac{f_{x}}{F_{1}(a)}=\frac{F(t,\text{id}_{x})}{F(\text{id}_{1},a)}=F(t,a)=\frac{F(\text{id}_{0},a)}{F(t,\text{id}_{y})}=\frac{F_{0}(a)}{f_{y}}

and

\frac{(f_{\varphi_{1}}\cdots f_{\varphi_{n}})}{F_{1}(\alpha)}=\frac{(F(I_{t},\text{id}_{\varphi_{1}})\cdots F(I_{t},\text{id}_{\varphi_{n}}))}{F(\mathsf{opcart},\alpha)}=\frac{F(\mathsf{opcart},\alpha)}{F(I_{t},\text{id}_{\psi})}=\frac{F_{0}(\alpha)}{f_{\psi}}

If we instead started with a tight transformation
f:F_{0}\to F_{1}
, we could use these naturality
equalities to define the VDF
F
.

3.

Loose arrows are VDFs
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
.

4.

n
-ary multicells are VDFs
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
.

This completes the proof.
∎

Explication 4.3

(Modules)
.

When
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{E}
are pseudo-double categories, lax double functors of the form

\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
correspond to Paré’s modules,
while lax double functors of the form
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}

are what Paré calls multimodulations (c.f.
[
Par11
]
).
In general for VDCs,
we can unpack the data of a VDF
H:\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}

as follows:

(M0)

VDFs
F,G:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
obtained by restricting along the inclusions

s\times\text{id},t\times\text{id}:\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}\times\mathbb{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})\times\mathbb{D}
;

(M1)

For each loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{D}
a loose arrow

\Phi(\varphi):=H(\ell,\varphi):F(x)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}G(y)
;

(M2)

For each
n
-ary multicell
\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\varphi}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\mathbb{D}

and each loose arrow
\varphi_{i}
in the loose source of
\alpha
,
an
n
-ary multicell
\Phi_{i}(\alpha):=H(\mathsf{opcart}_{i},\alpha)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Fx_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Fx_{i-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Gx_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(x_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F(y_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G(y_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F({{{\boldsymbol{\varphi_{1}}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\varphi_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G({{{\boldsymbol{\varphi_{2}}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(b)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\psi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{i}(\alpha)}

where
\mathsf{opcart}_{i}
is the
n
-ary opcartesian multicell in
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})
with target
the unique non-identity loose arrow
\ell
and with loose source having
\ell

in the
i
th position.

The multicell assignment is required to satisfy functoriality in the following sense:

(M3)

If
\alpha=\text{id}_{\varphi}
is the unit multicell for a loose arrow
\varphi
,
then
\Phi_{1}(\text{id}_{\varphi})=\text{id}_{\Phi(\varphi)}
;

(M4)

If
\tfrac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\beta}
is the decomposition of a multicell in
\mathbb{D}
,
and if
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n})
where
\alpha_{i}
has arity
k_{i}
,
then for each
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq k_{i}
,

\Phi_{k_{1}+\cdots+k_{i-1}+j}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\beta}\right)=\frac{(F(\alpha_{1})\cdots F(\alpha_{i-1}),\Phi_{j}(\alpha_{i}),G(\alpha_{i+1})\cdots G(\alpha_{n}))}{\Phi_{i}(\beta)}

Note that in Definition 3.2 of
[
Par11
]
this data is unpacked further using the existence
of loose units and composites in
\mathbb{D}
.
When
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}
, this data coincides with the definition of a module in
\mathbb{E}
,
as unpacked in Definition
2.10
.
Note that there are many more action multicells in (M2) than the mere compatible left and right actions of
a loose arrow in a module out of a pseudo double category; in the intermediate case of
a VDC with decomposable multicells, we expect a module can have its actions indexed only by binary
multicells of
\mathbb{D},
but we do not pursue the details here.

4.2
Essentially unique decomposition of multicells in VDCs

As in the cases of multicategories and semicategories discussed above, we shall characterize
the exponentiable VDCs as those for which the exponential admits composites, which will reduce
to existence of essentially unique decompositions of multicells of various kinds. For motivation, we
next discuss a particular example of such decompositions in some detail.

Example 4.4

(A necessary kind of decomposition)
.

We give an example of one of the colimits, preservation of which by products will characterize
exponentiable VDC’s.

Let
\mathbb{J}
be the VDC freely generated by a unary, a ternary, and a binary multicell, juxtaposed as shown
below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

In other words,
\mathbb{J}
is the colimit of a diagram
D:J\to\mathsf{Vdc}
shown below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{2}}}

For any VDC
\mathbb{D},
we have the canonical comparison morphism

c:\mathbb{D}^{\prime}:=\colim_{j\in J}\mathbb{D}\times D(j)\to\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{J}
, whose invertibility
we aim to investigate.

To compute the colimit defining
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
, we first take the
levelwise colimit of the
\mathbb{D}\times D(j)
on objects, tight and loose arrows, and multicells of each arity.
This already produces the correct value of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
except on quaternary multicells, and indeed
c

is always an isomorphism away from the multicells. What is nontrivial is, thus, what happens
when we compose multicells of
\mathbb{D}
according to the single interesting multicell composite in
\mathbb{J}.

Consider, then, a composable triple
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\delta)
in
\mathbb{D}
like so:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

The set of quaternary multicells in the colimit
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
is a certain quotient of the set of such triples,
each of which provides a formal composite
\tfrac{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})}{\delta}.
The quotient
relation arises from the identity multicells of the loose arrows along the
middle row of
\mathbb{J}.
Specifically, we have a natural isomorphism of the quaternary multicells of
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}

with the quotient of the set
\Sigma
of composable triples
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\delta)
as above by the relation that,
given a situation of the form below, we must have

\left(\frac{\gamma_{1}}{\varepsilon_{1}},\frac{\gamma_{2}}{\varepsilon_{2}},\delta\right)\sim\left(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\frac{(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})}{\delta}\right):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

Let us rephrase. Recall that
\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
denotes the category of paths of loose arrows of length
n

in
\mathbb{D}.
Let
\mathbb{D}_{1,3}
denote the profunctor

\mathsf{MCell}_{1}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}(\varphi_{1},\psi_{1})\times_{\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}}\mathsf{MCell}_{3}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}(\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3},\varphi_{4};\psi_{2}):\mathsf{fc}_{4}(\mathbb{D}_{1})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\mathsf{fc}_{2}(\mathbb{D}_{1})

Then the argument above demonstrates the coend formula

\mathsf{MCell}_{4}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\varphi};\chi)=\int^{\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathsf{fc}_{2}(\mathbb{D}_{1})}\mathbb{D}_{1,3}(\boldsymbol{\varphi};\boldsymbol{\psi})\times\mathsf{MCell}_{2}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\psi};\chi).

In short, the invertibility of
c
in this case reduces to checking the
coend formula just above for
\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{J}.

Now,
\mathbb{J}
has exactly one quaternary multicell
\rho:=\frac{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}{\beta},
so

\mathsf{MCell}_{4}(\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{J})\cong\mathsf{MCell}_{4}(\mathbb{D}).
By all means we have a map

\pi:\int^{\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathsf{fc}_{2}(\mathbb{D}_{1})}\mathbb{D}_{1,3}(\boldsymbol{\varphi};\boldsymbol{\psi})\times\mathsf{MCell}_{2}\mathbb{D}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\psi};\chi)\to\mathsf{MCell}_{4}(\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{J})\cong\mathsf{MCell}_{4}(\mathbb{D})

given as for
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
by a composition like
\tfrac{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})}{\delta}.

The question is when this map
\pi
is an isomorphism. The surjectivity of
\pi
amounts to the condition
that every quaternary multicell in
\mathbb{D}
may be decomposed as a composite
\frac{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})}{\delta}
as above.
Injectivity says that such a decomposition is essentially unique, up to “interchanging”
unary multicells
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}
as above.

Since the invertibility of
\pi
is necessary for exponentiability of
\mathbb{D},
this already provides two
concrete ways in which
\mathbb{D}
can fail to be exponentiable. For instance, if
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{4}

is
freely
generated by a quaternary multicell
\mu
, then
\mathbb{D}
has no binary or ternary multicells at all,
so
\mu
has no decomposition, making
\pi
non-surjective. For a non-injective example, we could take
a pushout
\mathbb{J}+_{\partial\mathbb{J}}\mathbb{J}
, modulo the relation identifying the two quaternary multicells.

A Sketch For VDCs

We now aim toward applying Corollary
3.3
to
fully classify the exponentiable
objects in
\mathsf{Vdc}.
This category can be sketched via a sketch containing objects for the
objects, tight arrows, loose arrows, and unary multicells, strings of tight arrows and unary multicells
of lengths up to 3, multicells of each arity, as well as for gluings
of multicells of heights
2
and
3.
Let us be more precise.

Definition 4.5

(Tree)
.

By a
tree
, we shall mean a rooted finite planar tree in the sense of Kock (
[
Koc11
]
,)
of uniform height.

In particular, there is a unique root edge. We think of the root as the output, which determines an interpretation
of words including “input”, “output”, “source”, “target”, “parent”, and “child” for edges and vertices.
The root edge uniquely has no output vertex, while leaf edges are characterized as having no input vertex.
The input edges of a vertex are equipped with a total order, and all leaf edges are the same distance from the root.
∎

In particular, the smallest tree,
\mid
, is the one with a single edge and no vertices.

Every height-
n
tree
T
determines a VDC
\mathbb{J}(T)
given by
gluing
n
tight layers of multicells, one multicell per node, with the arity of the multicell
corresponding to each node given by the node’s number of children, and the multicells corresponding
to adjacent sibling nodes glued along their tight edges, while parents’ multicells are glued to their children
along a loose edge. The resulting gluing
has a single maximal multicell of arity the total number of leaves in the tree. An
example with
n=3
and
10,8,
and
4
edges in the respective layers
is depicted below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Precisely:

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\mathsf{PTEmb}
be the category of trees and embeddings preserving arities
and planar orderings (i.e.
\mathsf{TEmb}/\mathrm{List}
in the notation of
[
Koc11
]
.)

If
T
is a tree of height
n,
we define a diagram
C(T):J(T)\to\mathsf{Vdc}

as follows:

•

For each edge
e
of
T,

J(T)
has an object
e
for which
C(T)(e)=\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}

is the walking loose arrow.

•

For each node
v
of
T
with
k(v)
children (note that
k(v)
may be
0
),
J(T)
has an object
v

for which
C(T)(v)=\mathsf{Sq}_{k(v)}
is the walking
k
-ary multicell.

•

If an edge
e
has a parent vertex
s(e),
then
J(T)
contains a morphism

e\to s(e)
sent by
C(T)
to the inclusion
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\to\mathsf{Sq}_{k(s(e))}
of the loose target,
while if
e
is itself the
i
th parent of the vertex
t(e)
,
then
J(T)
contains a morphism
e\to t(e)
sent by
C(T)
to the inclusion

\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\to\mathsf{Sq}_{k(t(e))}
of the
i
th loose source.

•

The total order on each node’s input edges induces a total order on
all the nodes of each height. If a node
v
is the successor of
w
in this order,
then
J(T)
contains a span
w\leftarrow wv\to v
sent by
C(T)
to the
span
\mathsf{Sq}_{k(w)}\leftarrow\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}\to\mathsf{Sq}_{k(v)}
including
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}
as the
tight target at
w
and the tight source at
v.

Then we define the VDC
\mathbb{J}(T):=\colim C(T)
in
\mathsf{Vdc}.

Furthermore,
C(T)
is evidently natural in
T,
so that
\mathbb{J}
becomes a functor
\mathsf{PTEmb}\to\mathsf{Vdc}.

∎

Note in particular that for
\mid
the tree with no nodes, then
\mathbb{J}(\mid)=\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}.

These virtual double categories include almost all those needed to define our desired sketch
for
\mathsf{Vdc}.

Definition 4.7
.

We define the
virtual cubical site

\square_{v\partial}
(in analogy with the cubical site in
[
GM03
]
) as the full subcategory of
\mathsf{Vdc}

spanned by the walking object
\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}
, the walking tight arrow
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}
, the pushouts

\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}[n]:=\underbrace{\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}\cup_{\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}}\cdots\cup_{\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}}\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}}_{n}
, and
\mathbb{J}(T)

for each tree
T.

∎

Let
\mathsf{inrt}\subseteq\square_{v\partial}
denote the wide subcategory generated by
morphisms that in the image of
\mathbb{J}
together with morphisms among the
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}[n]

which are inert in the sense of the simplicial category, that is,
sending generating arrows to generating arrows. Thus all the inert morphisms are characterized
by refusing to map a generating cell to a nontrivial composite.

If
\mathsf{el}\subseteq\mathsf{inrt}
is the full subcategory spanned by
\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}
,
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
,
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}
, and

\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}
, then every object of the virtual cubical site is canonically glued from elements of

\mathsf{el},
by definition. More explicitly, for each

\mathbb{D}\in\square_{v\partial}
we have a natural colimit cocone
C(\mathbb{D})^{\rhd}
under the diagram
of all inert maps from elementaries to
\mathbb{D}
, i.e. the diagram

C(\mathbb{D}):\mathsf{el}\downarrow\mathbb{D}\to\mathsf{el}\hookrightarrow\square_{v\partial}

with colimit
\mathbb{D}
. This recovers the defining diagram
C(T)=C(\mathbb{J}(T))
for trees
T
while also covering
the cases of the
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}[n].

(Here
\mathsf{el}\downarrow\mathbb{D}
is the comma category for the inclusions
\mathsf{el}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{inrt}\hookleftarrow\{\mathbb{D}\}
.)

Definition 4.8

(Sketch for VDCs)
.

The sketch
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{S})
for VDCs has

\mathsf{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\subseteq\square_{v\partial}
as the full subcategory
spanned by the following objects:

1.

O:=\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b},\downarrow:=\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight},\triangle:=\downarrow\cup_{O}\downarrow
, and

, for the tight category;

2.

A_{T}:=\mathbb{J}(T)
for every tree
T
of height
\leq 3.
7
7

7

If
T
is a tree of height
1
it is uniquely determined by its single branching factor
n,
in which case

A_{n}:=A_{T}
will represent the
n
-ary multicells of a VDC.

The sketched cones in
\mathsf{S}
are precisely the opposites of the cocones

C(\mathbb{D})^{\rhd}
in
\mathsf{C}^{op}
for each
\mathbb{D}\in\mathsf{C}^{op}\backslash\mathsf{el}
.
Informally, these are the cones making the objects and tight arrows, as well as the loose
arrows and unary multicells, form a category,
along with those forcing a model to admit a composition of layers of multicells of height 2
and those forcing that composition to be associative.

Now consider exponentiability. Taking products with any virtual double
category preserves the colimits for paths of tight arrows, since every category is exponentiable.
Thus the exponentiable virtual double categories will be precisely those that preserve all the colimits
freely forming the pasting virtual double categories
\mathbb{J}(T)
for every tree
T

of height
2
or
3
. As illustrated in Example
4.4
, preservation
of such colimits is equivalent to existence of essentially unique decompositions of multicells,
as we now turn to show.

4.3
Reduction of height of decompositions

In order to phrase our characterizations of exponentiable VDCs we will need some notation and terminology
for decompositions of multicells in VDCs. Recall from Section
2
we have the profunctor

\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}):\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})^{op}\times\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}
of multicells for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
,
with associated category obtained by the Grothendieck construction.
Viewing this profunctor as a category via the collage, we can define the category of height
n
pasting diagrams
in
\mathbb{D}
as the iterated pullback

\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D}):=\mathsf{fc}^{n-1}(\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}))\times_{\mathsf{fc}^{n-1}(\mathbb{D}_{1})}\cdots\times_{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})

We will notate a pasting diagram, viewed as an object in
\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
, by

{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha_{n}}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{8.6314pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{8.6314pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\alpha_{1}\end{array}}
, and for each
0\leq i\leq n
we refer to the
sequence of sequences of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\varphi_{i,1}}\cdots\boldsymbol{\varphi_{i,m}}
which are the
sources of the
\boldsymbol{\alpha_{i}}
as the
i
th
interface
of the pasting diagram.

The category
\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
is the generalization of the category
\mathsf{Fun}([n],\mathsf{C})
of
length
n
-sequences of composable arrows in a category
\mathsf{C}
to the case of VDCs.
Note that for each
1\leq i\leq n-1
, we have a natural functor

c_{i}:\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{Paste}_{n-1}(\mathbb{D})
given by composing
i
th and
(i+1)
st layers of the
pasting diagram.
We will write
c:\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\to\mathsf{Paste}_{1}(\mathbb{D})=\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})
for the functor given by composing the whole pasting diagram. For a tree
T
of height
n
, indexing a shape of pasting diagrams in
\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
, we will write
c_{i}(T)
for the height
(n-1)
tree obtained by composing along the
i
th layer.

For an
m
-ary multicell
\alpha
in
\mathbb{D}
, we write

\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D};\alpha):=c^{-1}(\{\alpha\})\subseteq\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
for the
(non-full) subcategory of height-
n
pasting diagrams which compose to
\alpha
, with morphisms those which are
identities on the loose source and target of
\alpha
. We will say that
\alpha

admits essentially unique decompositions
if for each integer
n\geq 2
, the set of connected
components
\pi_{0}\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
is a singleton for each height
n
tree
T
with
m

leaves, corresponding to a pasting diagram of shape
T
lifting
\alpha
.
Here two decompositions of
\alpha
in
\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
will be said to be

equivalent up to associativity
if they lie in the same connected component.

Our first characterization of exponentiable VDCs will be those admitting
essentially unique decompositions for all multicells.

Definition 4.9

(VDCs with Decomposable multicells)
.

We will say a VDC
\mathbb{D}
has
decomposable multicells
if every multicell
\alpha
in
\mathbb{D}

admits essentially unique decompositions.
∎

In practice showing that a VDC has decomposable multicells directly from the definition is unreasonable.
Thus, in the process of characterizing exponentiability, we will aim to discover apparently much weaker
conditions which are in fact sufficient for existence and essential uniqueness of decompositions.
Let’s first show we can reduce from considering decompositions into arbitrarily many layers to the case of
decompositions into two layers. Explication
4.12
may help fix
the basic idea in the reader’s mind before reading the general proof of the next two
results.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a VDC whose multicells admit essentially unique decompositions into
2
layers,
let
n\geq 2
be an integer, and let
\alpha
be a multicell in
\mathbb{D}
.
If two
(n+1)
-layer decompositions of
\alpha
over the same tree map to the same connected component
under the functor
c_{1}:\mathsf{Paste}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)\to\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
,
then they are in the same connected component in
\mathsf{Paste}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
.

Proof.

Let
\beta={\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\beta_{n+1}}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{17.61499pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{17.61499pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{1}\end{array}}
and

\gamma={\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\gamma_{n+1}}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{17.39633pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{17.39633pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\gamma_{1}\end{array}}
be
(n+1)
-layer decompositions
of
\alpha
with the same arities that map to the same connected component under
c_{1}
.
We wish to show that the zig-zag of morphisms witnessing that
c_{1}(\beta)
and
c_{1}(\gamma)
are in the same
connected component lifts to a possibly differently shaped zig-zag of morphisms witnessing
that
\beta
and
\gamma
are in the same connected component.

Note that we can consider a zig-zag such that
each morphism consists of a sequence of identity multicells on all interfaces but one.
Then all morphisms which are non-trivial on an interface other than the
1
st interface lift to

\mathsf{Paste}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
. Thus, it suffices to consider the case where the morphisms in the
zig-zag consist of identity multicells on all interfaces but the first interface. In particular, this
observation allows us to reduce to the case where
n=2
.

Proceeding inductively on the length of the chain
of zig-zags, we can further reduce to the case where the zig-zag consists of a single morphism.
Without loss of generality suppose the morphism is from
c_{1}(\beta)
to
c_{1}(\gamma)
. Then we have the map
of pasting diagrams of multicells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{3,k_{3,m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{3,k_{3,m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{3,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{3,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,k_{2,m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{2,k_{2,m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{2,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1,m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1,m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{{\beta_{3}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{{\gamma_{3}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{{\sigma}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\beta_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\gamma_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma_{1}}}
id

where the bottom rectangular box only commutes after composing the multicells. But then if
\boldsymbol{\rho}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{{\sigma}}}{\boldsymbol{\gamma_{2}}}
, it follows that
\frac{\boldsymbol{\beta_{2}}}{\beta_{1}}
and
\frac{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\gamma_{1}}
are 2-layer decompositions of the same multicell
\chi
in
\mathbb{D}
, and hence by assumption lie in the same connected component of
\mathsf{Paste}_{2}(\mathbb{D};\chi)
. Once again, we can reduce to the case where the decompositions being in the same connected component is witnessed by a single morphism from one to the other. If we have such a morphism
\boldsymbol{\sigma^{\prime}}
from
\frac{\boldsymbol{\beta_{2}}}{\beta_{1}}
to
\frac{\boldsymbol{\rho}}{\gamma_{1}}
, then our previous map of pasting diagrams lifts to an actual map in
\mathsf{Paste}_{3}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{3,k_{3,m}}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{3,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{3,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,k_{2,m}}}}
