In either case, we see that the decompositions are in the same connected component, completing the proof.
∎

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a VDC. Then
\mathbb{D}
has decomposable multicells if and only if all multicells in

\mathbb{D}
admit essentially unique decompositions into
2
layers.

Proof.

We will prove that a multicell
\alpha
, of arity
m
, admits essentially unique decompositions into
n
-layers for each
n\geq 2
by induction on
n
, where the base case
n=2
is by assumption. Thus, suppose
\alpha
admits essentially unique decompositions into
n\geq 2
layers, and we aim to show it also admits essentially unique decompositions into
(n+1)
-layers. First, to show existence of such decompositions, let
T
be a tree of height
(n+1)
with
m
leaves. Then
c_{1}(T)
is a height
n
tree with
|\alpha|
leaves, so by the inductive hypothesis we have an essentially unique decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n+1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n+1,m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,k_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{{\varphi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\lambda_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\lambda_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\beta_{n+1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{2}}}

By the base case of
n=2
we have an essentially unique decomposition of
\beta_{2}=\frac{\boldsymbol{\alpha_{2}}}{\alpha_{1}}
using the arities from the root and first layer of the tree
T
, which gives the decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n+1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n+1,m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n,k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2,k_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1,k_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0,1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{{\varphi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\lambda_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\lambda_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\beta_{n+1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\alpha_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}

Now, to prove essential uniqueness suppose we had another decomposition of shape
T
,
{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\gamma_{n+1}}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{17.39633pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{17.39633pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\gamma_{1}\end{array}}
.
Composing the bottom two layers
\delta_{1}=\frac{\boldsymbol{\gamma_{2}}}{\gamma_{1}}
, we obtain an
n
-layer decomposition of
\alpha
of shape
c_{1}(T)
, so by the inductive hypothesis it lies in the same connected component as the decomposition
{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\beta_{n+1}}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{17.61499pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{17.61499pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{2}\end{array}}
. Then by Lemma
4.10
these two decompositions lie in the same connected component of
\mathsf{Paste}_{n+1}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
, completing the proof.
∎

Explication 4.12

(Binary-to-Ternary Case)
.

To explicate the central idea of the above lemma-corollary pair, let’s consider the inductive step going from 2-layer decompositions to 3-layer decompositions in the case of a pair of binary decompositions. Suppose we had a quaternary multicell that we can essentially uniquely decompose in 2-layers, and we wanted to decompose into three layers using binary multicells as in the pasting diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

(2)

To construct such a decomposition we first construct a 2-layer decomposition of the shape obtained by composing the bottom two layers in our desired decomposition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Then, to obtain a 3-layer decomposition we use the fact that we can decompose the bottom trinary multicell in terms of a 2-layer decomposition with a binary multicell, giving the originally desired decomposition in Eq.
2
. Lemma
4.10
then guarantees essential uniqueness of this 3-layer decomposition since we have essential uniqueness on the level of 2-layers.

The 2-layer formulation of having decomposable multicells has an elegant description in terms of coends.
Namely, a VDC
\mathbb{D}
has decomposable multicells if and only if the natural transformation

\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}))\odot\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{comp}}}{{\Rightarrow}}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\mu_{\mathsf{fc}},1):\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1}))^{op}\times\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}

is an isomorphism, where
\mu_{\mathsf{fc}}:\mathsf{fc}^{2}\to\mathsf{fc}

is the multiplication of the free category monad. Thus the codomain profunctor is that of multicells with domain
a list of lists of loose arrows, rather than a flat list thereof.

In terms of coends, this says that the natural composition map

\int^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})}\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}))(-,\boldsymbol{\varphi})\times\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\boldsymbol{\varphi},-)\to\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\mu_{\mathsf{fc}}(-),-)

(3)

is an isomorphism. This condition is also the cellular analogue of the classical Conduché condition for exponentiable functors between categories. We will explore this point more in Section
A
.

4.4
Exponentiability of VDCs is equivalent to decomposability of multicells

We can now show that having decomposable multicells is equivalent to being exponentiable by showing
that this is precisely the condition induced by requiring that the product
\mathbb{D}\times-

preserves the sketched cones in our sketch for virtual double categories.

Remark 4.13

(Colimits for Trees)
.

Before stating and proving the main theorem, it will be helpful to understand the colimits of the diagrams

\mathsf{el}\downarrow\mathbb{J}(T)\to\mathsf{Vdc}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{D}\times-}\mathsf{Vdc}

for
T
a tree.
We can express
\colim_{i:\mathbb{c}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{J}(T)}(\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{c})
as the colimit of a diagram in
\mathsf{Vdc}
obtained from the tree
T
as follows:

1.

Replace each edge in
T
by a copy of
\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
.

2.

Replace each node in
T
with
r
children by
\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{r}
.

3.

Glue the VDC’s corresponding to an edge appropriately into those corresponding to its
parent and child nodes, if they exist.

4.

Glue the VDC’s corresponding to adjacent nodes in
T
appropriately.

Objects and loose arrows in the colimit are then precisely pairs of such in
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{J}(T)
,
with no relations. Similarly, since the tight category functor
(-)_{0}:\mathsf{Vdc}\to\mathsf{Cat}
preserves colimits
and finite products, the tight arrows in the colimit are precisely pairs of tight arrows in

\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{J}(T)
, with no new relations.

Finally, a multicell in the colimit corresponds to an equivalence class of tuples of a subtree
T^{\prime}\subseteq T

of
T
, together with for each node in
T^{\prime}
a multicell in
\mathbb{D}
of arity equal to that of the
node, such that the multicells have compatible boundaries.
The equivalence relation on these tuples comes from associativity of composition along with the spans

\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{r}\xleftarrow{\mathbb{D}\times s_{i}}\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{D}\times t}\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{k_{i}}

appearing in the diagram. Namely, if
T^{\prime}
is a height
n
tree, then the data of the multicells in

\mathbb{D}
gives a pasting diagram of height
n
(i.e. an object in
\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D})
).
Then the spans above imply that we can move sequences of unary multicells between rows without changing the
equivalence class of the resulting tuple. For example, if
T^{\prime}
was a subtree of height
2
whose root had

r
children and whose
i
th node in the second layer had
k_{i}
children, then the two pasting diagrams
below would be identified in the colimit:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{r}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{r}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\chi_{1}}^{\prime}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\chi_{2}}^{\prime}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\chi_{r}}^{\prime}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\chi_{1}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\chi_{2}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\chi_{r}}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\left(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\sigma_{1}},k_{1}\right)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\sigma_{2}},k_{2}\right)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\left(\frac{\alpha_{r}}{\sigma_{r}},k_{r}\right)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\alpha_{1},k_{1})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\alpha_{2},k_{2})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\alpha_{r},k_{r})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\beta^{\prime},r)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\left(\frac{\sigma_{1}\cdots\sigma_{r}}{\beta},r\right)}}}

Theorem 4.14

(Exponentiable VDCs Have Decomposable Multicells)
.

A VDC
\mathbb{D}
is exponentiable if and only if it has decomposable multicells.

Proof.

First, fix a tree
T
, and consider the natural map

c:\colim_{i:\mathbb{c}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{J}(T)}(\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{c})\to\mathbb{D}\times\mathbb{J}(T)

(4)

induced by composition in
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{J}(T)
.
For a subtree
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
of height
n
with
m
leaves, let
\square(T^{\prime})
denote the associated
glued multicell in
\mathbb{J}(T)
, and fix an
m
-ary multicell
\alpha\in\mathbb{D}
.
Then from the description of the above colimit in Remark
4.13
, the map in
Eq.
4
having
(\alpha,\square(T^{\prime}))
in its image is equivalent to
\alpha
admitting
a decomposition into a pasting diagram with arities specified by the subtree
T^{\prime}
.
Further, that
c
in Eq.
4
should have at most one multicell mapped to
(\alpha,\square(T^{\prime}))
is
equivalent to
\alpha
either not admitting a decomposition of the appropriate shape, or any two
decompositions being related by passing sequences of unary multicells between layers. But this is precisely
the relation that says that two pasting diagrams lying over the same multicell
\alpha
in

\mathbb{D}
are in the same connected component of
\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
.

Next, recall that by Corollary
3.3
and the definition of the sketch for
VDCs (Definition
4.8
), to show
\mathbb{D}
is exponentiable it is
equivalent to show that
\mathbb{D}\times-
preserves the gluings
A_{T}
where
T
is a tree of height
2
or
3
.
Thus,
\mathbb{D}
is exponentiable if and only if Eq.
4
is an isomorphism for all height
2
and

3
trees
T
. Additionally, from the above argument, this is equivalent to
\mathbb{D}
having essentially unique
decompositions into
2
and
3
layers for its multicells. But by Corollary
4.11

\mathbb{D}
has
decomposable multicells if and only if it has essentially unique decompositions into
2
layers, so the claim
follows.
∎

The description of exponentiable VDCs in Theorem
4.14
provides a usable
technique for testing whether a VDC is exponentiable, which we can immediately apply
to provide a minimal example of a VDC that is not exponentiable, namely the walking loose arrow
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
.

Example 4.15

(Counter-example to Exponentiability)
.

The walking loose arrow
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
is not an exponentiable VDC because there are no nullary or binary multicells to decompose the unary multicell
\text{id}_{\ell}
.

We refer the reader to Section
6
for further examples and non-examples of exponentiable VDCs.

5
Other Characterizations of Exponentiability

In this section, we provide three alternative characterizations of exponentiable virtual double
categories. The first is in terms of
pro-representability
.

5.1
Exponentiability of VDCs is equivalent to pro-representability

Our next goal is to obtain a further reduction of the characterization of
exponentiability via decomposable multicells. The choice of decompositions included in this reduction are partially motivated by
Pisani’s characterization of exponentiable multicategories in terms of pro-monoidal
multicategories
[
Pis14
]
. We first pick out a class of trees
(in the sense of Definition
4.5
) which will detect decomposability
of multicells into arbitrary shapes.

Definition 5.1

(Binary-Nullary Tree)
.

A
binary-nullary tree

T
is a tree of height at least
2

such that, in every layer of nodes
except the last, at most one node is non-unary, with
arity at most
2.

If the only non-unary node has arity
2
(resp. arity
0
) in each layer except the last,
then we say that
T
is a
binary-unary
(resp.
nullary-unary
) tree.

Binary-nullary trees thus look like the examples below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}

The left-hand example is, in particular, binary-unary, while the middle example is nullary-unary.

Definition 5.2

(Pro-Representable VDC)
.

A VDC
\mathbb{D}
is said to be
pro-representable
if the following conditions hold:

(P1)

For every binary-nullary tree
T
of height
2,

\mathbb{D}
admits essentially unique decompositions
of shape
T.

(P3)

For every height
2
tree
T^{\prime}
there is some binary-nullary tree
T
with
T^{\prime}
as some partial
composite,
such that every decomposition of a
\mathbb{D}
-cell of shape
T^{\prime}
arises from a decomposition of shape
T

up to equivalence.

∎

Note that
T^{\prime}
in (P3) may be of any height, and in general will have to be of height greater than
2.

Note also that by definition all VDCs with decomposable multicells are pro-representable.
We will now show that the converse is also true, starting with existence of decompositions.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
satisfy (P1) above. Then
\mathbb{D}
admits decompositions
of shape
T
for every tree
T
of height
2.

Here a decomposition of a multicell
\alpha
in
\mathbb{D}
of shape
T
is a morphism

\mathbb{J}(T)\to\mathbb{D}
sending the maximal composite cell of
\mathbb{J}(T)
to
\alpha.

Proof.

Let
\alpha
be an
n
-ary multicell in
\mathbb{D}
, and let
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{m})

be a partition of
n
, so that
n=\sum k_{i}.

Note that such a partition uniquely determines a tree of height
2,
according to whose shape we must
show
\alpha
admits a decomposition.

First, we consider the case where
k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\geq 1
are all positive integers.
To prove this case we proceed by induction on
d=\max\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m}\}
.
If
d=1
, then we have the decomposition
\frac{\text{id}_{\varphi_{1}}\cdots\text{id}_{\varphi_{n}}}{\alpha}

where
\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}
is the loose source of
\alpha
.
Now, inductively suppose
d\geq 2

and the claim holds for
d-1
. Let
1\leq i_{1}<...<i_{r}\leq m
be the indices such that
k_{i_{t}}=d
.
Replacing
k_{i_{t}}
by
((d-1),1)
in the partition, we obtain a new partition
n=\ell_{1}+\cdots+\ell_{m+r}

where
\max\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{m+r}\}=d-1
. Thus, by the inductive hypothesis,
\alpha
admits a
decomposition
\frac{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{m+r}}{\beta}
where
\beta_{i}
has arity
\ell_{i}
.

Now, since
\mathbb{D}
satisfies (P1), we can decompose
\beta
as

\frac{\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{m+r-1}}{\gamma},
where
\gamma_{i_{1}}
is a binary multicell
and all other
\gamma_{j}
are unary. Pasting these two decompositions, we obtain a new decomposition

\frac{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m+r-1}}{\gamma}
where

\delta_{i_{1}}=\frac{(\beta_{i_{1}},\beta_{i_{1}+1})}{\gamma_{i_{1}}}
has arity
(d-1)+1=d
.
By induction on
r,
we obtain a decomposition of
\alpha
of the desired shape.

Now, let’s consider the case where the partition
n=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{m}
may contain zeros. Let
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}\leq m
denote the indices such that
k_{i_{t}}=0
. Then we proceed inductively on
r
. If
r=0
, the claim follows from the first case. Now, suppose
r\geq 1
such that the claim holds for
r-1
. Then the partition
n=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{i_{r}-1}+k_{i_{r}+1}+\cdots+k_{m}
contains
r-1
zeros. Thus, by the inductive hypothesis
\alpha
admits a decomposition
\frac{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{i_{r}-1},\beta_{i_{r}+1}\cdots\beta_{m}}{\beta}
where
\beta_{j}
has arity
k_{j}
. Since
\mathbb{D}
satisfies (P1), we can decompose
\beta
as
\frac{\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{m}}{\gamma}
where
\gamma_{i_{r}}
is nullary and the remaining
\gamma_{j}
are unary. Composing these factorizations gives a decomposition
\frac{\delta_{1}\cdots\delta_{m}}{\gamma}
of the desired shape, proving the inductive step, and the claim.
∎

(Inserting Binary and Nullary Decompositions)
.

We will explicate the inductive step in Lemma
5.3
in the case of going from a binary decomopsition to a trinary decomposition, and then inserting a nullary multicell. Thus, for a quaternary multicell consider the following binary-nullary decomposition:

If we want instead to insert a nullary multicell, we can use (P1) on the bottom layer of the decomposition, and then compose the top to layers of the resulting 3-layer decomposition to obtain the desired 2-layer decomposition below right:

Property (P1) in the definition of pro-representability is also
sufficient for showing that multicells can be essentially uniquely
decomposed into the shape of arbitrary binary-nullary trees.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
satisfy (P1) above. Then
\mathbb{D}
admits
essentiallly unique decompositions of shape
T
for every binary-nullary tree
T
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the height,
N
, of the binary-nullary tree
T
.
If
N=2
, then the claim is precisely condition (P1), which holds by assumption.
Now, suppose
N\geq 3
, and that the claim holds for binary-nullary trees of height
N-1
.
Composing the bottom two layers of such an
N
-layer pasting diagram

{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\alpha_{N}}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{10.38948pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{10.38948pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\alpha_{1}\end{array}}

produces an
(N-1)
-layer pasting diagram of binary-nullary shape. We can thus decrease
the height of a tree while staying in the binary-nullary space, which is the main point.
Indeed, by the inductive hypothesis the resulting pasting diagrams are essentially unique,
so any other
N
-layer pasting diagram
{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\beta_{N}}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{9.77606pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{9.77606pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{1}\end{array}}

for
\alpha
of the same shape gets mapped to the same connected component of

\mathsf{Paste}_{N-1}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)
.
Then by Lemma
4.10
this implies that the original pasting diagrams are equivalent, as desired.
Thus, by induction
\mathbb{D}
admits essentially unique decompositions of shape
T
for any binary-nullary tree
T
.
∎

To show that pro-representable VDCs have decomposable multicells, it remains to show essential uniqueness of the decompositions from Lemma
5.3
.

Corollary 5.6
.

If
\mathbb{D}
is a pro-representable VDC, then
\mathbb{D}
has decomposable multicells, and thus is exponentiable.

Proof.

Fix an
n
-ary multicell
\alpha
in
\mathbb{D}
.
By Lemma
5.3
,
\alpha
admits decompositions of shape
T

for any tree
T
of height
2
.
It remains to show that any two such decompositions are equivalent.

By property (P3), for any height
2
tree
T
, there exists an
N
-layered binary-nullary tree
T^{\prime}

that partially composes to
T
such that every decomposition of
\alpha
of shape
T
is equivalent
to a decomposition which is the partial composite of a decomposition of shape
T^{\prime}
.
However, by Lemma
5.5
, all decompositions of
\alpha
of shape
T^{\prime}

are equivalent.
Thus, since equivalent decompositions of shape
T^{\prime}
compose to equivalent decompositions of shape
T
,
and since every decomposition of shape
T
is equivalent to one coming from a decomposition of shape
T^{\prime}
,
it follows that all decompositions of shape
T
are equivalent.
By Corollary
4.11
it follows that
\alpha
admits essentially unique decompositions, as desired.
∎

Remark 5.7

(Insufficiency of (P1))
.

(P1) alone is not sufficient for exponentiability.
The need for property (P3) comes from the fact that we could have a 2-layer decomposition of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

which is not equivalent to a 2-layer decomposition admitting a binary-nullary decomposition, since we may not be able to decompose the binary and nullary multicell in the second layer simultaneously to obtain decompositions of the form bottom left or bottom right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

due to the tight sources and targets that need to coincide in the decompositions.

However, in the case where
\mathbb{D}
has trivial underlying tight category, and hence corresponds to a multicategory, condition (P1) does suffice to guarantee exponentiability, since there are no constraints
arising from tight arrows in that case.

5.2
Exponentiability of VDCs is equivalent to malleability

In addition to the classes of VDCs discussed so far, it is conjectured by Arkor that the exponentiable VDCs
are the
malleable
VDCs, defined in
[
Ark25
]
.
Once the definition is expanded, we will see that malleable VDCs are indeed precisely those having
decomposable multicells. Before stating the definition we need to recall the construction of the loose
Kleisli VDC for a monad on a VDC
[
CS10
, Definition 4.1]
. Here a monad on a VDC is a monad
in the 2-category
\mathbf{Vdc}
.

Definition 5.8

(Kleisli VDCs)
.

The loose Kleisli VDC associated to a VDC
\mathbb{D}
equipped with a monad
T:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
is denoted
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D},T)
and defined as follows:

1.

Its objects and tight arrows are as in
\mathbb{D}
;

2.

A loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
corresponds to a loose arrow
U(\varphi):x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}Ty
in
\mathbb{D}
;

3.

A nullary multicell, as below left, is equivalent to a multicell in
\mathbb{D}
as below right

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tx}

4.

An
n
-ary multicell,
n\geq 1
, as below left, is equivalent to an
n
-ary multicell in
\mathbb{D}
as below right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Tx_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T^{n}x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Tx_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tz}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{U(\varphi_{1})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{T(U\varphi_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{T^{n-1}(U\varphi_{n})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mu\circ\cdots\circ T^{n-2}\mu}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{U(\psi)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

∎

We write
\mathbb{K}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{D},T):=\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D},T))
for the VDC of
T
-monoids in
\mathbb{D}
.
Cruttwell and Shulman have a very general framework in
[
CS10
]
,
but for this paper we will only need to consider the
T
-monoids as in
[
Bur71
,
Lei04
]
,
where
T=\mathsf{fc}
is the free category monad on the category of graphs.
Such
T
-monoids give one way to define virtual double categories.
Namely, a VDC
\mathbb{D}
is equivalent to the associated
\mathsf{fc}
-monoid

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma_{n}\mathsf{Sq}_{n}\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{fc}(G_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{D}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Loose}(\mathbb{D})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{D})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces:=}

in
\mathsf{Grph}
, the category of graphs. Thus we have the monoid multiplication

m:\mathsf{fc}(G_{1})\times_{\mathsf{fc}(G_{0})}G_{1}\to G_{1}
which pastes height-2 arrangements of multicells
as well as the monoid unit
e:G_{0}\to G_{1},
which picks out identity tight arrows
and unary multicells.

Using the normalized embedding
\mathbb{K}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{D},T)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{K}\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{D}),\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(T))

constructred in
[
CS10
, Theorem 8.7]
, we can further view the VDC
\mathbb{D}
as a
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathsf{fc})
-monoid in

\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathsf{Grph})
. This
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathsf{fc})
-monoid is carried by by a profunctor internal to graphs
whose codomain is the category internal to graphs carried by the span

M
itself carrying the desired
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathsf{fc})
-monoid
is carried by the span of graphs
\mathsf{fc}(G_{0})\leftarrow G_{1}\rightarrow G_{0}
by
which we already gave a simpler reconstitution of
\mathbb{D},
and the multiplication of the monoid
is a cell
M,M\Rightarrow M,
in the loose Kleisli VDC of
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathsf{fc})
on
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathsf{Grph})
.
We can now recall Arkor’s definition of malleable VDCs.

Definition 5.9

(Malleable VDC (
[
Ark25
]
))
.

A VDC
\mathbb{D}
is said to be
malleable
if when viewed as a
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathsf{fc})
-monoid in

\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathsf{Grph})
, its multiplication multicell is cartesian in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathsf{Grph})
.
∎

To work with this definition we can use the fact that
\mathsf{Grph}=\mathsf{Set}^{\bullet\rightrightarrows\bullet}

along with the isomorphisms in Proposition
6.9
and
Example 7.3 of
[
Ark25
]
,
which imply that
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}(\mathsf{Grph})\cong\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}_{n}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}_{u}(\bullet\rightrightarrows\bullet),\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof})
.
Then the category internal to graphs associated to
\mathbb{D}
corresponds to the graph of categories

(\mathbb{D}_{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{D}_{0})
, while the span carrying the
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathsf{fc})
-monoid structure
corresponds to the graph of profunctors
\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})\rightrightarrows\mathbb{D}_{0}(-,-)
.

Then
\mathbb{D}
being malleable means that the natural transformation

\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathsf{fc})(\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}))\odot\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})\Rightarrow\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\mu_{\mathsf{fc}},1)

induced by composition is an isomorphism, which is precisely Eq.
3
,
so by Corollary
4.11
we have the following:

Proposition 5.10

(Malleable VDCs have Decomposable Multicells)
.

A VDC
\mathbb{D}
is malleable if and only if it has decomposable multicells.

We can now state the main theorem of the paper:

Theorem 5.11

(Characterization of Exponentiable VDCs)
.

For a VDC
\mathbb{D}
, the following are equivalent:

1.

\mathbb{D}
is exponentiable;

2.

\mathbb{D}
is pro-representable;

3.

\mathbb{D}
has decomposable multicells;

4.

\mathbb{D}
is malleable;

5.

The exponential
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathbb{D}}
exists in the category of large VDCs.

So far we’ve shown the equivalences of conditions (1) through (4).
We now turn to the last equivalent condition, involving the existence of the exponential
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathbb{D}}.
8
8

8

This condition can be understood to be intimately related to the
characterization of pro-monoidal multicategories in terms of bi-cocontinuous monoidal copresheaf categories (c.f.
[
Day70
]
).
We will explicate this relation once we’ve established conditions for the representability of exponentials in
Section
7
.

5.3
Exponentiability reduced to the case of
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}

In personal communication, Arkor has indicated that an upcoming draft of
[
Ark25
]

will contain a proof that
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
detects exponentiability for VDCs along very similar lines to the argument
below.

Before proving
(1)\iff(5)
we will need the fact that all representable VDCs are exponentiable
(this is proved directly in
[
Ark25
]
).
We will use the equivalence
(1)\iff(3)
to provide a simple proof of this fact.

Proposition 5.12

(Representable Implies Exponentiable)
.

All representable VDCs are exponentiable.

Proof.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a representable VDC. From the proof that exponentiable VDCs are precisely those which have
decomposable multicells, it suffices to show that each
n
-ary multicell
\alpha

in
\mathbb{D}
admits essentially unique decompositions of height 2.
Let
0\leq m_{1}\leq\cdots\leq m_{r}=n
be a sequence of non-negative integers, and let

\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{r}
denote the resulting partition of the loose source of
\alpha
into a
sequence of sequences of loose arrows. Then since
\mathbb{D}
has all opcartesian multicells, we have a canonical decomposition of

\alpha
relative to this partition:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\odot(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\odot(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\odot(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{r})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{opcart}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{opcart}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{opcart}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\exists!\alpha^{\flat}}}

Further, by the universal property of opcartesian multicells, any other decomposition
\frac{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{r}}{\beta}
of the same shape factors uniquely through this universal one as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{m_{r}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\odot(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\odot(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{r})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{r}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{opcart}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{opcart}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{r}^{\flat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

Finally, the uniqueness in the factorizations through opcartesian multicells ensures that

\frac{\beta_{1}^{\flat}\cdots\beta_{r}^{\flat}}{\beta}=\alpha^{\flat}
, so the two decompositions are equivalent.
It follows that
\mathbb{D}
admits essentially unique decompositions of multicells, and hence is exponentiable by the equivalences
(1)
\iff
(3)
\iff
(4) in Theorem
5.11
which we’ve proved so far.
Therefore all representable VDCs are exponentiable, as desired.
∎

Note that the proof of
5.12
shows
that we could weaken the hypotheses on
\mathbb{D}
by instead requiring that
it has all loose units and that a sequence of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\varphi}

admits a loose composite if it appears as the subsequence of the loose source
for a multicell in
\mathbb{D}
.

The argument for showing
(1)\iff(5)
in Theorem
5.11
will follow as in the proof
(ii)
\iff
(iii) in Theorem 2 in
[
Tow06
]
. Indeed, although Theorem 2 in
[
Tow06
]
is stated for locales,
the proof applies more generally in any category with finite limits.

Proposition 5.13

(Exponentials via Universal Example (Townsend))
.

Let
\mathsf{C}
be a category with finite limits, and suppose there exists an object
u\in\mathsf{C}
such that for any other
object
x\in\mathsf{C}
, there exists an exponentiable object
e(x)\in\mathsf{C}
and a regular monomorphism

x\hookrightarrow u^{e(x)}
. Then an object
x\in\mathsf{C}
is exponentiable if and only if the exponential
u^{x}
exists.

From Proposition
5.13
, to prove (1)
\iff
(5) in Theorem
5.11
it suffices to show that for every VDC
\mathbb{D}
, we have some exponentiable VDC
p(\mathbb{D})
together with a regular monomorphism
\mathbb{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{p(\mathbb{D})}
. To motivate the exponentiable VDC
p(\mathbb{D})
that we will use, we will modify the categorical hom-bifunctor to a hom-bifunctor for virtual double categories. Recall that for a category
\mathsf{C}
, its hom-bifunctor
\mathsf{C}(-,-):\mathsf{C}^{op}\times\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
is precisely the loose unit for the object
\mathsf{C}
in the profunctor VDC
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}=\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan})
.

Our first generalization of the hom-functor comes from looking at
T
-monoids in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
, for a monad
T
on

\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
, which under the normalized embedding in
[
CS10
, Theorem 8.7]
correspond to
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(T)
-monoids
in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
. Using this perspective, a
T
-monoid determines a category
\mathsf{C}
along with a profunctor

T(\mathsf{C})^{op}\times\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{Set}
with multiplication and unit multicells. In certain cases we can
internalize this profunctor in
T
-monoids, such as when
T=\mathsf{fm}
is the free monoid monad on
\mathsf{Set}
:

Example 5.14
.

If
T=\mathsf{fm}
is the free monoid monad, then
\mathsf{fm}
-monoids are precisely multicategories
\mathsf{M}
, and
\mathsf{fm}(\mathsf{M})
is the multicategory whose objects are sequences of objects in
\mathsf{M}
, and whose multimorphisms are sequences of multimorphisms in
\mathsf{M}
. In this case,
\mathsf{fm}(\mathsf{M})^{op}
is also a multicategory, since sequences of objects can be identified with objects. Then the profunctor along with its multiplication and unit multicells which encode the multicategory
\mathsf{M}
can be enhanced to a functor of multicategories
\mathsf{fm}(\mathsf{M})^{op}\times\mathsf{M}\to\mathsf{Set}_{\times}
, where
\mathsf{Set}_{\times}
is the multicategory whose multimorphisms are given by maps out of products.

In order to obtain an analogous generalization for VDCs we also need to treat the case where
\mathsf{Set}
is replaced by a pre-sheaf category
\mathsf{PSh}(\mathsf{C})
, for
\mathsf{C}
some small category. In this case the profunctor appearing in the last examples is replaced by a profunctor valued pre-sheaf
\mathsf{C}^{op}\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}_{1}
, which in the case of VDCs recovers the graph of profunctors discussed in Subsection
5.2
. As with multicategories, we can internalize the data of this graph of profunctors with its multiplication and unit multicells in terms of a VDF, which for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
takes the form:

\mathbb{D}(-,-):\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}

(5)

Note that
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})
has loose arrows and multicells given by sequences of loose arrows and multicells in
\mathbb{D}
, respectively. Thus,
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})
is a strict double category, and hence it makes sense to take its tight opposite. In more detail we have the following:

Lemma 5.15

(Hom VDF)
.

For every VDC
\mathbb{D}
, there exists a hom VDF
\mathbb{D}(-,-):\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
.

Proof.

We define the hom VDF to consist of the following data:

1.

A pair of objects
(d,e)\in\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}\times\mathbb{D}
is mapped to the set of tight arrows
\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})_{d,e}
.

2.

A pair of tight morphisms
(d_{0}\xleftarrow{f}d_{1},e_{0}\xrightarrow{g}e_{1})
is mapped to the function
\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})_{d_{0},e_{0}}\xrightarrow{g\circ-\circ f}\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})_{d_{1},e_{1}}
given by using post- and pre-composition.

3.

A pair of loose arrows
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}:a_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}a_{n},\psi:d_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}d_{1})
is mapped to the span

\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})_{a_{0},d_{0}}\xleftarrow{s}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\psi)\xrightarrow{t}\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})_{a_{n},d_{1}}

where the apex of the span is the set of multicells with loose source
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
and loose target
\psi
.

4.

Consider a pair of
n
-ary multicells
((\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}),\beta)
, where each
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}
is a sequence of compatible multicells in
\mathbb{D}
with loose target
\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{i}
and loose source
\boldsymbol{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}}_{i}
, and
\beta
is an
n
-ary multicell with loose source
\boldsymbol{\psi}
and loose target
\psi^{\prime}
. The resulting
n
-ary multicell in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
is given by the map of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})(b_{0},c_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})(b_{k_{n}},c_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})(a_{0,0},d_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})(a_{k_{n},0},d_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{g_{0}\circ-\circ f_{0}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\beta\circ-\circ(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n})}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{g_{1}\circ-\circ f_{k_{n}}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

induced by pasting multicells, where
P=\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},\psi_{1})\times_{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})(b_{k_{1}},c_{1})}\cdots\times_{\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})(b_{k_{n-1}},c_{n-1})}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{n},\psi_{n}).

The associativity and unitality of pasting in
\mathbb{D}
ensures that this data defines a VDF

\mathbb{D}(-,-):\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
, as desired.
∎

Alternatively, we can construct the VDC
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})
using the composite of left adjoints in the adjunctions

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Dbl}_{s}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Vdc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

(6)

where
\mathsf{Dbl}_{s}
is the category of strict double categories and strict double functors between them. Explicitly,
\mathbb{F}_{c}
sends a unital VDC
\mathbb{D}
to the strict double category
\mathbb{F}_{c}(\mathbb{D})
with the same underlying category as
\mathbb{D}
, and with loose arrows given by finite sequences of loose arrows in
\mathbb{D}
, while multicells are given by finite sequences of multicells in
\mathbb{D}
.

Since
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}
is a strict double category, by Proposition
5.12
it is exponentiable in
\mathsf{Vdc}
, so we can transpose the hom VDF to a VDF
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}
. On underlying tight categories
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}
restricts to the ordinary Yoneda embedding for
\mathbb{D}_{0}
. A loose arrow
\psi:y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}z
is sent by
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}
to the functor
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})_{1}^{op}\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}_{1}
that maps a sequence of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\varphi}:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x_{n}
to the span with apex the set of multicells with loose source
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
and loose target
\psi
, while mapping a sequence of multicells to the function given by pre-composition by the multicells.

It remains to show that this VDF is a regular monomorphism in the category of large VDCs.
The following result implies that it suffices to show it is fully-faithful, injective on objects, and injective on loose arrows. Here we say that a VDF
i:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{D}
is fully-faithful if it is fully-faithful on underlying tight categories, and for every loose source
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
and loose target
\psi
in
\mathbb{A}
, the induced map on multicells
\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{A})(\boldsymbol{\varphi},\psi)\to\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{B})(i(\boldsymbol{\varphi}),i(\psi))
is bijective.

Lemma 5.16

(Fully-Faithful and Injective on Objects VDFs are Regular Monomorphisms)
.

Let
i:\mathbb{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{B}
be a fully-faithful, injective on objects, and injective on loose arrows VDF. Then
i
is the equalizer of its cokernel pair.

Proof.

Let
q_{1},q_{2}:\mathbb{B}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{B}\cup_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbb{B}
denote the cokernel pair for the fully-faithful, injective on objects, and injective on loose arrows VDF
i:\mathbb{A}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{B}
. Then if
F:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{B}
is a VDF such that
q_{1}\circ F=q_{2}\circ F
, for each object
c
in
\mathbb{C}
,
F(c)
must be in the image of
\mathbb{A}
, since the only objects identified between the two copies of
\mathbb{B}
in the pushout
\mathbb{B}\cup_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbb{B}
are those in the image of
\mathbb{A}
. Similarly, for each loose arrow
\varphi
in
\mathbb{C}
,
F(\varphi)
must be in the image of
\mathbb{A}
. But since
i
is fully-faithful, these two results together imply
F(\mathbb{C})\subseteq i(\mathbb{A})
, and as
i
is also injective on objects and loose arrows
F
uniquely specifies a VDF
F^{\prime}:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{A}
such that
i\circ F^{\prime}=F
, as desired.
∎

To prove that
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}
is fully-faithful, injective on objects, and injective on loose arrows, we will first show an analogue of the Yoneda lemma. In the following statement
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})
for a sequence of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
denotes the sequence of loose arrows
(\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(\varphi_{1})\cdots\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(\varphi_{n}))
in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}
.

Lemma 5.17

(Yoneda Lemma for VDCs)
.

For a VDC
\mathbb{D}
, and a sequence of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\varphi}:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x_{n}
in
\mathbb{D}
,
evaluation at the sequence of identity multicells

\operatorname{id}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}=(\operatorname{id}_{\varphi_{1}}\cdots\operatorname{id}_{\varphi_{n}})

viewed as a unary multicell in
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})
induces a natural isomorphism

\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\\
\Phi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\xrightarrow{\text{ev}_{\operatorname{id}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}\Phi(\boldsymbol{\varphi})

where
\Phi:F\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}G
is an arbitrary loose morphism in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}
.

Note that Lemma
5.17
applied to the case where
\Phi
is of the form
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(\psi)
for some loose arrow
\psi:y_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y_{1}
in
\mathbb{D}
gives fully-faithfullness of
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}
. We will first give a direct proof of Lemma
5.17
using the classical Yoneda lemma for 1-categories.

Proof of Lemma
5.17
.

Using the description of multicells in
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})
as sequences of multicells in
\mathbb{D}
, multicells in

\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}
with loose source
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})
and loose target

\Phi
are equivalent to the data of natural transformations
\gamma_{0}:\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})(-,x_{0})\Rightarrow F
,

\gamma_{1}:\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})(-,x_{n})\Rightarrow G
, and

\Gamma:\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})_{1}(-,\boldsymbol{\varphi})\Rightarrow\Phi
which are compatible under whiskering
with source and target functors.
In particular, the claim now follows by the ordinary Yoneda lemma for the categories
\mathbb{D}_{0}
and

\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})_{1}
.
∎

More generally this result can be phrased and proved purely in the language of monoids in a VDC with restrictions. So as not to interrupt the current exposition on exponentiability, we leave this discussion to Appendix
C
.

Next, observe that
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}
is also injective on objects, and similarly for loose arrows, since for any two distinct objects
x
and
x^{\prime}
,
the representable functors
\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})_{-,x}
and
\mathsf{Tight}(\mathbb{D})_{-,x^{\prime}}
are distinct. Therefore, for each VDC
\mathbb{D}
, we have a fully-faithful, injective on objects, and injective on loose arrows VDF
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}:\mathbb{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}
, which by Lemma
5.16
is a regular monomorphism. The desired exponentiability result now follows from Proposition
5.13
:

Lemma 5.18

(Exponentiability of VDCs via Span Exponential)
.

A VDC
\mathbb{D}
is exponentiable in
\mathsf{Vdc}
if and only if the VDC
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathbb{D}}
exists in the category of large VDCs.

This completes the proof of Theorem
5.11
.

6
Examples and Counterexamples

6.1
Examples of Exponentiable VDCs

As a consequence of Corollary
5.12
, for any category
\mathsf{C}
, the tight VDCs
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{C})
and
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}_{u}(\mathsf{C})
are both exponentiable,
and the VDC
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathbf{B})
is exponentiable for any bicategory
\mathbf{B}
. For example, the walking object VDC
\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}
is exponentiable,
and
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}}=\mathbb{C}\mathsf{haotic}(\mathbb{E}_{0})
is the chaotic VDC associated to the category
\mathbb{E}_{0}.

On the other hand, because
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}
the non-unital loose arrow is not exponentiable (c.f. Example
4.15
),
the functor
(-)_{1}
does
not
admit a right adjoint, and so we don’t get analogous chaotic VDCs of loose arrows in
\mathbb{E}.

The following lemma allows us to construct some interesting examples of exponentiable VDCs by hand.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a VDC with no nullary multicells. If
\mathbb{D}
satisfies both (P1) and (P3) of
Definition
5.2
with
T
restricted to range over binary-unary trees,
9
9

9

That is,
\mathbb{D}
admits
essentially unique decompositions whose shape is a binary-unary tree of height 2 (Definition
5.1
), and
every height-2 decomposition of any shape essentially arises from one shaped by a binary-unary tree.

then the unitalization
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{D})
is exponentiable.

Proof.

By definition of the multicells in the unitalization
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{D})
, it still satisfies (P1) of
Definition
5.2
for binary-unary trees
T
.
Thus, by the equivalence
(1)\iff(2)
in Theorem
5.11
it suffices to show
property (P1) is satisfied for height
2
unary-nullary trees
T
,
and that property (P3) for arbitrary height
2
trees
T^{\prime}
is not broken by applying
\mathbb{F}_{u}.

First, for any multicell
\alpha
, as below, the universal property of opcartesian multicells implies that for any partition of the loose source
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1},\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{2})
, we have the decomposition below right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

these decompositions are terminal
in a sense that guarantees the essential uniqueness clause of (P1). Thus (P1) holds for

Now it remains to prove that property (P3) holds for

Fix an arbitrary height

tree

.
First, note that by definition of

,

after forgetting the freely added loose units.
Then a decomposition

in

arises from either a decomposition of a multicell in

or from
a factorization of a tight arrow, as in the following example, assuming

:

where the top layer
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
consists only of opcartesian multicells
and the center layer
\boldsymbol{\delta}
consists only of multicells in
\mathbb{D}

and possibly unary multicells of the form
I_{a}
for
a
a tight arrow in
\mathbb{D}
.

Since
\mathbb{D}
satisfies (P3),
\frac{\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\beta}
can
be decomposed into a binary-unary tree shape.
Further, since
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
only consists of opcartesian multicells,
which necessarily all have identity tight boundaries,
we can bring each non-identity opcartesian cell in
\boldsymbol{\gamma}

into its own separate layer.
Finally, we can decompose each opcartesian multicells of arity
\geq 3

as composites of opcartesian multicells of arity
2
and
1
, producing a sequence of binary-unary trees
which we graft together to show that
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{D})
satisfies property (P3) of
Definition
5.2
, and hence is exponentiable, as desired. ∎

As an immediate consequence we can determine which of the unitalizations
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n})
are exponentiable:

Corollary 6.2
.

The VDC
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n})
is exponentiable for
0\leq n\leq 2
, and is non-exponentiable for
n\geq 3
.

Proof.

First, observe that when
n\geq 3
, Theorem
4.14
implies that the VDC
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n})
is
non-exponentiable since we can’t factor out a binary multicell from the unique
n
-ary multicell coming from
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}
.
As a consequence, we see that unital VDCs need not be exponentiable.

For the first part of the claim note that
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{0})
and
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{1})
are both representable,
and hence are exponentiable by Proposition
5.12
. However, the VDC
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{2})
is not representable.
Nonetheless,
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{2}
, as it only has identity multicells and a single non-identity binary multicell,
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
6.1
: the only binary-unary trees of height
2

whose composite is less than ternary are the three below, with respect to which every virtual double category admits essentially unique decompositions.
Furthermore, the same shapes gives the only height-2 trees for which
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{2}
admits any cell decompositions whatsoever, so that
decompositions of height
2
serve as their own binary-unary refinements.
Thus, by Lemma
6.1
we have that
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{2})
is exponentiable.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}

∎

VDCs of cospans

An important class of exponentiable VDCs that do not arise as representable VDCs are the cospan VDCs.

Proposition 6.3

(Cospans give exponentiable VDCs)
.

For any category
\mathsf{E}
, the VDC

is exponentiable, and is representable if and only if
\mathsf{E}
admits finite pushouts.

Thus,
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{E})
has decomposable multicells, and therefore by Theorem
5.11
is exponentiable.
∎

Exponentiable multicategories as exponentiable VDCs

As mentioned previously, the naming convention for pro-representable VDCs comes from their relation to promonoidal multicategories,
which are precisely the exponentiable multicategories (c.f.
[
Pis14
]
), where monoidal categories are the representable multicategories.
These exponentiable multicategories can be realized as a certain class of exponentiable VDCs through the fully-faithful embedding

B:\mathbf{MultCat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Vdc}

sending a multicategory to the VDC with trivial underlying tight category, one loose arrow for each object, and multicells precisely the multimorphisms in the multicategory.
The essential image of this embedding consists precisely of those VDCs with trivial underlying tight category. Additionally, this embedding fits into a 2-adjunction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{MultCat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Vdc}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Coll}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dashv}

where
\mathsf{Coll}(\mathbb{D})=\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}\cup_{\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(\mathbb{D})}\mathbb{D}
is given by collapsing the underlying tight category of
\mathbb{D}
.

The fact that
B
preserves exponentials follows from the more general result in
[
Ark25
, Prop 3.16]
which shows that
\mathbf{MultCat}
admits powers by exponentiable VDCs, and
B
strictly preserves these exponentials. Thus, it remains only to prove that
B
preserves and reflects exponentiable objects.

Proposition 6.4

(Exponentiable Multicategories in VDCs)
.

The 2-functor
B:\mathbf{MultCat}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{Vdc}
preserves and reflects exponentiable objects as well as exponentials.

Proof.

Recall that a multicategory
\mathsf{M}
is promonoidal, or equivalently exponentiable, if and only if for all
k_{1},...,k_{n}\geq 0
, the composition function

\int^{(c_{1},...,c_{n})\in\mathsf{M}_{0}^{\times n}}\mathsf{M}(c_{1},...,c_{n};a)\times\Pi_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{M}(b_{k_{i-1}+1},\ldots,b_{k_{i}};c_{i})\xrightarrow{\circ}\mathsf{M}(b_{1},...,b_{m_{n}};a)

is a bijection, where
m_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}k_{i}
and we take
k_{0}=0.

However, under the identification
B
of multicategories with VDCs having trivial underlying category,
these composition functions being bijections corresponds exactly to the statement that the associated VDC

B(\mathsf{M})
admits arbitrary decompositions for multicells up to equivalence, or equivalently by Theorem
5.11
is exponentiable.
Thus,
\mathsf{M}
is exponentiable as a multicategory if and only if
B(\mathsf{M})
is exponentiable as a VDC.
∎

6.2
A non-characterization: pro-double categories á la Arkor

In
[
Ark25
, Definition 3.13]
, Arkor defines a pro-double category as follows:

Definition 6.5

(Arkor)
.

(Pro-double category)
A pro-double category is a pseudocategory object in the bicategory of small categories, profunctors, and natural transformations, with representable source and target.
∎

This involves giving a graph of categories
s,t:\mathbb{D}_{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{D}_{0},
an “identity”
profunctor
I:\mathbb{D}_{1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\mathbb{D}_{0},
and a “composition” profunctor

\odot:\mathbb{D}_{2}\to\mathbb{D}_{1},
respecting sources and targets,
where
\mathbb{D}_{n}
is the iterated pullback of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
over
\mathbb{D}_{0}

and we take the pullback in
\mathsf{Cat}
rather than (where it need not exist) in
\mathsf{Prof}.

As profunctors,
I
and
\odot
comprise the data of nullary and binary multicells in a hypothetical
exponentiable VDC associated to the pro-double category
\mathbb{D}
.

As a pseudo-category object, such a
\mathbb{D}
also contains unitors and, critically, an associator as
below:

It is left implicit in Arkor’s definition that, when
\alpha
is whiskered with
s
and
t,

the result should be the identity natural transformation, but this requirement appears clearly in (1.4) of

[
Mar06
]
, on which Arkor, Bourke, and Ko rely in their treatment
[
ABK24
]
of
partly strict 2-categorial limit sketches.

Thus if we draw a value of
\odot
as a binary multicell and a value of the hom profunctor of
\mathbb{D}_{1}
as a unary
cell, then schematically, the associator
\alpha
gives an equivalence between the two ways of building ternary
cells out of binary ones:

Let us be clear what this means concretely:
if we apply
\alpha
to a decomposed ternary multicell
\tau
in
\mathbb{D}
whose tight source is the path
f_{1},f_{2},
then

\alpha(\tau)
will have tight source
f_{1}^{\prime},f_{2}^{\prime}
such that
f_{1}f_{2}=f_{1}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime},
and similarly for the tight
targets. But the definition of pseudo-category has no way to ask, what might seem more natural, that
f_{i}=f_{i}^{\prime}
,
since the individual components of the tight source and target paths are not available for a pseudocategory
in a general bicategory. Indeed, these components are not even well-defined
for the composition of profunctors, quotienting as it does by the internal action of unary multicells.

Furthermore,
\alpha
must satisfy a pentagon coherence condition. One of the sides of the pentagon
must be a map
\beta
as below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{2}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\odot\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\odot\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\odot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{1}\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\odot\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\odot\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\odot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Schematically,
\beta
applies the associator
\alpha
in the upper-left corner of a quaternary multicell
as below, without changing the remainder:

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

However, a concern now arises. As discussed above, while
\alpha
must preserve the
composite
of
the tight source and target paths of a ternary multicell, we are unable to ask that it preserve the individual
components of those paths. Consequently, it is unclear that the map
\beta
, as we have attempted to
define it, even exists, since it must implicitly rewrite also the unary multicells.

Abstractly, the problem is that, whereas the functor
(-)\times A
defines a pseudo-double functor

\mathbb{P}\mathrm{rof}\to\mathbb{P}\mathrm{rof}
for any category
A,
insofar as if

H:B\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}C,K:C\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}D,
then the canonical map

(H\odot K)\times A\to(H\times A)\odot(K\times A):(A\times B\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}A\times D)

is an isomorphism, the same need not hold if we set
A,B,C,D,H,K
over a fifth category
X
and
consider the functor
(-)\times_{X}A:\mathbb{P}\mathrm{rof}/X\to\mathbb{P}\mathrm{rof}.

10
10

10

Here,
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}/X
is the slice double category (
[
GP99
, 1.7]
),
whose tight category is
\mathsf{/}X
and whose loose arrows are profunctors equipped with a cell to
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}.

This functor is, in fact, only lax:

Example 6.6

(Pullback is lax)
.

Consider the following presentation of a situation in
\mathbb{P}\mathrm{rof}/X,
where

X
is the path of length
2,

A
the path of length
1,
and we fix the functor
f:A\to X

picking out the long arrow. Then the restriction cell makes the hom-profunctor
I_{A}
into a profunctor over

I_{X}.
Suppose furthermore that
B,C,D
are all terminal and equipped with functors
to
X
picking out the three objects, and
H:B\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}C,K:C\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}D
are both generated by a
single heteromorpism, as drawn below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}

We then compare the two profunctors
(H\odot K)\times_{X}A,(H\times_{X}A)\odot(K\times_{X}A):\{(\bullet,a_{1})\}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\{(\bullet,a_{2})\}.
The one sends
has a single heteromorphism
(h\odot k,f),
while the other is empty.

This means that, if we have a morphism
H\odot K\to H^{\prime}\odot K^{\prime}
of profunctors over
X,

we cannot in general derive a morphism
(H\times_{X}A)\odot(K\times_{X}A)\to(H^{\prime}\times_{X}A)\odot(K^{\prime}\times_{X}A),

which is the shape of thing we would need to define
\beta
as above. Thus it does not seem possible to fully
realize Arkor’s definition of pro-double category, which in a strange
way disproves that branch of the conjecture.

More precisely, we will prove the following:

Proposition 6.7

(Not all exponentiable VDCs are pro-double)
.

There exists an exponentiable VDC
\mathbb{D}
for which no morphism of profunctors of the signature of the supposed map
\alpha\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}}\mathbb{D}_{1}
above exists.

.

Note that by construction
\mathbb{C}
has no nullary multicells, and every multicell is either unary,
binary, or the unique trinary multicell, which decomposes into binary multicells as above.
In particular, it follows that
\mathbb{C}
satisfies property (P1) of Definition
5.2
for binary-unary trees,
since these are the only binary-unary decompositions of a trinary multicell.
Additionally, every 2-layer decomposition in
\mathbb{C}
is
already a binary-unary decomposition.
Thus,
\mathbb{C}
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
6.1
, so its unitalization
\mathbb{D}:=\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{C})
is exponentiable.

However, no map of profunctors
