\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{0}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lambda_{2}^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\lambda_{3}^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{2}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\psi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\psi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\psi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\psi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{3}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{2}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{3}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\beta_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\beta_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\beta_{2}^{\prime\prime}}}}

such that
\frac{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}{\beta_{1}}=\frac{(\alpha_{1}^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime\prime})}{\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}}
and
\frac{\alpha_{3}}{\beta_{2}}=\frac{\alpha_{3}^{\prime\prime}}{\beta_{2}^{\prime\prime}}
. However, note that the equality
\frac{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}{\beta_{1}}=\frac{(\alpha_{1}^{\prime\prime},\alpha_{2}^{\prime\prime})}{\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}}
is only possible if
\alpha_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\alpha_{1}^{\prime}
,
\alpha_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\alpha_{2}^{\prime}
and
\beta_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\beta_{1}^{\prime}
. Thus, since there are no unary multicells
\alpha_{3}^{\prime\prime}
and
\beta_{2}^{\prime\prime}
with the specified boundaries such that
\frac{\alpha_{3}}{\beta_{2}}=\frac{\alpha_{3}^{\prime\prime}}{\beta_{2}^{\prime\prime}}
, no such map can exist.
∎

Exponentiating Normal VDCs

As the previous section of counter-examples illustrates, unitality is not sufficient for exponentiability of VDCs.
Nonetheless, unital VDCs provide a host of examples of exponentiable VDCs,
due to the fact that it is in general easier to detect when a unital VDC is exponentiable.
Before providing conditions for VDCs with units to be exponentiable, we’ll begin by
explicating the behaviour of exponentials in
\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
under the forgetful functor to VDCs.

First, note that the since forgetful functor
U:\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}\to\mathsf{Vdc}
is cocontinuous and conservative, it creates all colimits.
In particular, it follows that for a unital VDC
\mathbb{D}
, the functor
\mathbb{F}_{u}(U(-))\times\mathbb{D}:\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}\to\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
commutes with all colimits if and only if the functor
U\mathbb{F}_{u}(-)\times U(\mathbb{D}):\mathsf{Vdc}\to\mathsf{Vdc}
commutes with all colimits.
But
\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
is also locally finitely presentable, so we obtain that
U
reflects exponentiable objects:

Proposition 6.8
.

If
\mathbb{D}\in\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
is a normal VDC, and if
U(\mathbb{D})\in\mathsf{Vdc}
is exponentiable,
then
\mathbb{D}
is exponentiable in
\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
.

We will leave a full characterization of the exponentiable normal VDCs,
in analogy with Theorem
4.14
, to future work.

For unital VDCs
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{E}
such that the exponential exists in
\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
,
we will denote it by
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}_{n}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
.
Using the exponentiation adjunction in
\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
, and the descriptions in Lemma
4.2
,
we have the following description of
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}_{n}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
’s data:

1.

Objects are normal VDFs
\mathbb{D}\cong\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}_{u}\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
.

2.

Tight arrows are normal VDFs
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
, which are precisely tight transformations between normal VDFs.

3.

Loose arrows are normal VDFs
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
, which are VDFs
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
whose restriction to either object in
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose})
yields a normal VDF.

4.

n
-ary multicells are normal VDFs
\mathbb{F}_{u}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n})\times\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
.

Thus, the exponential is a sub-VDC of
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
with inherited loose units.
We can use the exponential in
\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
to internalize the adjunction
U\dashv\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}
in the following sense:

Proposition 6.9
.

If
\mathbb{D}
is a unital VDC which is exponentiable in
\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
, then for any VDC
\mathbb{E}
we have a natural isomorphism

\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(U(\mathbb{D}),\mathbb{E})\cong\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}_{n}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E}))

Proof.

Using the Yoneda lemma the result follows from the following sequence of natural isomorphisms for an arbitrary unital VDC
\mathbb{A}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(U(\mathbb{D}),\mathbb{E}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(U(\mathbb{A}),\mathbb{E}^{U(\mathbb{D})})

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(U(\mathbb{A})\times U(\mathbb{D}),\mathbb{E})

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(U(\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{D}),\mathbb{E})

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}(\mathbb{A}\times\mathbb{D},\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E}))

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}_{n}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{E})))

where in the middle we used that
U
preserves limits.
Thus, we have a natural isomorphism

\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(U(\mathbb{D}),-)\cong\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}_{n}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(-))

∎

Now that we have a description of exponentials in
\mathsf{Vdc}_{n}
,
let’s see how the pro-representability condition in Definition
5.2

simplifies for normal VDCs.

Proposition 6.10
.

If
\mathbb{D}
is a normal VDC, then
\mathbb{D}
is pro-representable if and only if

\mathbb{D}
satisfies both (P1) and (P3) of Definition
5.2

with
T
restricted to range over binary-unary trees.

Proof.

The necessity is immediate, so suppose
\mathbb{D}

satisfies both (P1) and (P3) of Definition
5.2

with
T
restricted to range over binary-unary trees.
The fact that
\mathbb{D}
satisfies (P1) for unary-nullary trees follows
from the existence of loose units, as in the proof of Lemma
6.1
.

For property (P3) fix an arbitrary height 2 tree
T^{\prime}
.
Then if
\frac{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}{\beta}
is a decomposition in
\mathbb{D}

with shape
T^{\prime}
, we can use the universal property of loose units
to pull out nullary shapes from
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
,
from left to right, as in the example below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{3}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\varphi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{3}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{2}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{3}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\psi}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{{{\psi}}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{3}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{1}^{\flat}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\alpha_{3}^{\flat}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

The result is a decomposition where the second layer contains no nullary multicells
and above the second layer, each layer has a single nullary multicell
while the rest of the multicells are unary.
We can then apply (P3) for binary-unary trees
T
to decompose the bottom two layers
into a binary-unary tree, so that the whole decomposition has the shape of a
binary-nullary tree.
Thus,
\mathbb{D}
satisfies (P3), and hence is pro-representable, as desired.
∎

7
Representability of Exponentials

Our next goal is to determine sufficient conditions on an exponentiable VDC
\mathbb{D}
and a VDC
\mathbb{E}
so that the exponential
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{D}}
has (weak) composites. Using these conditions we will also provide sufficient conditions for the VDC
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{D},\mathbb{E})
of virtual double functors to be representable.

7.1
Relative Decomposition Conditions for VDCs

In order to find a sufficient condition for representability of exponentials, we will consider a decomposition condition inspired by Paré’s AFP condition (c.f.
[
Par13
]
) and Behr et. al’s cylindrical decomposition property (c.f.
[
BMZ23
]
).
Namely, we will follow Paré’s approach in having the factorization condition be only up to essentially unique decompositions, but unlike in
[
Par13
]
, our globular decomposition conditions for VDCs do not involve vertical reduction relations (i.e. loose reduction relations in our current terminology), as these explicitly involve composition of loose arrows, which is not possible in general VDCs.
Nonetheless, even without this condition we can still prove representability results for exponentials, while also providing a number of VDCs which satisfy these properties.

Our globular decompositions will arise as a special case of the following more general notion of decompositions relative to a VDF
F:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{D}
, which is analogous to the right Conduché condition in
[
GP07
]
. Recall from Theorem
5.11
that a VDC
\mathbb{D}
is exponentiable if and only if the composition transformation

\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}))\odot\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})\xrightarrow{\mathsf{comp}}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\mu_{\mathsf{fc}},1):\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1}))^{op}\times\mathbb{D}_{1}\to\mathsf{Set}

is an isomorphism.

Definition 7.1

(Relative Decomposition)
.

Let
i:\mathsf{C}\hookrightarrow\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
be a wide subcategory and set
i_{1}:\mathsf{C}_{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{D}_{1}

for the inclusion of
\mathsf{C}\cap\mathbb{D}_{1}
into
\mathbb{D}_{1}.

Next let
\mathsf{Q}:\mathsf{C}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\mathsf{C}_{1}

be a sub-profunctor of the restriction
\mathsf{MCell}(i,i_{1})
of
\mathsf{MCell}:\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\mathbb{D}_{1}
along
i
and
i_{1}.

(Note that
\mathsf{MCell}(i,i_{1})
contains all the multicells of
\mathbb{D},
since
\mathsf{C}
is wide, merely restricting the
actions of unary multicells on either side.)

Then for an integer
n\geq 0
,
we say
\mathbb{D}

has
n
-ary
\mathsf{Q}
-decompositions
if the composition transformation

\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}))(1,i)\odot\mathsf{Q}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{comp}}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\mu_{\mathsf{fc}},i_{1}):\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1}))\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\mathsf{C}_{1}

is an isomorphism on the fiber over
n
-ary multicells.
We say
\mathbb{D}

has
\mathsf{Q}
-decompositions
if it has
n
-ary
\mathsf{Q}
-decompositions for all
n\geq 0
,
and
positive
\mathsf{Q}
-decompositions
if it has
n
-ary
\mathsf{Q}
-decompositions for
n\geq 1
.
∎

Diagrammatically, this definition says that any
n
-ary multicell below left admits an essentially unique decomposition, as below right,
given any partition of the length of its domain
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
to determine the arities in
\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\#}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{a^{\prime}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{b^{\prime}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\chi}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\psi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\#}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

where
\beta\in\mathsf{Q}(\boldsymbol{\chi},\psi)
and the essential uniqueness is up to
lists of unary multicells falling in
\mathsf{C}\subseteq\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
.

Definition 7.2

(Globular Decompositions)
.

Let
i_{g}:\mathsf{gl}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1}))\hookrightarrow\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
be the wide subcategory
whose morphisms are those sequences of unary multicells with outermost tight arrows given by identities,
and let
\mathsf{gCell}(\mathbb{D})\subseteq\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(i_{g},i_{g1})
be the sub-profunctor consisting of globular non-nullary multicells in
\mathbb{D}
.
Then for an integer
n\geq 0
we say that
\mathbb{D}
has
n
-ary globular decompositions
if it has
n
-ary
\mathsf{gCell}(\mathbb{D})
decompositions.
Further, if
\mathbb{D}
has the
n
-ary globular decompositions for all
n\geq 0
,
we simply say it has globular decompositions,
and if it has
n
-ary globular decompositions for all
n>0
we say it has positive globular decompositions.
∎

Having globular decompositions and positive globular decompostions
will be our main generalizations of Paré’s AFP condition to VDCs.
Note that in the definition we exclude decompositions where the bottom layer is a nullary multicell.
The motivation for this restriction is that very few VDCs would have
0
-ary globular decompositions
if decompositions with nullary bottom layer were included.
Indeed, if a VDC has
0
-ary globular decompositions
while allowing for globular nullary multicells,
then it can only have nullary multicells with loose target an endo-loose arrow
a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}a

and with tight boundary components being equal.
This is also noted in Section 5 of
[
Par13
]
.
Nonetheless, there are two important examples of VDCs which would
have
0
-ary globular decompositions with nullary bottom layer,
along with our current notion of globular decompositions.

Example 7.3
.

All VDCs with discrete underlying tight category have globular decompositions,
since every multicell is globular.
For example:

1.

The loose embedding of a bicategory
\mathbf{B}
as the VDC
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathbf{B})
has globular decompositions.

2.

The categorification of a multicategory
\mathsf{M}
as the VDC
B(\mathsf{M})
has globular decompositions.

In addition to these examples, cospan VDCs, which we saw in Proposition
6.3
were exponentiable,
also have globular decompositions.

Proposition 7.4

(Cospan VDCs have Globular Decompositions)
.

For any category
\mathsf{E}
, the VDC
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{ospan}(\mathsf{E})
always has globular decompositions.

Proof.

The proof follows as in Proposition
6.3
. Namely, if we have a multicell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{n-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

then if
f_{i}:x_{i}\to w_{1}
denote the unique maps for the middle
x_{i}
’s, so

f_{i}=b_{i}\cdot e_{i}=a_{i+1}\cdot e_{i+1}
, then we can slide the tight boundary maps up as in the decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{n-1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

Additionally, this is the terminal decomposition: indeed any other such decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{n-1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{n}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{1}}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{n-1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{n-1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{n}}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{1}}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{n}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

is equivalent to this one via the intermediary 3-layer decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{n-1}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{n}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{1}}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{n-1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{a_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{n-1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{n}}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{1}}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{n-1}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{n}^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}

as desired. The same argument applies when

, though in this case the terminal decomposition is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{e_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d_{1}}}

∎

For any VDC
\mathbb{D}
, we can also show that the strict double category
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}

(See Equation
6
) has positive globular decompositions, which will be important for later embedding results.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be an arbitrary VDC. Then the VDC
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}
has positive globular decompositions.

Proof.

Recall that multicells in
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})
are given by compatible sequences of multicells in
\mathbb{D}
.
Thus, an
n
-ary multicell in
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}
, for
n\geq 1
, takes the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}

where
\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n}
are multicells in
\mathbb{D}
, and
the list of lists
(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n})

is viewed as a single loose arrow in
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}
.
We then have a natural decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{n}}

where in the center row each
\boldsymbol{\psi}_{i}
is viewed as a single loose in
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}
,
so that the top row consists of unary multicells and the bottom row is an
n
-ary opcartesian multicell.

To show essential uniqueness suppose we had another globular decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\chi_{1,1}\cdots\chi_{1,l_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\chi_{n,1}\cdots\chi_{n,l_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1,1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1,l_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\psi}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n,1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n,l_{n}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{1,1}\cdots\gamma_{1,l_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma_{n,1}\cdots\gamma_{n,l_{n}})}

where
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i}=(\chi_{i,1}\cdots\chi_{i,l_{i}})
is viewed as a single loose arrow in
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}
.
Then we have the canonical decomposition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{n}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{k_{n}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\chi_{1,1}\cdots\chi_{1,l_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\chi_{n,1}\cdots\chi_{n,l_{n}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1,1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1,l_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n,1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n,l_{n}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1,1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\psi}_{1,l_{1}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\psi}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n,1}\cdots\boldsymbol{\psi}_{n,l_{n}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{n}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\gamma_{1,1}\cdots\gamma_{1,l_{1}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\gamma_{n,1}\cdots\gamma_{n,l_{n}})}}

where all the middle multicells
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}=(\gamma_{i,1}\cdots\gamma_{i,l_{i}})
are viewed as unary multicells, completing the proof.
∎

To see a first application of VDCs having globular decompositions,
we describe multicells in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
when
\mathbb{A}
is an exponentiable VDC
with
n
-ary globular decompositions for some
n
.

Lemma 7.6

(Multicells in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
for
\mathbb{A}
Having Globular Decompositions)
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is an exponentiable VDC with
n
-ary globular decompositions for some
n\geq 1
, and
\mathbb{X}
is an arbitrary VDC,
then
n
-ary multicells in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
are uniquely determined by their action on globular multicells.
Additionally, any assignment on globular multicells which is compatible with the actions on the boundary loose arrows extends to such a multicell.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma:(\alpha_{0}{\;}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}{}_{\Psi}{\;}\alpha_{1}):(^{F_{0}\cdots F_{n}}_{G_{0}\;\;\;\;G_{1}})
be an
n
-ary multicell in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
. Then for an
n
-ary multicell in
\mathbb{A}
below left, we can factor it using

n
-ary globular decompositions as below right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha^{\prime}}}

Functoriality of pasting for the multicell
\Gamma
then gives the equality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{f_{0}(a)}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{1}(b)}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}({\boldsymbol{\varphi}}})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{F_{0}a}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{F_{n}b}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{0}(\text{id}_{z})}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{1}(\text{id}_{w})}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\Psi(\psi)}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\Psi(\psi)}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\beta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\alpha^{\prime}}}

Thus,
\Gamma
is determined by its image on globular multicells, as desired.

Conversely, suppose that for the same boundary we have an assignment
\Gamma
on globular
n
-ary multicells such that for any pasting equality in
\mathbb{A}
below left, we have the equality below right in
\mathbb{X}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}w^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}w^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}u}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}({{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{F_{0}a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{F_{n}b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}({{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{F_{0}a^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{F_{n}b^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}({{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}}})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{f_{0}(z)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{f_{1}(w)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}({{\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime\prime}}})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{f_{0}(z^{\prime})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{f_{1}(w^{\prime})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{c^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{d^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Psi\psi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{G_{0}c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{G_{1}d}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Psi\psi^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{G_{0}c^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{G_{1}d^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Psi\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Psi\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\beta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\beta^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\beta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\gamma^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi\gamma^{\prime}}}

Then these assignments assemble into an
n
-ary multicell in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
.
∎

7.2
Local colimits and the characterization theorem

Next, we can define local colimits in a VDC in analogy with Definition 2.2.1 of
[
Par13
]
.
First, for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
with objects
x,y\in\mathbb{D}
, let
\mathsf{gCell}_{n}(\mathbb{D})(x,y)

denote the category whose objects are length
n
sequences of loose arrows
x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
, and
whose morphisms are sequences of unary multicells, with the outermost tight arrows being identities.
Then for a category
\mathsf{I}
, a functor
\boldsymbol{\varphi}:\mathsf{I}\to\mathsf{gCell}_{n}(\mathbb{D})(x,y)
,
tight arrows
a:x\to z
and
b:y\to w
, and a loose arrow
\psi:z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}w
, we will write

\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\mathbb{D})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\varphi}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for the set of cocones under
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
with specified boundary.
When
a
and
b
are not written explicitly, the boundary will be understood to be identity arrows.
Here a cocone under
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
is a collection of multicells

\left\{\alpha_{i}:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\varphi(i)}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}_{i\in\mathsf{I}}
satisfying

\frac{\boldsymbol{\varphi}(p)}{\alpha_{j}}=\alpha_{i}
for all
p:i\to j
in
\mathsf{I}
.

We define a stable notion of local colimits in VDCs, so that colimits in
\mathsf{gCell}(\mathbb{D})(x,y):=\mathsf{gCell}_{1}(\mathbb{D})(x,y)

must both exist and satisfy a property virtualizing the preservation of local colimits by composition of loose arrows.

Definition 7.7

(Local
\mathsf{I}
-Colimits)
.

Let
\mathsf{I}
be a small category and let
\mathbb{D}
be a VDC. Then
\mathbb{D}
is said to have local
\mathsf{I}
-colimits if the following are satisfied:

(L1)

For each
x,y\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{D})
, category
\mathsf{gCell}(\mathbb{D})(x,y)
has
\mathsf{I}
-colimits.

(L2)

For every pair of sequences of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
and
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{2}:z\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}w
,
and every functor
\varphi:\mathsf{I}\to\mathsf{gCell}(\mathbb{D})(y,z)
with colimit
\colim_{\mathsf{I}}\,\varphi:y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}z
and
colimit cocone
\lambda_{i}:\varphi_{i}\Rightarrow\Phi
, pre-composition by the cone legs induces a bijection

\mathsf{Sq}_{|\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}|+1+|\boldsymbol{\chi}_{2}|}(\mathbb{D})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1},\colim_{\mathsf{I}}\,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\chi}_{2}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{Sq}_{|\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}|+1+|\boldsymbol{\chi}_{2}|}(\mathbb{D})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1},\varphi,\boldsymbol{\chi}_{2}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

between multicells and cocones for any fixed tight arrows
a:x\to x^{\prime}
and
b:w\to w^{\prime}
, and any fixed loose arrow
\psi:x^{\prime}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}w^{\prime}
.

We will say a VDC has
weak local
\mathsf{I}
-colimits
if it only satisfies (L2) for
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{2}

being empty sequences of loose arrows, and in this case we refer to the condition as (wL2). ∎

We will be most often considering local
\mathsf{I}
-colimits in VDCs
\mathbb{D}
with weak non-nullary composites,
in which case (gL2) (i.e. condition (L2) when
a
and
b
are identity tight arrows) can be rephrased as saying
that the functors given by composing loose arrows preserve the
\mathsf{I}
-colimits in (L1). On the other hand,
(wL2) gives a tight-base change invariance of colimits. When the VDC being considered has restrictions (L2) follows from (gL2).

With Lemma
7.6
we can use local colimits to provide sufficient conditions for
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
to have (weak) non-nullary composites. The arguments are largely analogous to those due to Paré in
[
Par13
]
.

Theorem 7.8
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is a small exponentiable VDC that has positive globular decompositions and
\mathbb{X}
is a large (weakly) locally cocomplete VDC with (weak) non-nullary composites, then
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
has (weak) non-nullary composites.

Before proceeding to the proof, we’ll construct a candidate for the weak composite in the exponential.

Construction 7.9
.

Let
\mathbb{A}
be an exponentiable VDC that has
n
-ary globular decompositions,
for
n\geq 2
some integer, and let
\mathbb{X}
be a weakly locally cocomplete VDC with weak non-nullary composites.
Consider a length-
n
sequence of loose arrows in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
,
\boldsymbol{\Phi}:F_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}F_{n}
.
To define a candidate for its composite
\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi}):F_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}F_{n}
we need to define

\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})
on loose arrows in
\mathbb{A}
, and on unary multicells.
Roughly speaking, we will define
\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})
on a loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{A}
by
approximating
\varphi
by domains of globular
n
-ary multicells, applying
\boldsymbol{\Phi}
to their domains, and colimiting all the results.

In more detail: given a loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{A}
, let
\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}
,
n\geq 2
,
be the category whose objects are globular
n
-ary multicells in
\mathbb{A}
with loose target
\varphi
,
and whose non-identity morphisms
\alpha\to\beta
consist of compatible sequences of multicells
(\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n})\in\mathsf{MCell}_{n}(\mathbb{A})

such that
\alpha=\frac{\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n}}{\beta}
. Note that necessarily the tight source of
\gamma_{1}
and the
tight target of
\gamma_{n}
are identities, so
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in\mathsf{gCell}_{n}(\mathbb{A})
.
Then we define a functor
\Gamma^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}_{\varphi,n}:\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}\to\mathsf{gCell}(\mathbb{X})(F_{0}x,F_{n}y)
by sending an object
\alpha:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{n}\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
to the composite

\Gamma_{\varphi,n}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}(\alpha):=\Phi_{1}\psi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n}\psi_{n}

and sending a morphism
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\varphi_{n}^{\prime}\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}\to{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{n}\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
to the unique multicell appearing in the factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\psi}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi\varphi_{1}^{\prime}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi\varphi_{n}^{\prime}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi\psi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi\psi_{n}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Phi\psi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi\psi_{n}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{opcart}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{opcart}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\Gamma_{\varphi,n}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}(\boldsymbol{\gamma})}

Then for any functor
Q:\mathsf{gCell}(\mathbb{X})(F_{0}x,F_{n}y)\to\mathsf{C}
, a cocone
\kappa:Q\Gamma_{\varphi,n}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{c}
with tip
c\in\mathsf{C}
is equivalent to a family of maps
\kappa(\alpha):Q(\Phi_{1}\psi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n}\psi_{n})\to c
indexed by objects of
\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}
such that if
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:\alpha\to\beta
is a morphism in
\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}
, then
\kappa(\beta)\circ Q(\Phi_{1}\gamma_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n}\gamma_{n})=\kappa(\alpha)
.

Using the fact that
\mathbb{X}
is locally cocomplete and
\mathbb{A}
is small,
so that the categories
\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}
are also small,
we can define
\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})(\varphi):=\colim_{\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}}\;\Gamma_{\varphi,n}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}
.
For an object
\beta:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime}\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
in
\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}
,
let
\lambda_{\beta}:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}^{\prime})\\
\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})(\varphi)\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

denote the associated cocone leg for the colimit.
To define
\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})
on a unary multicell
\sigma:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\varphi&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
note that for any
\alpha\in\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}
, as below left, we can use

n
-ary globular decompositions to obtain the essentially unique decomposition below right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varphi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\chi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

We can then define a cocone
\kappa(\sigma)
with leg
\kappa(\sigma)_{\alpha}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\delta}}{\lambda_{\beta}}

The essential uniqueness of the decomposition, and the fact that
\lambda
constitutes a cocone ensures that this definition is independent of the choice of decomposition, and also defines a cocone. By the universal property of local colimits (specifically property (wL2)), the cocone
\kappa(\sigma)
uniquely induces a multicell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\sigma)}

Uniqueness of these decompositions ensures that this definition is functorial in the multicells. Using these unique multicells we obtain our desired functor
\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi}):\mathbb{A}_{1}\to\mathbb{X}_{1}
defining a loose arrow
F_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}F_{n}
in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
. We will also denote this functor by
\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n}
. In other words, we’re defining the composite by the coend

\int^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\mathsf{gl}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{A}_{1}))}\mathsf{gCell}(\mathbb{A})(\boldsymbol{\varphi},-)\star(\Phi_{1}(\varphi_{1})\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n}(\varphi_{n}))

(7)

where
\star
denotes the tensoring of
\mathbb{X}_{1}
over
\mathsf{Set}
, which exists since
\mathbb{X}
is locally cocomplete.

Finally, in order to be a composition candidate we need a comparison multicell. The universal property of local colimits defining
\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})
on loose arrows and multicells, along with Lemma
7.6
, then allows us to define a globular multicell
\odot_{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}:(\Phi_{1}\cdots\Phi_{n})\Rightarrow\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})
, which at a globular
n
-ary multicell
\alpha:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\psi_{1}\cdots\psi_{n}\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
is given by the leg of the colimit cocone
\lambda_{\alpha}:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\Phi_{1}\psi_{1}\cdots\Phi_{n}\psi_{n}\\
\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})(\varphi)\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
.
∎

In order to prove Theorem
7.8
we will show that this multicell defined in the construction is (weakly) opcartesian. We’ll begin with proving the weak case:

Proof of the Weak Case for Theorem
7.8
.

Suppose
\mathbb{A}
is a small exponentiable VDC that has positive globular decompositions,
and
\mathbb{X}
is a large weakly locally cocomplete VDC with weak non-nullary composites.
Consider a multicell
\Gamma:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\Phi}&\\
f_{0}&&f_{1}\\
&\Psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
.
Then for any globular
n
-cell
\alpha:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}\\
\psi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
we obtain the multicell
\Gamma(\alpha):\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\varphi}&\\
f_{0}(\text{id}_{x})&&f_{1}(\text{id}_{y})\\
&\Psi\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

with the functoriality of pasting ensuring that this defines a cocone, as in property (wL2). Thus, by the universal property of the colimit we obtain a unique multicell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{0}(\text{id}_{x})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f_{1}(\text{id}_{y})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\Gamma^{\flat}(\text{id}_{\psi})}

through which the
\Gamma(\alpha)
factor as
\frac{\odot_{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}(\alpha)}{\Gamma^{\flat}(\text{id}_{\psi})}
. It remains to show that this definition of
\Gamma^{\flat}
is compatible with pasting from below, as in the proof of Lemma
7.6
. Observe that if we paste a unary multicell
\sigma:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\psi&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi^{\prime}&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
below our cocone defining
\Gamma^{\flat}(\text{id}_{\psi})
, then we obtain a new cocone given by the family of multicells
\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{\sigma}\right)=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha)}{\Psi\sigma}
.

By property (wL2) for local colimits, this cocone induces a unique multicell
\Gamma^{\flat}(\sigma):\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\psi)&\\
f_{0}(a)&&f_{1}(b)\\
&\Psi\psi^{\prime}&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

which the cocone legs factor through. Uniqueness then ensures that we have the pasting equalities

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}w}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\psi)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{F_{0}a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{F_{n}b}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\psi)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{f_{0}(a)}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{f_{1}(b)}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\psi)}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{f_{0}(\text{id}_{x})}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{f_{1}(\text{id}_{y})}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\psi^{\prime})}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{0}(\text{id}_{z})}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{1}(\text{id}_{w})}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\Psi\varphi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{G_{0}a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{G_{1}b}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\Psi\psi^{\prime}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\Psi\psi^{\prime}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\Psi\psi^{\prime}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{(\Phi_{1}\odot\cdots\odot\Phi_{n})(\sigma)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Gamma^{\flat}(\sigma)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Gamma^{\flat}(\text{id}_{\varphi})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Gamma^{\flat}(\text{id}_{\psi})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\Psi(\sigma)}}}

making
\Gamma^{\flat}
a well-defined unary multicell in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
. Further, by construction it follows that we have the desired equality of pastings in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{f_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{f_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\odot(\boldsymbol{\varphi})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{f_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{f_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\odot_{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma^{\flat}}}

Additionally, any other unary multicell in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
giving such a pasting equality is uniquely determined on globular multicells by the universal property of the colimit, so that by Lemma
7.6

\Gamma^{\flat}
is unique. Hence,
\odot_{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}
is weakly opcartesian.
∎

Finally, we prove the case for strong non-nullary composites.

Proof of the Strong Case for Theorem
7.8
.

Suppose
\mathbb{A}
is a small exponentiable VDC that has positive globular decompositions,
and
\mathbb{X}
is a large locally cocomplete VDC with non-nullary composites.
Consider a multicell
\Gamma:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&(\boldsymbol{\Omega},\boldsymbol{\Phi},\boldsymbol{\Xi})&\\
f_{0}&&f_{1}\\
&\Psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
. Then we need to find a unique multicell
\Gamma^{\flat}:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&(\boldsymbol{\Omega},\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi}),\boldsymbol{\Xi})&\\
f_{0}&&f_{1}\\
&\Psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

which it factors through via
\odot_{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}
. To define this multicell on a multicell
\delta:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&(\boldsymbol{\omega},\varphi,\boldsymbol{\xi})&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

in
\mathbb{A}
, we observe that for any globular multicell
\beta:{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}\\
\varphi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
, we can apply
\Gamma
to the composite
\frac{(\text{id}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}},\beta,\text{id}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}})}{\delta}
to obtain the multicell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ky}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}\boldsymbol{\omega}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{0}}}(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\Phi}\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{1}}}(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\left(\frac{\text{id}_{\boldsymbol{\omega}},\beta,\text{id}_{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}{\delta}\right)}

By functoriality of
\Gamma
this procedure defines a cocone, which by property (L2) factors through a unique multicell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{0}x^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{F_{n}y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ky}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{0}z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{G_{1}w}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}\boldsymbol{\omega}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\odot(\boldsymbol{\Phi})(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\Xi}}\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{f_{1}}}(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Psi\psi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\Gamma^{\flat}(\delta)}

Uniqueness along with Lemma

ensures that

.
