Examples and special cases

If
\mathbb{X}
has a discrete underlying category, and we are just interested in weak composites,
then we can drop the hypothesis that positive globular decompositions exist.

Proposition 7.10

(Weak Composites of Exponentials into Tightly Discrete VDCs)
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is a small exponentiable VDC and
\mathbb{X}
is a large locally cocomplete VDC with a discrete underlying tight category and weak
n
-ary composites for some
n\geq 2
,
then
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
also has weak
n
-ary composites.
If in addition
\mathbb{X}
has terminal underlying tight category and (weak)
0
-ary composites (i.e. (weak) units), then
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
also has (weak) units.

Proof.

The first claim follows exactly as in the proof of the weak case in Theorem
7.8
, where now
\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}
is replaced by the category
\mathsf{I}_{\varphi,n}^{\prime}
whose objects are all
n
-ary multicells in
\mathbb{A}
with loose target
\varphi
, and for which a morphism
\alpha\to\beta
consists of a sequence of unary multicells
(\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n})
such that
\alpha=\frac{\gamma_{1}\cdots\gamma_{n}}{\beta}
. The same argument carries through since under any VDF all multicells in
\mathbb{A}
become globular multicells in
\mathbb{X}
.

For the nullary composites in the case that
\mathbb{X}
is a multicategory with (weakly) representable unit, we write
\bullet
for the unique object in the underlying tight category and
I_{\bullet}:\bullet\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}\bullet
for its loose unit.
Then
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
also has terminal underlying tight category, and for the unique functor
!:\mathbb{A}_{0}\to\mathbb{X}_{0}
, we can define the (weak) unit

I_{!}:!\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}!
by sending every loose arrow
\varphi
to the (weak) unit
I_{\bullet}
, and sending every unary multicell to the identity
\text{id}_{I_{\bullet}}
.
Then we have a natural nullary multicell
\eta:!\Rightarrow I_{!}
given by sending any nullary multicell in
\mathbb{A}
to a fixed opcartesian nullary multicell
witnessing
I_{\bullet}
as the unit of
\bullet
in
\mathbb{X}
. Then the universal property of the (weak) opcartesian multicell for the unit in
\mathbb{X}
ensures that the nullary multicell
u

is (weakly) opcartesian as well, completing the proof.
∎

For instance, if
\mathsf{C}
is a cocomplete monoidal category, then the VDC
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(B\mathsf{C})
is locally cocomplete, with discrete tight category, and the
monoidal product provides composites. Thus exponentials of the form
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(B\mathsf{C})^{\mathbb{A}}
always have weak composites. Indeed,
it is enough here that
\mathsf{C}
has a
colax
monoidal structure; in fact we do not know whether
we can eliminate the “weak” hypothesis above.
This leads to interesting
examples of generalized Day convoluations for colax monoidal categories, as defined in
[
Gra25
]
,
which are dual to Leinster’s notion of unbiased lax monoidal categories in
[
Lei04
, Definition 3.1.1]
:

Example 7.11

(Colax Monoidal Convolution Structure)
.

If
(\mathsf{C},\otimes,I)
is a cocomplete colax monoidal category with
\otimes
preserving colimits in either variable, then for any small exponentiable VDC
\mathbb{A}
, the functor category
\mathsf{C}^{\mathbb{A}_{1}}
has a colax monoidal convolution structure induced by the weak representability of
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(B\mathsf{C})^{\mathbb{A}}
.
The colax monoidal structure is given on a sequence of functors
F_{1},...,F_{n}:\mathbb{A}_{1}\to\mathsf{C}
by the coend

\int^{(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})\in\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{1})}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{A})(\boldsymbol{\varphi},-)\star(F_{1}(\varphi_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{n}(\varphi_{n}))

(8)

where
\star
denotes the tensoring of
\mathsf{C}
over
\mathsf{Set}
, which exists since
\mathsf{C}
is cocomplete.

As an illustration, if
(\mathsf{C},\otimes,I)=(\mathsf{Set},\times,\{*\})=:\mathsf{Set}_{\times}
,
which we can view as the full sub VDC of
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
with only object the one element set
\{*\}
,
then for any pro-monoidal multicategory
\mathsf{M}
the exponential
(B\mathsf{Set}_{\times})^{B\mathsf{M}}
is always representable,
which is equivalent to giving a bi-cocontinuous monoidal structure on the functor category
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathsf{M}_{1},\mathsf{Set})
.
(Here we use Proposition
7.10
only for the units, and Theorem
7.14
for the strong representability.)
In particular, these observations along with Proposition
6.4
and characterization (6) in Theorem
5.11

imply that a multicategory
\mathsf{M}
is pro-monoidal if and only if the exponential
\mathsf{Set}_{\times}^{\mathsf{M}}
exists in
\mathsf{MultCat}
,
in which case it is necessarily representable, and hence uniquely determined by a bi-cocontinuous monoidal structure on
\mathsf{Fun}(\mathsf{M}_{1},\mathsf{Set})
.

7.3
Representability of VDCs of VDFs

Using Theorem
7.8
along with the following two lemmas, we will obtain sufficient conditions for

\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{X})
to be (weakly) representable using Theorem A.1 in
[
CS10
]

and the observation in Remark 6.4 of
[
Ark25
]
.
In personal communication, Arkor has indicated that he’s simultaneously developed some similar results, to appear in
a future version of
[
Ark25
]
.

Lemma 7.12

(Restrictions in Exponential)
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is a small exponentiable VDC and
\mathbb{X}
is a VDC with restrictions, then the VDC
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
also has restrictions which are created by evaluation at loose arrows.

Proof.

If
\Phi:F\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}G
is a map of spans of categories,

(F,\Phi,G):(\mathbb{A}_{0}\xleftarrow{}\mathbb{A}_{1}\xrightarrow{}\mathbb{A}_{0})\to(\mathbb{X}_{0}\xleftarrow{}\mathbb{X}_{1}\xrightarrow{}\mathbb{X}_{0})

corresponding to a loose arrow in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
, and
f:H\Rightarrow F
,
g:K\Rightarrow G
are natural transformations, then the restriction
\Phi(f,g):H\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}K
is determined at a loose arrow
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
in
\mathbb{A}
by a choice of cartesian multicell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ky}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gy}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(f,g)(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cart}}

so that
\Phi(f,g)(\varphi)=\Phi(\varphi)(f(a),g(b))
. For a unary multicell
\gamma:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\varphi&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
in
\mathbb{A}
,
\Phi(f,g)(\gamma)
is given using the unique factorization through the cartesian multicell below:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ky}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ky}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fx}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gy}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Hz}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kw}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fz}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gw}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fz}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gw}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(f,g)(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(a)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g(b)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(f,g)(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Ha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Kb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Gb}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(f,g)(\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{f(c)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g(d)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Phi(\psi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cart}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists!\Phi(f,g)(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi(\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{cart}}

Uniqueness ensures that this is functorial in
\gamma
, and hence
\Phi(f,g)
defines a loose arrow in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
, with the associated cartesian multicell induced by the pointwise cartesian multicells.
∎

Lemma 7.13

(Local Colimits in Exponential)
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is a small exponentiable VDC with positive globular decompositions, and
\mathbb{X}
is a large VDC with weak non-nullary composites which has local
\mathsf{I}
-colimits, then
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
also has local
\mathsf{I}
-colimits, and they are created by evaluation at loose arrows.

Proof.

Fix functors
F,G:\mathbb{A}_{0}\to\mathbb{X}_{0}
and a diagram
D:\mathsf{I}\to(\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}})_{1}(F,G)
, where the objects in
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}})_{1}(F,G)
are maps of spans

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{A}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{X}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

and morphisms are natural transformations
\Phi\Rightarrow\Psi
whose components are all globular unary multicells. Let
\colim_{\mathsf{I}}\,D:\mathbb{A}_{1}\to\mathbb{X}_{1}
denote the colimit in the functor category, which exists since for each loose arrow
\varphi:a\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}b
in
\mathbb{A}
, the resulting functor
D(-,\varphi):\mathsf{I}\to\mathbb{X}_{1}(Fa,Gb)
lands in a category with
\mathsf{I}
-colimits. Since
\colim_{\mathsf{I}}\,D
lies in
(\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}})_{1}(F,G)
, it is also a colimit there. Further, property (wL2) is satisfied since the local
\mathsf{I}
-colimits in
\mathbb{X}
satisfy (wL2), and the colimit
\colim_{\mathsf{I}}\,D
is computed pointwise. Finally, since weak composites in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
can be constructed as a colimit of composites in
\mathbb{X}
, as in Theorem
7.8
, the local
\mathsf{I}
-colimits in
\mathbb{X}^{\mathbb{A}}
are preserved under weak composition by loose arrows on either side due to the fact that this is the case in
\mathbb{X}
and colimits commute.
∎

Recall that Theorem A.1 in
[
CS10
]
says that if
\mathbb{X}
is a representable VDC with restrictions and local coequalizers,
then
\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}(\mathbb{X})
is also representable. Note that here we do not need for
\mathbb{X}
to have restrictions, as we just need property (wL2)
for local coequalizers. However, as noted in
[
CS10
]
, in practice most examples of such
\mathbb{X}
satisfy (wL2) due to having restrictions.
Additionally, Remark 6.4 of
[
Ark25
]
says that we can weaken the assumptions further by only requiring

\mathbb{X}
to have non-nullary composites and local reflexive coequalizers, since these are the only things used in the proof of Theorem A.1 in
[
CS10
]
.
Putting Theorem
7.8
together with Lemma
7.12
and Lemma
7.13
, we can now obtain our main representability result:

Theorem 7.14

(Representability of
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{X})
)
.

If
\mathbb{A}
is a small exponentiable VDC with positive globular decompositions, and

\mathbb{X}
is a VDC with (weak) non-nullary composites and local colimits, then

\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{X})
is a (weakly) representable unital VDC which is locally cocomplete.
Further, if
\mathbb{X}
has restrictions, so does
\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathbb{A},\mathbb{X})
.

Combining these results with Lemma
7.5
and Lemma
5.17
we obtain the following embedding result.

Corollary 7.15
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be an arbitrary VDC.
Then the Yoneda embedding
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}:\mathbb{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}
embeds
\mathbb{D}
into a large VDC which is locally cocomplete, has restrictions, and has non-nullary composites.
If
\mathbb{D}
admits loose units, then a choice of such units corresponds to an extension of
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}
to a unital embedding
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}^{\prime}:\mathbb{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}},\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan})
into a large locally cocomplete equipment.

Using Proposition
6.9
it follows that for any unital VDC
\mathbb{D}
, a choice of units provides a unital embedding

\rho_{\mathbb{D}}^{\prime}:\mathbb{D}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}},\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan})\cong\mathbb{V}\mathsf{df}_{n}(\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}},\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof})

(9)

We’ll explore the universal properties satisfied by these embeddings in future work.

Appendix A
Exponentiable Virtual Double Functors

In this section we investigate exponentiable morphisms between VDCs. The arguments in this section are heavily inspired by the section on powerful morphisms in Street and Verity’s paper on factorization and torsors
[
SV10
]
. In particular, we use the fact that a morphism
p:E\to B
in a finitely complete category
\mathsf{E}
is powerful, or exponentiable, if and only if the pullback functor
-\times_{B}E:\mathsf{E}_{/B}\to\mathsf{E}
admits a right adjoint. Since we will be considering the case where
\mathsf{E}=\mathsf{Vdc}
, Theorem
3.2
along with Proposition
3.4
imply that such an adjoint exists if and only if the functor
-\times_{B}E
preserves the colimiting cones described in Corollary
3.5
.

Before generalizing our exponentiability results, we’ll begin by explicating what a right adjoint to
-\times_{B}E
would do on objects if it existed. Note that if
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
is an exponentiable VDF, so that we have a right adjoint
\Pi_{P}(-\times\mathbb{D}):\mathsf{Vdc}\to\mathsf{Vdc}_{/\mathbb{E}}
, then for a VDC
\mathbb{C}
, the structure of
s_{\mathbb{C}}:\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{E}
can be determined by the adjunction

\mathsf{Vdc}_{/\mathbb{E}}(Q:\mathbb{A}\to\mathbb{E},s_{\mathbb{C}})\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{A}\times_{Q,\mathbb{E},P}\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})

Thus, in order to use this adjunction to understand the structure of
\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D})
we must explicate what the VDC
\mathbb{A}\times_{Q,\mathbb{E},P}\mathbb{D}
looks like for the various free walking VDCs.

Observe that if
x:\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}\to\mathbb{E}
is an object in
\mathbb{E}
, then since products with
\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}
give underlying tight categories, the fiber
P^{-1}(x)=\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}\times_{x,\mathbb{E},P}\mathbb{D}
is precisely the VDC
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(P_{0}^{-1}(x))
. Similarly, if
a:x\to x^{\prime}
is a tight arrow in
\mathbb{E}
, corresponding to a VDF
a:\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}\to\mathbb{E}
, then
P^{-1}(a)=\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}\times_{a,\mathbb{E},P}\mathbb{D}
can be identified with the collage of the fibers

\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(P_{0}^{-1}(x)+_{P_{0}^{-1}(a)}P_{0}^{-1}(x^{\prime}))

That is,
P^{-1}(a)
consists of disjoint copies of the categories
P_{0}^{-1}(x)
and
P_{0}^{-1}(x^{\prime})
, along with for each tight arrow
b:y\to y^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{D}
with
P(b)=a
, a tight morphism
(t,b):(0,y)\to(1,y^{\prime})
.

Next, if
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x^{\prime}
is a loose arrow in
\mathbb{E}
, corresponding to a VDF
\varphi:\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\to\mathbb{E}
, then
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\times_{\varphi,\mathbb{E},P}\mathbb{D}=P^{-1}(\varphi)
contains disjoint copies of the categories
P_{0}^{-1}(x)
and
P_{0}^{-1}(x^{\prime})
. Additionally, for each loose arrow
\psi:y\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{D}
with
P(\psi)=\varphi
,
P^{-1}(\varphi)
has a loose arrow
(\ell,\psi):(0,y)\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}(1,y^{\prime})
, and for each unary multicell
\alpha
in
\mathbb{D}
lying over
\text{id}_{\varphi}
,
P^{-1}(\varphi)
has a unary multicell
(\text{id}_{\ell},\alpha)
. In particular, the data in
P^{-1}(\varphi)
is determined by the subspan of categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}^{-1}(x)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}^{-1}(\varphi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}^{-1}(x^{\prime})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

Finally, for a multicell
\alpha:\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&\boldsymbol{\varphi}&\\
a&&b\\
&\psi&\end{smallmatrix}\right)

in
\mathbb{E}
, which can be given by a map
\alpha:\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}\to\mathbb{E}
,
P^{-1}(\alpha)
contains
P^{-1}(a),P^{-1}(b),P^{-1}(\varphi_{1}),\ldots,P^{-1}(\varphi_{n})
, and
P^{-1}(\psi)
as sub-VDCs, along with a multicell for each multicell of
\mathbb{D}
lying over
\alpha
.

Relativizing Proposition
4.1
we can obtain an analogous result for
\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{E}
.
In the following proposition, if
\mathsf{P}\to\mathsf{Q}
is a map of profunctors, and if
\alpha
is a heteromorphism in
\mathsf{Q}
, we write
\mathsf{P}_{/\alpha}
for the pullback in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}_{1}
, which is a profunctor between fiber categories whose heteromorphisms are the heteromorphisms in
\mathsf{P}
lying over
\alpha
.

Proposition A.1

(Elements of the exponential dependent product VDC)
.

If
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
is a VDF and
\mathbb{C}
is a VDC such that the dependent product
s_{\mathbb{C}}:\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D})\to\mathbb{E}
exists, then the VDC
\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D})
consists of the following data:

1.

For
x\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{E})
, objects
F\in\mathsf{Ob}(\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D}))_{/x}\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{O}\mathsf{b}\times_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
over
x
correspond to functors
F:P_{0}^{-1}(x)\to\mathbb{C}_{0}
.

2.

For
a:x\to x^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{E}
, tight arrows
f\in\mathsf{Arr_{T}}(\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D}))_{/a}\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}\times_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
over
a
correspond to functors
\alpha:P_{0}^{-1}(x)+_{P_{0}^{-1}(a)}P_{0}^{-1}(x^{\prime})\to\mathbb{C}_{0}
extending those on objects.

3.

For
\varphi:x\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{E}
, Loose arrows
\Phi\in\mathsf{Loose}(\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D}))_{/\varphi}\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{oose}\times_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C})
over
\varphi
correspond to maps of spans of categories

(P_{0}^{-1}(x)\leftarrow P_{1}^{-1}(\varphi)\rightarrow P_{0}^{-1}(x^{\prime}))\to(\mathbb{C}_{0}\leftarrow\mathbb{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}_{0})

4.

For a multicell
\alpha
in
\mathbb{E}
, multicells
\Gamma\in\mathsf{Sq}_{n}(\Pi_{P}(\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{D}))_{/\alpha}\cong\mathsf{Vdc}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{q}_{n}\times_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{D},\mathbb{C}),
over
\alpha
are given by a map of profunctors
\mathsf{MCell}_{n}(\mathbb{D})_{/\alpha}\to\partial^{*}\mathsf{MCell}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
over
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{2},
where

\partial:\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathbb{D}_{1})_{/s(\alpha)}^{op}\times(\mathbb{D}_{1})_{/t(\alpha)}\to\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathbb{C}_{1})^{op}\times\mathbb{C}_{1}
is the functor which together with the natural transformations putting
\mathsf{MCell}_{n}(\mathbb{D})_{/\alpha}
over
\mathbb{C}_{0}^{2}
determines the boundary data of
\Gamma.

To begin characterizing exponentiable VDC morphisms, we begin by relativizing Definition
4.9
.
Unlike in the absolute case, we’ll need to consider factorizations of tight arrows as well as decompositions of multicells.
For a tight arrow
a
in a VDC
\mathbb{D}
, we will write
\mathsf{Fact}_{n}(\mathbb{D};a)
for the category whose objects
are factorizations of
a
into
n
composable tight arrows, and whose morphisms are maps of such factorizations.

Definition A.2
.

We say a VDF
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
lifts decompositions essentially uniquely if the following hold:

(D1)

for every tight arrow
a
in
\mathbb{D}
, every integer
n\geq 2
, and every factorization
\mathbf{b}\in\mathsf{Fact}_{n}(\mathbb{E};P(a))
, the pullback
\mathsf{Fact}_{n}(\mathbb{D};a)\times_{\mathsf{Fact}_{n}(\mathbb{E};P(a))}\{\mathbf{b}\}
is (nonempty and) connected.

(D2)

for every multicell
\alpha
in
\mathbb{D}
, every integer
n\geq 2
, and every decomposition
\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{8.6385pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{8.6385pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\gamma_{1}\end{array}\in\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{E};P(\alpha))
, the pullback
\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{D};\alpha)\times_{\mathsf{Paste}_{n}(\mathbb{E};P(\alpha))}\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{n}\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{8.6385pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\vdots\\[-8.0pt]
\rule{8.6385pt}{0.4pt}\\[-8.0pt]
\scriptscriptstyle\gamma_{1}\end{array}\right\}
is (nonempty and) connected.

∎

Explication A.3

(Decomposition Lifting)
.

Condition (D1) in decomposition lifting for a VDF
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
says that the underlying
functor on tight categories is a Conduché fibration, or equivalently that for each tight arrow
a
in
\mathbb{D}
,
every factorization of its image
P(a)
in
\mathbb{E}
lifts to an essentially unique factorization of
a
in
\mathbb{D}
,
where the maps appearing in the associativity relation are mapped to identities in
\mathbb{E}
.
Similarly, condition (D2) in decomposition lifting says that any multicell in
\mathbb{D}
, below upper left, lying over a multicell in
\mathbb{E}
which admits a decomposition, below right, also admits a decomposition, as below bottom left:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(x_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(x_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}\ni}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\in\mathbb{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(y_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(y_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(x_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(x_{k_{m}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exists}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(y_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(y_{1})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{P(\boldsymbol{\varphi})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{P(a)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{P(b)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{P(\psi)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{0}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{m}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{P(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1})}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{P(\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{m})}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{1}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{0}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{m}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{1}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{{P(\psi)}}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P(\alpha)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{m}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\delta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

Further, any two such decompositions are equivalent via unary multicells lying over identity unary multicells. That is, if we have two decompositions, as on the left and right below, we get a zig-zag of multicells (of length possibly
>1
, though we just depict the length
1
case) given by pulling out unary multicells in fibers from either the top or bottom:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{0}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{m}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{k_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{0}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{w_{m}^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{0}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{m}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{1}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{0}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{m}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{0}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{m}^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{0}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{c_{m}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{0}\circ c_{0}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\boldsymbol{\varphi}_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{e_{m}\circ c_{m}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{1}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{0}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{m}^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{1}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{0}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\chi_{m}^{\prime\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{d_{1}^{\prime\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{m}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\delta^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta_{m}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\frac{\beta_{1}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\frac{\beta_{m}^{\prime}}{\sigma_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\delta^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\delta^{\prime\prime}}}

where
P(\sigma_{i})=\text{id}_{\chi_{i}}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
.

The arguments in Lemma
4.10
and Corollary
4.11
extend directly to the relative setting to give the following:

Corollary A.4
.

A VDF
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
lifts decompositions if and only if it lifts 2-layer decompositions.

Under this reduction the condition for lifting decompositions can be phrased in terms of coend isomorphisms. Namely,
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
lifts decompositions if and only if for each multicell
\alpha\in\mathbb{E}
with decomposition
\alpha=\frac{\boldsymbol{\beta}}{\gamma}
, the composition map

\int^{\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in P^{-1}(s(\gamma))}\mathsf{fc}(\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D}))_{/\boldsymbol{\beta}}(-,\boldsymbol{\varphi})\times\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})_{/\gamma}(\boldsymbol{\varphi},-)\to\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})_{/\alpha}(\mu_{\mathsf{fc}}(-),-)

is an isomorphism, and for each tight arrow
a\in\mathbb{E}
with decomposition
a=c\circ b
, the composition map

\int^{y\in P^{-1}(s(c))}\mathsf{Arr_{T}}(\mathbb{D})_{/b}(-,y)\times\mathsf{Arr_{T}}(\mathbb{D})_{/c}(y,-)\to\mathsf{Arr_{T}}(\mathbb{D})_{/a}(-,-)

is an isomorphism. Note that this second condition is precisely the condition that the functor on underlying tight categories
P_{0}
is a Conduché fibration, which is to say it is an exponentiable map of categories.

The last ingredient we need to relativize the argument in Theorem
4.14
is a description of the colimits for the cocones.

Remark A.5

(Relative Colimits for Sequences of Tight Arrows)
.

For a sequence of tight arrows
\boldsymbol{a}:x_{0}\to x_{n}
in
\mathbb{E}
, corresponding to a VDF
\boldsymbol{a}:\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}[n]\to\mathbb{E}
, we obtain the diagram

\mathsf{el}\downarrow\mathbb{T}\mathsf{ight}[n]\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{a}}\mathsf{Vdc}_{/\mathbb{E}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{D}\times_{\mathbb{E}}-}\mathsf{Vdc}

that we want to understand the colimit of. The resulting diagram is given by the wide span

P^{-1}(a_{1})\xleftarrow{t}P^{-1}(x_{1})\xrightarrow{s}\cdots\xleftarrow{t}P^{-1}(x_{n-1})\xrightarrow{s}P^{-1}(a_{n})

where each object lies in the image of
\mathbb{T}\mathsf{}(-)
, so we can compute this colimit in
\mathsf{Cat}
. The objects in the colimit are the objects in
\coprod_{i=0}^{n}P^{-1}(x_{i})
, while a morphism between an object
y\in P^{-1}(x_{i})
and
z\in P^{-1}(x_{j})
, for
i<j
, is an equivalence class of sequences of morphisms
(y,x_{i})\to(y_{1},x_{i+1})\to\cdots\to(z,x_{j})
. As in Remark
4.13
, associativity induces the equivalence relation, but now for a sequence of morphisms
(y,x_{i})\to\cdots\to(z,x_{j})
we are only able to slide arrows along identities, such as
\text{id}_{x_{k}}
, if they lie in the fiber
P^{-1}(x_{k})
.

Remark A.6

(Relative Colimits for Trees)
.

Let
T
be a tree with
m
-leaves, and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{E}
be an
m
-ary multicell together with a decomposition
\beta:\mathbb{J}(T)\to\mathbb{E}
relative to the tree
\alpha
. Then we obtain the associated diagram

J(T)=\mathsf{el}\downarrow\mathbb{J}(T)\xrightarrow{\beta}\mathsf{Vdc}_{/\mathbb{E}}\xrightarrow{\mathbb{D}\times_{\mathbb{E}}-}\mathsf{Vdc}

which we want to understand the colimit of. We can express
\colim_{i:\mathbb{c}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{J}(T)}(\mathbb{D}\times_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{c})
as the colimit of a diagram obtained from the tree
T
by replacing the products in Remark
4.13
with pullbacks, or equivalently products in
\mathsf{Vdc}_{/\mathbb{E}}
before forgetting to
\mathsf{Vdc}
. In particular, we get that objects and loose arrows in the colimit are pairs of such in
\mathbb{D}
and
\mathbb{J}(T)
, such that the component in
\mathbb{D}
must live in the appropriate fiber of
P
. Further, tight arrows are as described in Remark
A.5
.

Finally, a multicell in the colimit corresponds to an equivalence class of tuples of a subtree
T^{\prime}\subseteq T
of
T
, together with for each non-leaf node in
T^{\prime}
a multicell in
\mathbb{D}
living in the appropriate fiber of
P
, such that the multicells have compatible boundaries. The equivalence relation on the tuples is as in Remark
4.13
, except that we can only move sequences of unary multicells that lie over identities in
\mathbb{E}
.

With these preliminaries, the proof technique for the absolute case in Theorem
4.14
extends to the relative case. Indeed, extending the argument for Theorem
4.14
to the relative context using the descriptions of the colimits in Remarks
A.5
and
A.6
, we see that Corollary
3.5
implies a VDF
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
is exponentiable if and only if it lifts decompositions into 2 and 3 layers essentially uniquely. Corollary
A.4
then ensures that this is equivalent to
P
lifting all decompositions essentially uniquely.

Theorem A.7

(Characterizing Exponentiable VDFs)
.

A VDF
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
is exponentiable if and only if it lifts decompositions essentially uniquely.

Now, from Corollary 2.6 of
[
SV10
]
we have that all isomorphisms of VDCs are exponentiable (in fact all equivalences of VDCs are exponentiable), all composites of exponentiable morphisms are exponentiable, and arbitrary pullbacks of exponentiable morphisms are exponentiable. In particular, as discussed previously, products of exponentiable VDCs are exponentiable, and the fibers of exponentiable morphisms are exponentiable VDCs. In fact, for any exponentiable morphism
P:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
, and any VDF
F:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{E}
out of an exponentiable VDC, the fiber product
\mathbb{C}\times_{\mathbb{E}}\mathbb{D}
is exponentiable. Additionally, the characterization in Theorem
A.7
implies that VDC inclusions which are full with respect to decompositions of multicells are also exponentiable.

Generalizing the case of discrete opcartesian fibrations in
[
FL25
]
, Theorem
A.7
also implies that opcartesian fibrations of VDCs are exponentiable, though as exploring exponentiable morphisms of VDCs is not the central goal of the current work, we will not prove this here.

We’ll conclude by observing that
the loose embedding does not reflect exponentiable morphisms.

Proposition A.8
.

If
F:\mathsf{C}\to\mathsf{D}
is a functor between categories,
then
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(F):\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{C})\to\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{D})
is an exponentiable VDF.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(F)_{0}
is a functor from a discrete category,
it is necessarily a Conduché fibration.
Further, the only multicells in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{C})
and
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{D})
are unique
opcartesian multicells witnessing the strict composites in
\mathsf{C}
and
\mathsf{D}
.
Decompositions of such multicells correspond to composing a sequence
of arrows by first composing certain subsequences, and then composing the result.
In particular, all such decompositions lift uniquely to
\mathsf{C}
,
so
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(F)
is an exponentiable VDF.
∎

Appendix B
VDCs via Categories of Operators

For extending the main exponentiability proofs in this article to other contexts in higher category theory, it will be beneficial to recontextualize the data of a VDC. This reframing is motivated by the category of operators perspective for operads (i.e. multicategories) introduced by May and Thomason in
[
MT78
]
which has found numerous applications in homotopy theory, and especially higher algebra, as well as the theory of algebraic patterns introduced in
[
CH21
]
.
In the context of
\infty
-categories, this approach to VDCs has also been used to discuss enrichment in
\infty
-categories
[
GH15
]
.

This algebraic patterns perspective was used in
[
BLR26
]

simultaneously to the development of the current work to characterize exponentiable morphisms of algebrads
for rigid algebraic patterns in the context of
\infty
-categories.
From
[
CH21
]
such algebraic patterns arise from parametric right adjoint monads
T
on pre-sheaf categories,
while the algebrads correspond to
T
-multicategories.
Note that Proposition
A.8
shows that a
classification of exponentiable morphisms of
T
-multicategories
cannot be used to classify exponentiable morphisms of
T
-algebras in general.
The work below shows that our characterization of exponentiable VDCs is equivalent to this
characterization in terms of algebraic patterns in the context of 1-categories,
where the condition that the
\infty
-category of decompositions is weakly contractible
is replaced by the condition that the
1
-category of decompositions is connected.

In order to perform this reframing we use the display category equivalence observed by Bénabou in
[
Bén72
]
, and phrased in terms of lax functors in
[
PA97
]
. Specifically, the display category equivalence can be phrased in terms of VDCs as an equivalence

\mathsf{Cat}_{/\mathsf{A}}\simeq\mathbf{Vdc}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{A})^{op_{l}},\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan})

(10)

for every category
\mathsf{A}
, where on the right we have the category of VDFs
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{A})^{op_{l}}\to\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
and tight transformations between them. Here the superscript
op_{l}
indicates that we are reversing the direction of loose arrows in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{A})
. Since
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{A})^{op_{l}}
is a unital VDC, the adjunction
U\dashv\mathbb{M}\mathsf{od}
gives an isomorphism
\mathbf{Vdc}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{A})^{op_{l}},\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan})\cong\mathbf{Vdc}_{n}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{A})^{op_{l}},\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof})
, which will be used to describe VDCs as normal VDFs into
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
preserving certain opcartesian multicells.

To encode the data of a VDC
\mathbb{D}
we take
\mathsf{A}
in the above equivalence to be the opposite of the simplex category
\mathsf{\Delta}
. Recall that
\mathsf{\Delta}
has objects given by the finite (nonempty) linearly ordered sets
[n]:=\{0<1<\cdots<n\}
for each natural number
n\geq 0
, and has morphisms given by order preserving maps
f:[n]\to[m]
. The category
\mathsf{\Delta}
admits an
(\mathsf{epi},\mathsf{mono})
strict factorization system, where for each
n\geq 0
and each
0\leq i\leq n
, we will write
\sigma^{i}:[n+1]\to[n]
for the unique surjective order preserving map such that the pre-image of
i
has two elements, and we will write
\delta^{i}:[n-1]\to[n]
for the unique injective order preserving map whose image doesn’t contain
i
. For a subset
T\subseteq[n]
, we will also write
\delta^{T}:[|T|]\to[n]
for the unique injective order preserving map whose image is
T
.

The simplex category also comes equipped with another strict factorization system
(\mathsf{act},\mathsf{inrt})
where the left class
\mathsf{act}
consists of those morphisms that preserve top and bottom elements, and the right class
\mathsf{inrt}
consists of all interval inclusions
\delta^{[i,i+k]}:[k]\hookrightarrow[n]
, for
[i,i+k]\subseteq[n]
(c.f.
[
CH21
, Example 3.3]
). We refer to morphisms in
\mathsf{act}
as
active
and morphisms in
\mathsf{inrt}
as
inert
. We can equivalently describe the data of an active morphism
f:[n]\to[m]
in terms of a partition
m=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}
, for integers
k_{1},...,k_{n}\geq 0
, where the correspondence is given by defining
k_{i}=f(i)-f(i-1)
. For each
n\geq 0
, we will write
a_{n}:[1]\to[n]
for the unique active morphism from
[1]
to
[n]
. Using this data we can now define the category of operators construction for VDCs.

Construction B.1

(Category of Operators for a VDC)
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a VDC. We define the
category of operators
of
\mathbb{D}
to be a category
\mathsf{D}
together with a functor
p_{\mathbb{D}}:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
given by the following data:

1.

An object in
\mathsf{D}
is a pair of an object
[n]\in\mathsf{\Delta}
together with a sequence of loose arrows
(\boldsymbol{\varphi}:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x_{n})\in\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
. When
[n]=[0]
,
\mathsf{fc}_{0}(\mathbb{D}_{1})=\mathsf{Ob}(\mathbb{D})
consists of the objects of
\mathbb{D}
.

2.

A morphism
([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi}:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x_{n})\to([m],\boldsymbol{\psi}:y_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y_{m})
in
\mathsf{D}
consists of a pair of a morphism
\sigma:[m]\to[n]
in
\mathsf{\Delta}
,
together with a chain of compatible multicells
\boldsymbol{\chi}=(\chi_{1}\cdots\chi_{m})
in
\mathbb{D}
where
\chi_{i}
has loose target
\psi_{i}:y_{i-1}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y_{i}
and
loose source
(\varphi_{\sigma(i-1)+1}\cdots\varphi_{\sigma(i)}):x_{\sigma(i-1)}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x_{\sigma(i)}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\sigma(i-1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\sigma(i-1)+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\sigma(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{i}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{i-1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma(i-1)+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma(i-1)+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma(i)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

When
[m]=[0]
, so that
\boldsymbol{\psi}
corresponds to an object
y
in
\mathbb{D}
,
\boldsymbol{\chi}
corresponds to a tight arrow
x_{\sigma(0)}\to y
.

Composition is given by composition in
\mathsf{\Delta}
and composition of multicells in
\mathbb{D}
, while identity morphisms
([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})\to([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})
are given by pairs
(\text{id}_{[n]},\text{id}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})
of an identity morphism in
\mathsf{\Delta}
and a chain of identity multicells in
\mathbb{D}
. The functor
p_{\mathbb{D}}:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
is the natural projection onto the first coordinate.

Note that the fiber
\mathsf{D}_{n}:=p_{\mathbb{D}}^{-1}([n])
is isomorphic to the category of length
n
-chains of loose arrows in
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathsf{fc}_{n}(\mathbb{D}_{1})
, when
n\geq 1
, and is isomorphic to the underlying tight category for
\mathbb{D}
when
n=0
.

Given a VDF
F:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
, we have a natural functor
p_{F}:\mathsf{D}\to\mathsf{E}
over
\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
given on a pair
([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})
by
([n],F\boldsymbol{\varphi})
, and given on a morphism
(f,\boldsymbol{\chi}):([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})\to([m],\boldsymbol{\psi})
by
(f,F\boldsymbol{\chi})
. Since
F
is a VDF, the definition of identities and composites in
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{E}
imply that
p_{F}
is indeed a functor, and further this assignment extends to a functor
\mathsf{Vdc}\to\mathsf{Cat}_{/\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}}
. We will now describe the essential image of this functor.

Proposition B.2

(VDCs as Operator Categories over the Simplex)
.

A functor
q:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
is isomorphic to the category of operators of a VDC if and only if

1.

q
admits a functorial choice of cocartesian lifts of inert morphisms in
\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
;

2.

For each integer
n\geq 0
, the functor

(\delta^{[0,1]}_{*},\ldots,\delta^{[n-1,n]}_{*}):\mathsf{A}_{n}\to\underbrace{\mathsf{A}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{A}_{0}}\cdots\times_{\mathsf{A}_{0}}\mathsf{A}_{1}}_{n}

which is induced by the cocartesian lifts of the inert morphisms
\delta^{[i,i+1]}:[1]\cong[i,i+1]\subseteq[n]
and
\delta^{\{i\}}:[0]\cong\{i\}\subseteq[n]
, is an isomorphism;

3.

For each
\sigma:[m]\to[n]
in
\mathsf{\Delta}
, and each
x\in\mathsf{A}_{n},y\in\mathsf{A}_{m}
, the function

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{A}_{\sigma}(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{A}_{\sigma\delta^{[0,1]}}(x,\delta^{[0,1]}_{*}y)\underset{\mathsf{A}_{\sigma\delta^{\{1\}}}(x,\delta^{\{1\}}_{*}y)}{\times}\cdots\underset{\mathsf{A}_{\sigma\delta^{\{m-1\}}}(x,\delta^{\{m-1\}}_{*}y)}{\times}\mathsf{A}_{\sigma\delta^{[m-1,m]}}(x,\delta^{[m-1,m]}_{*}y)}}

induced by post-composing with the cocartesian lifts is a bijection, where the subscript on
\mathsf{A}
indicates what morphism in
\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
the morphisms in
\mathsf{A}
are lying over.

Before proving the proposition let’s explicate the three conditions appearing in the statement. First,
q
admitting cocartesian lifts of an inert morphism
i:[m]\cong[j,j+m]\subseteq[n]
implies that we get a functor
i_{*}:\mathsf{A}_{n}\to\mathsf{A}_{m}
, which will play the role of projecting a sequence of loose arrows onto a subsequence, or an object in the sequence. Additionally, the first condition says that these functors can be chosen in such a way that if
[m]\xrightarrow{i}[n]\xrightarrow{j}[k]
is a composable pair of inert morphisms, then
i_{*}j_{*}=(ji)_{*}:\mathsf{A}_{k}\to\mathsf{A}_{m}
, encoding the fact that projections should be strictly functorial. The second condition says that these cocartesian lifts do actually correspond to projections on the level of fibers, and
\mathsf{A}_{n}
corresponds to a limit over these projections. Finally, the third condition says that under our identification of the fibers of
\mathsf{A}
with the limit of the projections, a morphism
f:a\to b
in
\mathsf{A}
lying over
\sigma:[m]\to[n]
is equivalent to a sequence of compatible morphisms whose target is always an object either of
\mathsf{A}_{1}
, or
\mathsf{A}_{0}
if
m=0
.

Proof of
B.2
.

First, let’s show that the category of operators for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
satisfies conditions (1) through (3).

1.

If
i:[k]\cong[l,l+k]\subseteq[n]
is an inert morphism, then we have a natural functor

i_{*}:\mathsf{D}_{n}\to\mathsf{D}_{k}
given by projecting from a sequence of loose arrows

(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})
(resp. multicells
(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n})
) to the subsequence

(\varphi_{l+1}\cdots\varphi_{l+k})
(resp.
(\alpha_{l+1}\cdots\alpha_{l+k})
).
For a sequence of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\varphi}=(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n})
in
\mathbb{D}
,
the chosen cocartesian lift is then the morphism

(i,(\text{id}_{\varphi_{l+1}}\cdots\text{id}_{\varphi_{l+k}})):([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})\to([k],(\varphi_{l+1}\cdots\varphi_{l+k}))
.
This is cocartesian since any other morphism
(i\circ g,\boldsymbol{\alpha}):([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})\to([m],\boldsymbol{\psi})

factors uniquely as
(i,(\text{id}_{\varphi_{l+1}}\cdots\text{id}_{\varphi_{l+k}}))
followed by

(g,\boldsymbol{\alpha}):([k],(\varphi_{l+1}\cdots\varphi_{l+k}))\to([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})
. Further, this assignment on inert morphisms is functorial, proving (1).

2.

Since the functors
i_{*}
are defined to be projections, (2) follows.

3.

Similarly, the functors
i_{*}
being the natural projections also implies (3).

Next, suppose
q:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
is a functor satisfying properties (1)-(3), and write
(-)_{*}
for the functorial choice of cocartesian lifts for inert morphisms.
In particular, this choice fixes an isomorphism
\Phi_{n}:=(\delta^{[0,1]}_{*},\ldots,\delta^{[n-1,n]}_{*}):\mathsf{A}_{n}\to\mathsf{A}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{A}_{0}}\cdots\times_{\mathsf{A}_{0}}\mathsf{A}_{1}

for each integer
n\geq 0
, as well as a natural isomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{A}_{\sigma}(x,y)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{A}_{\sigma\delta^{[0,1]}}(x,\delta^{[0,1]}_{*}y)\underset{\mathsf{A}_{\sigma\delta^{\{1\}}}}{\times}(x,\delta^{\{1\}}_{*}y)\cdots\underset{\mathsf{A}_{\sigma\delta^{\{m-1\}}}}{\times}\mathsf{A}_{\sigma\delta^{[m-1,m]}}(x,\delta^{[m-1,m]}_{*}y)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi^{\sigma}}

for each morphism
\sigma:[m]\to[n]
in
\mathsf{\Delta}
. Then we define a VDC
\mathbb{A}
consisting of the following data:

1.

The underlying tight category of
\mathbb{A}
is the fiber category
\mathsf{A}_{0}
.

2.

The loose arrows of
\mathbb{A}
are the objects of the fiber category
\mathsf{A}_{1}
.

3.

For
n\geq 0
, the set of
n
-ary multicells with loose source
\boldsymbol{\varphi}:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x_{n}
and loose target
\psi:y_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y_{1}
is given by
\mathsf{A}_{a_{n}}(\Phi_{n}^{-1}(\varphi_{1}\cdots\varphi_{n}),\psi)
, while post composing with
\delta^{\{0\}}_{*},\delta^{\{1\}}_{*}:\mathsf{A}_{1}\to\mathsf{A}_{0}
serve as the tight source and target maps for the
n
-ary multicells.

4.

For a sequence of compatible multicells
(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n})
, where
\alpha_{i}
is
k_{i}
-ary, and an
n
-ary multicell
\beta
whose loose source is given by the loose targets of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
, their composite is defined as

\dfrac{(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n})}{\beta}:=\beta\circ(\Psi^{\tau_{(k_{i})}})^{-1}(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n})

where
\tau_{(k_{i})}:[n]\to[k_{1}+\cdots+k_{n}]
is given by
j\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{j}k_{j}
.

The unitality and associativity axioms for composition come from the associated axioms for
\mathsf{A}
. Thus,
\mathbb{A}
is a VDC, and by construction the operator category for
\mathbb{A}
is naturally isomorphic to the originally category
\mathsf{A}
over
\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
.
∎

From
[
CH21
, Lemma 9.10]
the active-inert factorization system on
\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
extends to an active-inert orthogonal factorization system on
\mathsf{D}
, where the active morphisms are those lying over active morphisms in
\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
, and the inert morphisms are the cocartesian lifts of inert morphisms in
\mathsf{\Delta}^{op}
.
Explicitly, an active morphism
(f,\boldsymbol{\chi}):([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})\to([m],\boldsymbol{\psi})
in
\mathsf{D}
is precisely either a tight arrow, if
m=n=0
, or a sequence of multimorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{\sigma(1)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\chi_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\varphi}(0,\sigma(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\sigma(1),\sigma(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\varphi}(\sigma(m-1),\sigma(m))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

in
\mathbb{D}
, with the whole sequence
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
as loose source. An inert morphism
(i,\boldsymbol{\chi}):([n],\boldsymbol{\varphi})\to([m],\boldsymbol{\psi})
in
\mathsf{D}
lying over the inclusion
i:[m]\cong[j,j+m]\subseteq[n]
is a sequence of unary isomorphism multicells from a sub-interval of the loose source into the loose target:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{j}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{j+1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{j+m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{y_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j+2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{j+m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

Next, composing with the display isomorphism
\mathsf{Cat}_{/\Delta^{\operatorname{op}}}\simeq\mathbf{Vdc}(\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{\Delta^{\operatorname{op}}})^{op_{l}},\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan})
,
we see that a VDC
\mathbb{D}
can be encoded by a normal VDF
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D}):\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{\Delta})\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
given by the following data:

1.

For each integer
n\geq 0
, the object
[n]
is mapped to the fiber
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{n}:=\mathsf{D}_{n}
.

2.

For each morphism
\sigma:[n]\to[m]
in the simplex category, the profunctor
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(\sigma):\mathsf{D}_{n}\times\mathsf{D}_{m}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
is given by
\mathsf{D}_{\sigma}(-,-)
.

3.

For each composable pair of morphisms
[n]\xrightarrow{f}[m]\xrightarrow{g}[k]
in the simplex category,
the image of the cocartesian cell for
g\circ f
is
\ell_{f,g}:\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f)\odot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(g)\Rightarrow\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(g\circ f)

given by composing sequences of multicells.

4.

The rest of the data of the VDF follows, since every cell in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{\Delta})
arises from a sequence of composable morphisms.

In particular, the description of the operator categories for VDCs translates to those normal VDFs
Q:\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{\Delta})\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
satisfying the following:

1.

For
i:[k]\hookrightarrow[n]
inert, the profunctor
Q(i):Q_{[k]}\times Q_{[n]}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
is represented by a functor
i_{*}:Q_{[n]}\to Q_{[k]}
so that
Q(i)\cong Q_{[k]}(i_{*}(-),-)
, and these representations can be chosen to be functorial in the inert morphisms (i.e. for inert morphisms
[k]\xrightarrow{i}[n]\xrightarrow{j}[m]
,
i_{*}j_{*}=(j\circ i)_{*}
, and
(\text{id}_{[k]})_{*}=\text{id}_{Q_{[k]}}
).

2.

For
i:[k]\hookrightarrow[n]
inert and
g:[l]\to[k]
arbitrary, the laxator cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{[l]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{[k]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{[n]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{g,f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{[l]}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q_{[n]}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(i)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q(i\circ g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

is opcartesian.

3.

For each
[n]\in\mathsf{\Delta}
, the natural map

(\delta^{[0,1]}_{*},\ldots,\delta^{[n-1,n]}_{*}):Q_{[n]}\to Q_{[1]}\times_{Q_{[0]}}\cdots\times_{Q_{[0]}}Q_{[1]}

in
\mathsf{Cat}=\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}_{0}
induced by the functors from (1), for the inert morphisms
\delta^{[i,i+1]}:[1]\to[n]
, is an isomorphism.

4.

For each morphism
\phi:[n]\to[m]
, the natural map of profunctors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q(\phi)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q(\phi_{[0,1]})(-,\delta^{[0,1]}_{*})\underset{{Q(\phi_{\{1\}})(-,\delta^{1}_{*})}}{\times}\cdots\underset{{Q(\phi_{n-1})(-,\delta^{\{n-1\}}_{*})}}{\times}Q(\phi_{[n-1,n]})(-,\delta^{[n-1,n]}_{*})}}

in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}_{1}
induced by the projections in (1) is an isomorphism.

One of the benefits of this profunctor description of VDCs is the ability to translate it into the theory of pullback sketches in VDCs.

Construction B.3

(Pullback Sketch for a VDC)
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a VDC, and let
\mathbb{Q}(\mathsf{\Delta}^{op},\mathsf{inrt})\subseteq\mathbb{Q}(\mathsf{\Delta}^{op})
denote the sub-VDC of
the quintet VDC whose only tight morphisms are inert morphisms. Then due to condition (1) in the description of VDCs as normal VDFs
into
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
, we can extend the normal VDF
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D}):\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{\Delta})\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
to a normal VDF

\mathsf{Q}(\mathbb{D}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathsf{\Delta}^{op},\mathsf{inrt})^{op_{l}}\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
where an inert morphism
i:[n]\hookrightarrow[m]

is mapped to the functor
i_{*}:\mathsf{D}_{m}\to\mathsf{D}_{n}
representing the profunctor
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(i):\mathsf{D}_{n}\times\mathsf{D}_{m}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
.
Additionally, the unique multicell
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}&f_{1}\cdots f_{n}&\\
i&&j\\
&g&\end{smallmatrix}\right)
witnessing the composite
f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}\circ i=j\circ g
is mapped to the natural transformation

\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{1})\odot\cdots\odot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{n})\Rightarrow\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(g)(j_{*}(-),i_{*}(-))

that takes in a sequence of towers of composable multicells, composes each tower, and then projects onto a subsequence of the multicells.

Under this reformulation conditions (1) and (2) for normal VDFs
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{\Delta})\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
representing VDCs become equivalent to the existence of such a normal VDF
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbb{D}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathsf{\Delta}^{op},\mathsf{inrt})^{op_{l}}\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
. Condition (3) then states that
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbb{D})_{0}
preserves pullbacks (i.e. sends pushouts in
\mathsf{inrt}\subseteq\mathsf{\Delta}
to pullbacks in
\mathsf{Cat}
), and condition (4) states that
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbb{D})_{1}
preserves pullbacks.
More explicitly, a colimit of a sequence of spans in
\mathsf{inrt}\subseteq\mathsf{\Delta}
is canonically of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{0},k_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{1},k_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{n-1},k_{n-1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0,k_{0}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{0},k_{1}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[m_{n-1},k_{n}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[k_{n}]}}

where
0\leq m_{0}\leq k_{0}\leq m_{1}\leq k_{1}\leq\cdots\leq m_{n-1}\leq k_{n-1}\leq k_{n}
, while such a colimit in
\mathbb{Q}(\mathsf{\Delta},\mathsf{inrt})_{1}

is uniquely determined by a diagram which gives a colimit of a sequence of spans when taking source and target projections.
Using the explicit definition of
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbb{D})
from
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})
, these colimits are mapped exactly to the limits
appearing in condition (4) of the characterization, after we apply
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbb{D})
and use conditions (1) and (2) to re-express the limits in (4).
In particular, if
f:[n]\to[m]
is an arbitrary morphism in
\mathsf{\Delta}
, then using conditions (1) and (2), condition (4) becomes equivalent to the natural map

\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f)\to\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{[0,1]})\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}(-,-)}\cdots\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}(-,-)}\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{[n-1,n]})

in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}_{1}
being an isomorphism. Here
f_{[i,i+1]}:[1]\to[f(i+1)-f(i)]
is the unique active morphism such that
f\circ\delta^{[i,i+1]}=\delta^{[f(i),f(i+1)]}\circ f_{[i,i+1]}
,
and the maps
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{i})\leftarrow\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{[i,i+1]})\to\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{i+1})
in
\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}_{1}

are given by projecting from a multicell onto its tight source and target, respectively:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{f(i+1)-f(i)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{*}^{\{0\}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{[i,i+1]})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\{1\}}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{0}(-,-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{i})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{i+1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{D}_{0}(-,-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

where
f_{i}=f\circ\delta^{\{i\}}
.

We can also use the equivalence in Equation
10
to describe a VDF
F:\mathbb{E}\to\mathbb{D}
as a normal VDF
\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{D}}(F):\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{D})^{op_{l}}\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
given by the following data:

1.

An object
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
in
\mathsf{D}
(i.e. a sequence of loose arrows in
\mathbb{D}
) is sent to the fiber category
F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\subseteq\mathsf{E}_{|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|}
whose objects are length
|\boldsymbol{\varphi}|
-sequences of loose arrows in
\mathbb{E}
lying over
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
, and whose morphisms are sequences of unary multicells between such loose arrows lying over
\text{id}_{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
.

2.

A map
\beta:(H_{1}\cdots H_{m})\to(J_{1}\cdots J_{n})
lying over
f:[n]\to[m]
in
\mathsf{D}
is sent to the profunctor
F^{-1}(\beta,f):F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{J})\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{H})
(i.e.
F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{J})\times F^{-1}(\boldsymbol{H})^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
) that maps an object
(\boldsymbol{I},\boldsymbol{K})
to the set of maps
\alpha:(K_{1},...,K_{m})\to(I_{1},...,I_{n})
lying over
\beta
, and maps sequences of unary multicells to the set function given by pasting the unary multicells above and below the sequences of multicells in the image of
(\boldsymbol{I},\boldsymbol{K})
.

This perspective will be beneficial for describing the exponentiable VDFs and exponentiable morphisms in more general contexts.

B.1
Exponentiability in terms of Operator Categories

In the operator category language, the exponentiable VDCs are precisely the active VDCs defined below:

Definition B.4

(Active VDC)
.

A VDC
\mathbb{D}
is said to be
active
if and only if for every active morphism
f:[n]\to[m]
, the laxator

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{a_{n},f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

for the associated VDF
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D}):\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{\Delta})\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
is an opcartesian cell,
where as above
a_{n}:[1]\to[n]
denotes the unique active morphism of that signature in
\mathsf{\Delta}.

∎

In terms of coends a VDC
\mathbb{D}
is active if and only if for every loose arrow
\psi
and every
2
-fold sequence of loose arrows
{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}
, the composition map

\int^{\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathsf{fc}_{|{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}|_{1}}(\mathbb{D}_{1})}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})(\boldsymbol{\chi},\psi)\times\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})({\boldsymbol{\varphi}},\boldsymbol{\chi})\xrightarrow{\circ}\mathsf{MCell}(\mathbb{D})({\boldsymbol{\varphi}},\psi)

is an isomorphism. But by Corollary
4.11
this is equivalent to
\mathbb{D}
having decomposable multicells, and hence by Theorem
5.11
it is equivalent to
\mathbb{D}
being exponentiable. Similarly, we can encode exponentiable VDFs in the same language using the following notion of active VDFs:

Definition B.5

(Active VDF)
.

A VDF
F:\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{E}
is said to be
active
if and only if for every active morphism
(f^{op},\boldsymbol{\chi}):([m],{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})\to([n],\boldsymbol{\psi})
in
\mathsf{E}
, and every active morphism
(a_{n}^{op},\alpha):([n],\boldsymbol{\psi})\to([1],\lambda)
, the laxator

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([1],\lambda)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([n],\boldsymbol{\psi})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([m],{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{a_{n},f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([1],\lambda)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([m],{\boldsymbol{\varphi}})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)(a_{n}^{op},\alpha)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)(f^{op},\boldsymbol{\chi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)\left(a_{m}^{op},\frac{\boldsymbol{\chi}}{\alpha}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

for the associated VDF
\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F):\mathbb{L}\mathsf{}(\mathsf{E})^{op_{l}}\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
is an opcartesian cell, and if
n=m=0
, then for all morphisms
(\text{id}_{[0]}^{op},a):([0],x)\to([0],y)
and
(\text{id}_{[0]}^{op},b):([0],y)\to([0],z)
, the laxator

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([0],z)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([0],y)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([0],x)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{a_{n},f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([0],z)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)_{([0],x)}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)(\text{id}_{[0]}^{op},b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)(\text{id}_{[0]}^{op},a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}_{/\mathbb{E}}(F)(\text{id}_{[0]}^{op},b\circ a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

is also an opcartesian cell.
∎

From Corollary
5.6
,
to check that a VDC is active we need only check the laxator

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{a_{n},f}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

is opcartesian when
f
is either an inner face map or a degeneracy map, and that for any active
f:[n]\to[m]
and any decomposition
f_{k}\circ\cdots f_{1}=f
where each
f_{i}
is either an inner face map or a degeneracy map, the partial composite map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{m}}}
id
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\ell_{f_{1},...,f_{k}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{m}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{opcart}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})_{m}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{1})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{k})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{n})}\odot{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}

has surjective component maps. Using this observation and the normal VDF perspective
\mathsf{Q}(\mathbb{D}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathsf{\Delta}^{op},\mathsf{inrt})^{op_{l}}\to\mathbb{P}\mathsf{rof}
for a VDC
\mathbb{D}
, we can identify an exponentiable VDC with the following data:

1.

A graph of categories
s,t:\mathsf{D}_{1}\rightrightarrows\mathsf{D}_{0}
, from which we can define
\mathsf{D}_{n}=\mathsf{D}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{D}_{0}}\cdots\times_{\mathsf{D}_{0}}\mathsf{D}_{1}
.

2.

Two graphs of profunctors

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{D}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{0})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{2})}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathsf{D}_{0}(-,-)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathsf{D}_{0}(-,-)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\mathsf{D}_{0}(-,-)}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}

For
f:[n]\to[m]
a degeneracy or inner face map, we define
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f):=\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{k_{1}})\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}(-,-)}\cdots\times_{\mathbb{D}_{0}(-,-)}\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{k_{n}})
where
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{1}\pm\delta_{i}
, so that all but one of the profunctors is
\mathbb{D}_{1}(-,-)
, and the remaining profunctor is either
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{0})
or
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(a_{2})
.
Additionally, for each binary-nullary tree
T
, which we can encode by a unique sequence of maps
f_{k}\circ\cdots f_{1}:[1]\to[m]
with each
f_{i}
either a degeneracy or inner face map, we define
\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(T):=\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{1})\odot\cdots\odot\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(f_{k})
.

3.

For any two binary-nullary trees
T
and
T^{\prime}
with the same number of leaves, a natural isomorphism
\alpha_{T,T^{\prime}}:\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(T)\to\mathsf{P}(\mathbb{D})(T^{\prime})
, satisfying the cocycle identities

\alpha_{T,T}=\text{id},\;\;\alpha_{T^{\prime},T^{\prime\prime}}\circ\alpha_{T,T^{\prime}}=\alpha_{T,T^{\prime\prime}}

This presentation exhibits exponentiable VDCs as a kind of unbiased pro-double categories.
Namely, we need only specify the data for nullary, unary, and binary multicells, as expected for a
pro-double concept, but we need to provide a compatible family of associators for all possible
towers of multicells that can be built from these. In particular, counterexamples like that
in the proof of Proposition
6.7
will not satisfy this
corrected notion of pro-double category, since we have explicitly added associators
for trees with four and more leaves rather than attempting to build them out of the basic associators,
which as we showed there is impossible in general.

Appendix C
Yoneda Lemma for monoids

As discussed in Section
5
, Lemma
5.17
is a special case of a more general result for monoids in a VDC. For the remainder of this section we fix a VDC
\mathbb{D}
, together with a monoid
x=(x_{0},x_{1}:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x_{0},\eta,\mu)
, where
\eta
is the unit nullary multicell and
\mu
is the multiplication binary multicell. We will phrase the Yoneda lemma for monoids in terms of left modules over the monoid, while dualizing would give an analogous statement for right modules.

Definition C.1

(Left Modules)
.

A left module over the monoid
x
consists of an object
y\in\mathbb{D}
, a loose arrow
\varphi:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
, and a globular multicell
\chi:\frac{(x_{1},\varphi)}{\varphi}
compatible with the multiplication and unit multicells in the sense of Definition
2.10
. A map of left modules
(x,\varphi,y)\to(z,\psi,w)
consists of a map of monoids
x\to z
together with a cell

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{a_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

which is compatible with the module multicells.
∎

Note that if
y\in\mathbb{D}
admits a loose unit, a left
x
-module structure on
y
is equivalent to a bimodule where the right action is the canonical one induced by a choice of loose unit.
Now, the role of representable presheaves in the 1-categorical Yoneda lemma will be replaced by restrictions of the monoid
x
along a tight morphism, as in the following construction.

and

.

Theorem C.3

(Yoneda Lemma for Left Modules in a VDC)
.

Let
\mathbb{D}
be a VDC and let
x=(x_{0},x_{1}:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}x_{0},\eta,\mu)
be a monoid in
\mathbb{D}
.
If
(\varphi:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y,\chi_{x})
is a left
x
-module, then for any tight arrow

a:z\to x_{0}
for which the restriction
x_{1}(1,a)
exists, we have a natural bijection
between maps of left modules below left and nullary multicells below right:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{x_{1}(1,a)}}

When
\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}
,
x=\mathsf{C}
is a category, and a left module is a set
A
together with an
A
-indexed family of presheaves
(P_{a}:\mathsf{C}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set})_{a\in A}
. Then Theorem
C.3
says that if we have a set function
g:B\to A
, together with a family of objects
(c_{b})_{b\in B}
in
\mathsf{C}
, then we have a bijection between families of natural transformations below left, and families of elements below right:

(\alpha_{b}:\mathsf{C}(-,c_{b})\Rightarrow P_{g(b)}(c_{b}))_{b\in B}\leftrightsquigarrow(x_{b}\in P_{g(b)}(c_{b}))_{b\in B}

given by evaluating at identity arrows. In order to obtain an alternate proof of Lemma
5.17
we can apply Theorem
C.3
to the case where the virtual double category is
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{Grph}),\mathsf{fc})
, the monoid
x
is the VDC
\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})
,
y=z=\mathsf{Edge}
is the graph with a single edge between two distinct vertices, and the tight arrow
a
picks out the sequence of loose arrows
\boldsymbol{\varphi}
in
\mathbb{D}
. Then a left module
\varphi:x_{0}\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}y
corresponds to a loose arrow
\Phi:F\mathrel{\vtop{\halign{#\cr\hfil$\mapstochar\mkern 5.0mu$\hfil\cr$\rightarrow$\crcr}}}G
in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}
, and module maps correspond to multicells in
\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}^{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})^{op_{t}}}{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\rho_{\mathbb{D}}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})\\
\Phi\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
while elements of
\Phi(\boldsymbol{\varphi})
correspond precisely to nullary multicells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Edge}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{fc}(\mathbb{D})_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Edge}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\boldsymbol{\varphi}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\shortmid}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

in
\mathbb{L}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{S}\mathsf{pan}(\mathsf{Grph}),\mathsf{fc})
.
